The Tate conjecture for surfaces of geometric genus one—embracing singularities
Haoyang Guo and Ziquan Yang

Abstract
. In this article, we aim to largely complete the program of proving the Tate conjecture for surfaces of geometric genus one, by introducing techniques to analyze those surfaces whose “natural models” are singular. As an application, we show that every elliptic curve of height one over a global function field of genus one and characteristic p ≥ 11 p\geq 11 satisfies the Birch–Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Statements of main results

This paper aims to largely complete the program of proving the Tate conjecture via the Kuga–Satake construction for surfaces of geometric genus one, whenever the moduli is of an expected form.
Previously, this has been achieved for K3 surfaces, after several decades of efforts made by many people in various contexts and generality.
Here is a non-exhaustive list:
[
Del72
,
Nyg83
,
NO85
,
Tan90
,
Tan95
,
And96
,
Cha13
,
Mau14
,
Mad15
,
KM16
,
IIK21
]
.
Later on, in characteristic
0
, Moonen
[
Moo17
]
proved the divisorial Tate conjecture (as well as the Mumford–Tate conjecture for the cohomology in degree
2
) for general varieties with Hodge number
h^{2,0}=1
, under a mild assumption on moduli.
This was then extended to postive characteristics by the second author with Hamacher and Zhao in
[
HYZ25
]
.

In this paper, we introduce techniques to treat the singularities on the natural models of elliptic and general type surfaces, an important issue left open in
[
HYZ25
]
.
Here the ‘‘natural models’’ refer to the Weierstrass normal forms for elliptic surfaces, and the canonical models for general type surfaces.

1
1

1

Note that, interestingly, for K3 surfaces there is no such thing as a “natural model”, and one has to artificially choose a quasi-polarization in order to consider moduli spaces.

The challenge posed by the singularities arises from the
failure of purity
of abelian schemes over a general positive or mixed characteristic base, which obstructs a direct construction of mixed characteristic period morphisms.
We shall explain more about this in
Section

1.2
below.

Our motivation to tackle this problem is twofold. One is to investigate the mysterious link between singularities and the Tate conjecture, which was already noted in the 1982 paper by Rudakov–Zink–Shafarevich
[
RZS82
]
(cf.
Remark

1.5
). The other is to contribute to the literature a clean example of the BSD conjecture, for which evidence is still meager.

Theorem 1.1
.

(
Theorem

4.33
)

Over a global function field
K
of genus
1
and characteristic
p\geq 11
, every elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}
of height
1
satisfies the BSD conjecture.
2
2

2

It is also proved for
p=5,7
except for 3 special cases in
Proposition

2.23
(cf.
Remark

4.35
).

Let us briefly recall the terminology.
Suppose that
K
is the function field of a smooth proper curve
C
and
\pi:X\to C
is the elliptic surface (with a zero section) obtained by taking the minimal model of
\mathcal{E}
.
Then the height of
\mathcal{E}
is the degree of the fundamental line bundle
(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\vee}
.
We refer the reader to
[
Mil75
]
and
[
KT03
]
on the equivalence between the Tate conjecture for
X
and the BSD conjecture for
\mathcal{E}
, and remark that elliptic K3 surfaces, treated back in
[
AS73
]
, correspond to the case when the genus of
K
is
0
and the height is
2
.
In
[
HYZ25
, Thm. A]
, the above result was obtained when all fibers of
\pi
are irreducible, or equivalently the Weierstrass normal form of
X
is smooth.

Similarly, in
[
HYZ25
, Thm. C]
, we proved the Tate conjecture for certain surfaces of geometric genus one over
k
, assuming that the canonical divisor is ample and not just big and nef. Now we can drop this assumption.
Below for a smooth projective surface
X
, we use
K_{X}
to denote its canonical divisor, and use
p_{g}=h^{2,0}
to denote its geometric genus.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
k
be a field finitely generated over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
for
p\geq 5
. Then any minimal smooth projective surface
X
over
k
with
p_{g}=K_{X}^{2}=1
and
h^{1,0}=h^{0,1}=0
satisfies the Tate conjecture.

Both
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
are special cases of a general theorem.
We state it in the rough form below and refer the reader to
Theorem

4.2
for the precise conditions.

Theorem 1.3
.

(
Theorem

4.2
)

Let
p\geq 5
be a prime number, let
\mathsf{M}
be a smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-scheme and let
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be a projective family of surfaces whose geometric fibers are generically smooth with
p_{g}=1
and have at worst rational double point (RDP) singularities.

Assume that
\mathsf{M}
admits a smooth morphism to another smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-scheme
\mathsf{B}
with a relative compactification
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, and satisfies two additional conditions:

(i)

The discriminant locus
\mathsf{D}\subseteq\mathsf{M}
of the family
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
and the relative boundary
\mathsf{H}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{\mathsf{M}}\smallsetminus\mathsf{M}
are both generically reduced in codimension
1
modulo
p
.

(ii)

Over
\mathbb{C}
, the Kodaira–Spencer map generically has rank
\geq 2
and does not vanish along a general fiber of
\mathsf{M}\to\mathsf{B}
.

Then the minimal (and hence any) resolution of any fiber of
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the Tate conjecture as long as it has the same Hodge diamond as the generic fiber or is non-supersingular
3
3

3

In this paper, a surface is said to be
supersingular
if its second crystalline cohomology is purely of slope
1
.
.

In the above theorem,
\mathsf{M}
should be interpreted as a coarse moduli space,
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
the family of natural models, and
\mathsf{B}
the moduli space of some auxilliary varieties.
For example, when
\mathsf{M}
parametrizes fibered surfaces,
\mathsf{B}
parametrizes the base curves; and when
\mathsf{M}
parametrizes complete intersections of
r
hypersurfaces in some fixed ambient space,
\mathsf{B}
parametrizes the
3
-fold given by intersecting the first
(r-1)
hypersurfaces.
Moreover, the fibers of
\mathsf{M}\to\mathsf{B}
are often open subspaces of certain linear systems, and hence can be compactified in a natural way, giving rise to the relative compactification
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
—this is exactly what happens for both
Theorem

1.1
and
1.2
. We also remind the reader that on natural models of elliptic or general type surfaces over an algebraically closed field, singularities are at worst rational double points.

Remark 1.4

(Other examples)
.

We expect that the hypotheses of our general theorem apply to almost every class of surfaces with
p_{g}=1
(e.g., those listed in
[
Moo17
, Thm. 9.4]
) with minor modifications.
For another example, the canonical model of a projective surface
X
over
\mathbb{C}
with
p_{g}=q=1
and
K_{X}^{2}=2
is a double cover of the symmetric square
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
of an elliptic curve
E
, ramified along a divisor in a fixed linear system on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
(
[
Cat10
, Prop. 20]
). We can naturally consider such surfaces in positive characteristics, and construct a coarse moduli space
\mathsf{M}
by taking
\mathsf{B}
to parametrize
E
and
\mathsf{M}
to parametrize suitable divisors on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
.
Then
Theorem

1.3
applies to
\mathsf{M}
for all but a few
p
where condition (i) might fail (the generic fiber of
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is polarized by its canonical divisor, which has degree
2
. To check the condition on Kodaira–Spencer in (ii), see
[
Cat10
, p.283]
).
However, we do not investigate these other examples here and leave the task to the interested readers, as verifying the hypotheses of
Theorem

1.3
becomes largely algebro-geometric considerations, like those in
Section

4.8
.

Remark 1.5

(Singularities and the Tate conjecture)
.

Before we delve into the proof, we emphasize that singularities in natural models are not mere technical obstacles.
Rather, they are fundamentally linked to the Tate conjecture:

•

The resolution of these singularities is itself an important source of divisors on the smooth models of these surfaces.

•

Many coarse moduli spaces
\mathsf{M}
are nearly simply connected, meaning the singularities on the fibers are
necessary
to induce non-trivial geometric monodromy on the family
\mathcal{X}
.
Otherwise, there would be no interesting variations of zeta functions or geometric Picard number among fibers.

In fact, this latter point is manifested in our arguments.
Let us also remark that back in 1982, Rudakov, Zink and Shafarevich (
[
RZS82
]
) proved the Tate conjecture for supersingular K3 surfaces with a degree
2
polarization precisely by considering singular degenerations: if such a surface is elliptic, then this follows from
[
AS73
]
; otherwise, the surface can be deformed to a singular surface, whose resolution must be elliptic, so one concludes by combining
[
Art74a
]
.
This strategy was later vastly extended by Maulik in
[
Mau14
]
.
In this paper, by both making use of singularities and overcoming the obstacles they induce, we hope to further elucidate the mysterious connection singularities and the Tate conjecture.

1.2.
Ideas of proof

We now explain the ideas of proof.
Our approach builds upon Madapusi’ work in
[
Mad15
]
, but introduces several key innovations that greatly expand its scope. Therefore, we start with a recollection of Madapusi’s method and point out the difficulties for generalizations.

Recollection of Madapusi’s Method.
Let
\mathsf{M}
be the moduli space of (quasi-polarized) K3 surfaces over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
and
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be the universal family.
The first step is to construct a period morphism
\rho:\mathsf{M}\to\mathscr{S}
(up to an étale cover of
\mathsf{M}
), where
\mathscr{S}
is the canonical integral model of an orthogonal Shimura variety, over which there is a universal Kuga–Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
.
Then the second step is to show that for each point
s\in\mathsf{M}
, the original Tate conjecture for divisors of the K3 surface
\mathcal{X}_{s}
can be translated to a variant of Tate’s theorem for a distinguished class of endomorphisms
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\rho(s)})\subset\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{\rho(s)})
, called
special endomorphisms
, of the abelian variety
\mathcal{A}_{\rho(s)}
.

In order to achieve the above strategy, the following two facts played essential roles in Madapusi’s arguments:

(A)

Quasi-polarized K3 surfaces over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
have a
fine
and regular moduli space
\mathsf{M}
, and abelian schemes over it satisfy a
purity
condition.

(B)

The integral period morphism
\rho:\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathscr{S}
is
étale
, where
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}
is some étale cover of
\mathsf{M}
.

Property
(A)
is needed for the very construction of an integral period morphism
\rho:\mathsf{M}\to\mathscr{S}
.
Indeed, unlike Shimura varieties of PEL type, those of Hodge or abelian type (including
\mathscr{S}
used here) lack a direct moduli interpretation on their integral models (cf.
[
Kis10
]
).
Instead, the only known general method of constructing morphisms to those integral Shimura varieties is to use their extension property as in
loc. cit.
, where the test schemes must satisfy a purity condition for abelian schemes.
4
4

4

For a given scheme
T
, the purity of abelian schemes over
T
is a condition on
T
.
It states that any abelian scheme
A_{U}\to U
can be extended over the entire scheme
T
, where
U\subset T
is an open subscheme away from a codimension
2
locus.

Even for Siegel moduli spaces, the purity condition is strictly necessary for the extension property (cf.
[
VZ10
, §6 Remarks]
).
Luckily, deformations of quasi-polarized K3 surfaces are sufficiently well understood so that the purity condition can be checked explicitly using Vasiu–Zink’s criterion in
loc. cit.
(
[
Mad15
, Thm. 3.8]
).

In
[
Mad15
]
, property
(B)
was used to establish the aforementioned translation between the divisor classes of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
and the special endomorphisms of
\mathcal{A}_{\rho(s)}
.
More precisely, the étaleness is used to show that special endomorphisms can be lifted to characteristic
0
, so that one can construct the corresponding divisor on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
via the Lefschetz
(1,1)
-theorem and a specialization argument.

For general surfaces of geometric genus one,
neither

(A)
nor
(B)
is available.
Our contribution is to introduce new ideas that enable us to extend the above strategy without these two conditions, and to provide techniques for studying the singular fibers and their periods.
Before we explain the details, the following picture illustrates the relationship of various geometric objects that appear in the arguments.

\mathsf{D}^{+}
\mathsf{D}^{-}
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
smooth
mildest singularity
worse singularity
\mathsf{M}
\mathscr{S}
s
T
\pi
\rho
(defined on
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
)
fibers:
\tau

As in
Theorem

1.3
, we let
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be the family of natural models over a coarse moduli space, with
\mathsf{D}
being the discriminant locus parametrizing singular fibers. The meaning of other symbols will be explained below.

Extended Period Morphisms

We first let
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}:=\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}
be the open smooth locus.
As observed in the previous work of the second author with Hamacher and Zhao
[
HYZ25
]
, one can still construct a period morphism
\rho:\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}
, where
\mathscr{S}
is a suitable integral orthogonal Shimura variety and
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
is an étale cover of
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
.

To analyze the singular fibers, the naive approach is to simultaneously resolve the family
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
over some cover
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{M}
(which exists thanks to Artin–Brieskorn resolution
[
Art74
]
), and then extend
\rho
over
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
.
However, as discussed above, extending
\rho
is a very delicate problem, which underscores the necessity of
(A)
in
[
Mad15
]
.
As Artin–Brieskorn resolution does not offer any effective control on
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
, which can a priori be very singular or ramified over
\mathsf{M}
, it is unclear whether
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
can be further resolved such that the purity of abelian schemes holds over it.

In order to remedy the above issues, we instead seek to partially extend the period morphism, and hence introduce the following notion.

Definition 1.6

(cf.
Definition

4.3
)
.

Given a normal
\mathsf{M}
-scheme
T
(with structure morphism denoted by
\pi
), a map
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
is called an
extended period morphism
if the family
\mathcal{X}|_{T}\to T
admits a simultaneous resolution, and for some open dense subscheme
T^{\circ}\subseteq T
, the map
\tau
fits into a commutative diagram as below

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

The above notion is inspired by the idea of taking “limiting Hodge structures” in classical Hodge theory.
Let us use
\breve{X}
to denote the minimal resolution of a surface
X
.
Given
s\in\mathsf{M}
, our method to prove the Tate conjecture for
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
is to find a suitable arithmetic surface
S
in
\mathsf{M}
passing through
s
such that:

•

after passing to a generically finite cover
T
of
S
, there is an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
, and

•

the morphism
\tau
is
motivically aligned
at
s
, meaning that for each
t\in T
that lifts
s
, there is a correspondence bewteen special endomorphisms of the Kuga-Satake abelian variety
\mathcal{A}_{\tau(s)}
and divisors on
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s}=\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
(see
Definition

4.8
).

With the above construction established, the Tate conjecture for
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
then follows quickly from a variant of Tate’s theorem for special endomorphisms as in
[
Mad15
]
.

Below are two important aspects for extended period morphisms.

(I)

Deformation techniques:
To prove that a point
t\in T
is motivically aligned, the general method is to show that, for every special endomorphism
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
, the pair
(t,\zeta)
can be deformed to some
t^{\prime}\in T
, such that
t^{\prime}
is motivically aligned.
This conceptualizes the key argument in
[
Mad15
]
for K3 surfaces that eventually appeals to the Lefschetz
(1,1)
-theorem.
In our more general situation, we shall extend this idea by not only considering mixed characteristic deformations, but also equi-characteristic ones.

(II)

Matching local systems:
For a point
t
in
T
such that
\pi(t)
is in the singular locus
\mathsf{D}
, we need to prove that the Shimura variety
\mathscr{S}
really “knows” the period of the resolution
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
.
Precisely, this means that the pullbacks of various automorphic local systems
\mathbf{L}_{?}
, with
?
being Betti, de Rham,
\ell
-adic, or crystalline, admit natural isomorphisms with the
?
-cohomology of the resolution of
\mathcal{X}
over
T
.
This is achieved by first doing this within the smooth locus
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
, and then proving that this is also true over the boundary
T\times_{\mathsf{M}}\mathsf{D}
.
For
?=\ell,B
, this follows from the basic facts about local systems and results of
[
HYZ25
]
.
For
?=\mathrm{crys}
, we make an essential use of the rigidity theorem on extending F-(iso)crystals due to de Jong and Kedlaya–Drinfeld (see
Theorem

2.2
).

The constructions for different loci.

Now we explain how to construct the desired extended period morphisms
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
.
Considerations of enumerative geometry suggests that one should expect a rough correspondence between the singularities on
\mathcal{X}_{s}
and the singularity of
\mathsf{D}
at
s
.
5
5

5

For plane curves, this is discussed in
[
EH16
, §7.7.2]
, but the principle is quite general.

In particular, the maximal smooth locus
\mathsf{D}^{+}
of
\mathsf{D}
should correspond to those fibers with mildest singularities.
As we will see below, points on
\mathsf{D}^{+}
and those on its complement
\mathsf{D}^{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{D}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}^{+}
will be treated separately via different constructions.

Case
s\in\mathsf{D}^{+}
:
By a standard Bertini-type argument, we can find a curve
C\subseteq\mathsf{M}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
that passes through the point
s
and interacts
\mathsf{D}^{+}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
transversely.
We also choose a mixed characteristic scheme
\mathcal{C}
that lifts the curve
C
.
Then, up to passing to a generically étale cover
\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}
, we may find an extended period morphism over the two dimensional scheme
T=\mathcal{C}_{1}
, where
the morphism
\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{M}}\mathsf{M}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}
extends over
\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{M}}\mathsf{D}
thanks to the Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich criterion.
Here it is critical that the curve
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is delicately constructed with the aid of Abhyankar’s lemma so that
\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{M}}\mathsf{D}
is étale over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
In particular, the construction enables us to analyze the behavior of the monodromy using Grothendieck’s specialization theorem for the tame fundamental groups at
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
Afterwards, the arguments of
[
HYZ25
]
for the smooth locus
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
can be extended to the boundary
\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{M}}\mathsf{D}
(see
Theorem

4.18
):
The idea is that if there is a special endomorphism
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
such that the pair
(t,\zeta)
does not lift to a point on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
in characteristic zero, then one can derive a contradiction regarding the dimension of monodromy invariants.

Case
s\in\mathsf{D}^{-}
:
This case is significantly more complicated and takes more inputs, as
\mathsf{D}^{-}
parametrize those with worse singularities and
\mathsf{D}
itself might be very singular.
In particular, the aforementioned mixed characteristic curve may not exist, so this time we let
T
be an actual surface over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, where a combination of Artin–Brieskorn resolution of the family
\mathcal{X}|_{T}\to T
and Lipman’s resolution of the base
T
allow us to construct an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
.

At this point, we know that the morphism
\tau
is motivically aligned at any point
t^{\prime}\in T
, as long as the image
\pi(t^{\prime})\in\mathsf{M}
is contained in
\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}^{-}
.
If for any given special endomorphism
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
, the pair
(t,\zeta)
can be deformed to any such
t^{\prime}
, then we win.
On the other hand, by a fact on special divisors for orthogonal Shimura variety, the pair
(t,\zeta)
always deforms along some divisor on
T
.
However, it is a priori possible that this divisor is contracted by
\pi:T\to\mathsf{M}
, and in particular the motivic alignedness has not been established on any point of this divisor.
We also note that the periods of the point
s
, defined as the image subscheme
\tau(\pi^{-1}(s))\subset\mathscr{S}
, can in principle be positive dimensional.

However, we make the following critical observation that prevents the above scenario:

Any
\pi
-exceptional curve is also
\tau
-exceptional.

Heuristically, the above is saying that up to finite ambiguity, any point in
\mathsf{D}^{-}
admits a unique “period” on
\mathscr{S}
.
To achieve the above observation, we apply the minimal model program for 3-folds in positive characteristic, a Beauville–Laszlo type gluing theorem for projective schemes, as well as the matching of F-crystals established in (II) above.
In the non-supersingular case, we also give a different argument using the positivity of Hodge bundle on
\mathscr{S}
.
Here we remark that the
\pi
-exceptional curves are not necessarily
(-1)
-curves, and in particular it is not clear if they can be blown down for the morphism
\tau
.
Nonetheless, we can show that it is not possible for
(z,\zeta)
to deform only along
\pi
-exceptional curves, as this would contradict the Hodge index theorem on some compactification of
T
.

Below we also comment on some technical caveats and subtleties of this paper.

Remark 1.7

(Matching isomorphism for crystalline coefficients)
.

In
[
HYZ25
]
, it was not necessary to match
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}
with the (primitive) crystalline cohomology of
\mathcal{X}
over
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
. But now it becomes important when dealing with points on
\mathsf{D}^{-}
, so we fill this in. As in
[
HYZ25
]
, much of the technicalities arise because we do not assume that the classical Kuga–Satake construction is
absolute Hodge
, or that the motives of the varieties in question are abelian.
So Madapusi’s argument to do this step in
[
Mad15
]
, which relies on Blasius’ result on the de Rhamness of the Hodge cycles on abelian motives
[
Bla94
]
, does not apply.

In this paper, we shall establish the matching of these F-(iso)crystals using the recent advances on the
p
-adic Riemann–Hilbert functor in
[
DLLZ23
]
and integral
p
-adic Hodge theory of Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze in
[
BMS18
]
.
Then we prove a compatibility statement for the outputs of the
p
-adic and classical Riemann–Hilbert functors using the analytic density of Noether–Lefschetz loci (see
Section

3.3
).
This steps addresses a question with a similar flavor to
[
DLLZ23
, Conjecture 1.4]
in our specific setting.
Therefore, we think these techniques might be of independent interest.

Remark 1.8

(Assumptions on
p
)
.

The assumptions on
p
in this paper only come from condition (i) in
Theorem

4.2
, together with the requirement that minimal resolutions of fibers of
\mathcal{X}
should have the correct Hodge numbers whenever they are supersingular.
In fact, the latter is not even required for points on
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
or
\mathsf{D}^{+}
.
Beyond the potential influence on Hodge numbers of resolutions, our method is insenstive to the
type
of singularities of fibers over
\mathsf{D}^{-}
with respect to
p
.
For example, we do
not
assume that
p
is coprime to the order of the Weyl group of the relevant RDPs (see
Example

4.34
)—imposing such restrictions is often necessary for studying the local monodromy around RDPs over mixed characteristic discrete valuation rings.
So in this regard our method is quite robust.

1.3.
Notations and conventions

Let
S
be a base scheme.
Given a field-valued point
s
on
S
, we write
k(s)
for its residue field; we write
\bar{s}
for a geometric point over
s
, when such a choice does not need to be specified.
When the letter
k
denotes a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
, we simply use
W
to denote its ring of Witt vectors
W(k)
.
When
k
is not necessarily perfect, we write
k^{p}
for the perfect closure of
k
.
For a morphism
f:S\to T
of finite type between schemes, we customarily use
\overline{S}
to denote some relative compactification, i.e., a scheme
\overline{S}
which is proper over
T
and contains
S
as an open dense subscheme.
We also remind the reader that a
global functional field
is the fraction field of a curve over a finite field.

By a (relative) surface
f:X\to S
we mean a separated morphism in the category of algebraic space whose fibers are of dimension
2
.
Note that a smooth proper surface over a field is automatically a scheme (
[
Art73
, Thm 4.7]
) and hence projective (
[
Kle66
, p. 328, Cor. 4]
).
Given a normal surface
X
over a field, we write
\breve{X}\to X
for the minimal resolution of
X
, which is unique up to unique isomorphism (see for example
[
Liu02
, §9.3.4, Prop. 3.32]
).

2.
Geometric preparations

To prepare for the proof of main theorems, we introduce various geometric ingredients in this section, which can be of independent interests.
These include results on F-crystals, resolutions of surface singularities, the minimal model program for threefolds, certain application of relative Abhyankar’s lemma, and geometry of elliptic surfaces in positive characteristics.

2.1.
Rigidity of F-crystals

In this subsection, we prove that the restriction functor of F-crystals along an open immersion is fully faithful, and give a fiberwise criterion for the local freeness of crystalline cohomology.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
S
be a scheme such that
p\mathcal{O}_{S}=0
,
F_{S}:S\to S
be the absolute Frobenius morphism, and
F_{S/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
be the inducded morphism on the crystalline topos
(S/\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\mathrm{crys}}
. An F-crystal
H
on
S
is a crystal of vector bundles on
(S/\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\mathrm{crys}}
together with a morphism
F:F_{S/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}^{*}H\to H
. It is said to be
non-degenerate
if for some
i\geq 0
, there exists a morphism
V:H\to F^{*}_{S/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}H
such that
VF=p^{i}\mathrm{id}_{H}
. Denote the category of non-degenerate F-crystals on
S
by
\mathrm{FCrys}(S)
.

The following theorem follows from a combination of results of
[
Jon98
]
and
[
DK17
]
:

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
with
W\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}W(k)
. Let
S
be a smooth
k
-variety and
U
be a dense open subset of
S
. Then the restriction functor
\mathrm{FCrys}(X)\to\mathrm{FCrys}(U)
is fully faithful.

Proof.

We let
M
and
N
be two objects of
\mathrm{FCrys}(X)
.
For any thickening
(T,X)\in(X/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
, denote the coherent
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-module given by evaluating
M
on
T
by
M_{T}
, and similarly for
N
.
As both
\mathrm{FCrys}(X)
and
\mathrm{FCrys}(U)
admit Zariski descent, we may assume both
U=\mathrm{Spec}(R[1/f])
and
X=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
are affine schemes, where
X
admits an étale morphism to the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
and
U=X\backslash V(f)
for a nonzerodivisor
f\in R
.
By deformation theory, there is a smooth
p
-adic affine formal scheme
\tilde{X}=\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})
over
W
together with a semilinear endomorphism
F_{\tilde{X}}:\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}
that lifts
X
with its Frobenius morphism.
Moreover, the object
(\tilde{X},X)
forms a weakly final object in the crystalline site
(X/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
.
We let
\tilde{f}
be any lift of
f
in
\tilde{R}
, and let
\tilde{U}=\mathrm{Spf}((\tilde{R}[1/\tilde{f}])^{\wedge}_{p})
and the
p
-complete localization of
\tilde{R}
at
\tilde{f}
.
Then similarly the thickening
(\tilde{U},U)
covers the crystalline site
(U/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
.
Here we notice that by construction, the map of rings
\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X})\to\mathcal{O}(\tilde{U})
is injective.

We first prove the faithfulness. By definition
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FCrys}}(M,N)
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Crys}}(M,N)
that preserves the Frobenius structures (and similarly for the restrictions on
U
), and it suffices to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Crys}}(M,N)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Crys}}(M|_{U},N|_{U})
is injective.
Moreover, since the thickenings
(\tilde{X},X)
and
(\tilde{U},U)
are weakly final in the sites
(X/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
and
(U/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
respectively,
the maps
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Crys}}(M,N)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}}(M_{\tilde{X}},N_{\tilde{X}})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Crys}}(M|_{U},N|_{U})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{U}}}(M_{\tilde{U}},N_{\tilde{U}})
are injective.
Thus it is left to show the injection of the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}}(M_{\tilde{X}},N_{\tilde{X}})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{U}}}(M_{\tilde{U}},N_{\tilde{U}})
, which follows from the local freeness of
M
and
N
together with the injection of the rings
\tilde{R}\to(\tilde{R}[1/\tilde{f}])^{\wedge}_{p}
.
Here we also notice that by the local freeness again, the group
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}}(M_{\tilde{X}},N_{\tilde{X}})
is
p
-torsionfree, and thus so is the subgroup
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FCrys}}(M,N)
.

We then prove the fullness.
Let
\varphi:M|_{U}\to N|_{U}
be a morphism between their restrictions to
U
.
By
[
DK17
, Thm 2.2.3]
, there exists some
r\geq 0
such that
p^{r}\varphi
can be extended to a morphism
\psi:M\to N
.
Moreover, continuing with the discussion in the last paragraph, an arrow in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FCrys}}(M,N)
is equivalent to a map of
\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}
-modules
M_{\tilde{X}}\to N_{\tilde{X}}
that is compatible with connections and Frobenius structures (
[
BO83
, Thm. 6.6]
).
Thus by the
p
-torsionfreeness of the hom groups, it suffices to show that
\psi\in p^{r}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}}(M_{\tilde{X}},N_{\tilde{X}})
.

Now we may adapt the argument of
[
Mau14
, Lem. 6.15]
. By induction we may assume
r=1
.
Up to possibly shrinking
X
and choosing bases of
M
and
N
, the map
\psi
is given by a matrix
P
with entries in
\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}
, and
it suffices to show that the entries of the matrix are contained in
p\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{X}}
.
Let
s\in X
be any
k
-point.
We may choose an affine smooth irreducible curve
C\subseteq X
which passes through
s
and
C\cap U\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{C,s}
be the localization of
\mathcal{O}_{C}
at
s
.
By
[
Jon98
, Cor. 1.2]
, the restriction of
\varphi
to
\mathrm{Spec}(K(C))
extends uniquely to a map of
F
-crystals
\varphi_{\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{C,s})}
over
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{C,s})
.
In particular, similarly to the second paragraph above, the restriction along
\mathcal{O}_{C,s}\to K(C)
is injective on hom groups, and we have
\psi|_{\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{C,s})}=p\varphi_{\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{C,s})}
.
Thus the reduction
\bar{P}
of the matrix
P
at the point
s\in\tilde{X}(k)
is zero.

Finally, we let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
given by
s
.
The above implies that the entries of the mod
p
-reduction
\bar{P}
are contained in
\mathfrak{m}
.
Since the choice of
s
is arbitrary, the entries of the reduction
\bar{P}
lie in the Jacobson ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, which is zero as
X
is reduced and finite type over
k
.
Hence the matrix
P
is divisible by
p
, which finishes the proof.
∎

For the later application, we also give a criterion on the local freeness of the integral crystalline cohomology.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
S
be a smooth
k
-variety and
f:X\to S
is a smooth proper morphism.
Suppose that for each
n
and for each closed point
s\in S
, we have
\dim\mathrm{H}^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{s}/k(s))
is equal to the rank of the F-isocrystal
R^{n}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}[1/p]
on
\mathrm{crys}(S/W)
.
Then
R^{n}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
is locally free for each
n
.

Proof.

If
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
is a point, this is a well known consequence of the universal coefficient theorem. The general case is actually not much different.
Up to shrinking
S
by an open subvariety, let us choose a formally smooth lifting
\widetilde{S}
of
S
over
W
.
We may assume that
\widetilde{S}=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
for some
p
-completely smooth
W
-algebra
R
.

Then it suffices to argue that the evaluation
\mathcal{H}
of
R^{n}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
on
\widetilde{S}
, which a priori is a coherent sheaf, is locally free over
\widetilde{S}
.
By the fiberwise criterion of the local freeness (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0FWG
]
) it suffices to show that
\dim\mathcal{H}{\otimes}k(s)=m
for all closed point
s
.

Consider
Rf_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
, which is an object in the derived category of
\mathcal{O}_{S/W}
-modules on
(S/W)_{\mathrm{crys}}
.
We let
C^{\bullet}
be the
R
-linear complex given by the evaluation of
Rf_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
on
\widetilde{S}
.
Then we have
\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet})=\mathcal{H}
.
On the other hand, by the base change formula of crystalline cohomology
[
BO78
, Cor. 7.12]
and the crystalline–de Rham comparison theorem, we have
\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet}{\otimes}_{R}^{\mathbb{L}}k(s))\simeq\mathrm{H}^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{s}/k(s))
.
Now we recall that there is a spectral sequence
[
Sta25
,
Tag 061Y
]

\mathrm{Tor}^{R}_{j}(\mathrm{H}^{i}(C^{\bullet}),k(s))\Rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i-j}(C^{\bullet}{\otimes}^{\mathbb{L}}_{R}k(s)),

inducing an injection
\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet}){\otimes}k(s)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{s}/k(s))
.
Note that by the semicontinuity of fiber dimensions of a coherent sheaf, we know that
\dim\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet}){\otimes}k(s)\geq\dim\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet}){\otimes}K(\widetilde{s})
, where
\widetilde{s}
is any lift of
s
on the generic fiber
\widetilde{S}_{\eta}
.
In addition, since
K(\widetilde{s})
is in characteristic zero, the dimension
\dim\mathrm{H}^{n}(C^{\bullet}){\otimes}K(\widetilde{s})
is equal to the rank of the
F
-isocrystal.
Hence by the assumption, the injection must in fact be an isomorphism, and we are done.
∎

2.2.
Resolving rational double points

We recall the Artin–Brieskorn resolution of the surface singularity, together with some basic notions and observations on the minimal resolution of surfaces.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
S
be a separated noetherian scheme (or algebraic space) and
f:X\to S
be a relative surface whose geometric fibers are normal.

•

A (minimal)
simultaneous resolution
of
X/S
is a smooth surface
Y/S
equipped with a proper and birational morphism
\pi:Y\to X
over
S
such that for each geometric point
s\to S
, the map of fibers
\pi_{s}:Y_{s}\to X_{s}
is the minimal resolution.

6
6

6

We remind the reader to not confuse the notion with the minimal model of a smooth surface. In particular, a minimal resolution of a (singular) surface is not necessarily a minimal model.

•

We say that
X/S
is Zariski-locally
resolvable
if
S
admits a Zariski open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
such that the restriction of
X
to
U_{\alpha}
admits a simultaneous resolution.
7
7

7

Note that simultaneous resolutions are usually non-unique when they exist.

We also record a useful fact about compactification of surfaces.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
S
be an irreducible smooth algebraic surface over a field
k
. Then there is always a smooth compactification
\overline{S}
of
S
, i.e., a smooth proper surface
\overline{S}
over
k
which contains
S
as an open dense subvariety.

Proof.

First, by Nagata’s compactification theorem
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0F41
]
, we can find some integral proper algebraic surface
S^{\prime}
over
k
which compactifies
S
. Then we obtain
\overline{S}
by applying Lipman’s theorem on resolution of surfaces
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BGP
]
. Note that condition (4) in
loc. cit.
is satisfied simply because
S^{\prime}
is quasi-excellent (see the second paragraph of
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0ADX
]
).
∎

Now we recall an important consequence of Artin–Brieskorn resolution for a family of surfaces.

Proposition 2.6

(Artin–Brieskorn resolution)
.

Let
S
be a connected smooth algebraic surface or curve over a field
k
, and let
f:X\to S
be a generically smooth relative surface.
Assume the geometric fibers of
f
are normal and have at worst RDP singularities, and the singular locus of
f
is finite over
S
.
Then there exists a surjective and generically étale morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
between smooth connected algebraic surfaces over
k
, such that
X|_{S^{\prime}}
is Zariski-locally resolvable.

Proof.

We prove the statement when
S
is a surface, the curve case follows from the similar argument, but is simpler.

By the Artin–Brieskorn resolution
[
Art74
, p. 332]
, there is an algebraic space
R
parametrizing simultaneous resolutions of
X/S
with the following properties.

(i)

The induced map on geometric points is a bijection.

(ii)

For every geometric point
s
on
S
, the induced map between Henselizations
\widetilde{R}_{s}\to\widetilde{S}_{s}
is surjective.

(iii)

The map
\widetilde{R}_{s}\to\widetilde{S}_{s}
is an isomorphism when
X_{s}
is smooth.

By taking elementary étale neighborhoods
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BGW
]
, we can find an étale cover
\{S_{\alpha}\}
of
S
, together with a finite surjective and generically étale morphism
R_{\alpha}\to S_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha
, such that that each composition
R_{\alpha}\to S_{\alpha}\to S
factors through the map
R\to S
.
Here the quasi-finiteness and the surjectivity follows from (i) and (ii), and the generic étaleness follows from (iii) and our assumption that
X/S
is generically smooth. Note that this step is quite general and does not need that
S
is a surface.

Next we resolve each
R_{\alpha}
and globalize them.
Assume that each
R_{\alpha}
or
S_{\alpha}
is irreducible, and let
U_{\alpha}
be a resolution of singularities of
R_{\alpha}
(
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BGP
]
).
Let
U
be a connected component of the fiber product of
U_{\alpha}
’s over
S
.
Then
U
dominates each
U_{\alpha}
.
By
Lemma

2.5
, we can find smooth compactifications
\overline{S}
,
\overline{U}_{\alpha}
and
\overline{U}
of
S,U_{\alpha}
and
U
respectively. Moreover, by applying
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0C5H
]
repeatedly, we may arrange that
U_{\alpha}\to S
extends to
\overline{U}_{\alpha}\to\overline{S}
, and
U\to U_{\alpha}
extends to
\overline{U}\to\overline{U}_{\alpha}
.
Then for every
\alpha,\beta
, the diagram

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{U}_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{U}_{\beta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{S}}}

commutes, because this is true when restricted to the open dense subvariety
U\subseteq\overline{U}
.
Finally, we take
S^{\prime}
to be preimage of
S
in
\overline{U}
.
∎

Finally, we check that rational double points are invariant under deformations, even in positive and mixed characteristic. This seems well known to experts on singularities. However, many classical references restrict to considering characteristic
0
, so here we provide a proof.

Proposition 2.7

(Deformation Invariance of RDP)
.

Let
R
be a strictly Henselian discrete valuation ring, and let
t
and
\bar{\eta}
be the special point and a geometric generic point of
T\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{Spec}(R)
respectively.

Let
f:X\to T
be a proper flat morphism of schemes of relative dimension
2
.
If
X_{s}
has only at worst RDP singularties, then so does
X_{\bar{\eta}}
.

Proof.

By
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BJJ
]
and the assumption on
f
, the fact that
X_{s}
is Gorenstein implies that the total space
X
is also Gorenstein.
In particular, the generic fiber
X_{\bar{\eta}}
is Gorenstein.
Since a RDP singularity is equivalent to a Gorenstein rational singularity, it remains to show that the singularity on
X_{\bar{\eta}}
is rational, i.e., the higher direct images of
\mathcal{O}_{\breve{X}_{\bar{\eta}}}
under the projection
\breve{X}_{\bar{\eta}}\to X_{\bar{\eta}}
all vanish. To check this, we may replace
T
by a finite ramified cover over which
f:X\to T
admits a simultaneous resolution. This is allowed thanks to
[
Art74
, Thm. 3]
(the deformation space of a RDP is smooth and irreducible).
Then the conclusion follows from the fact that the singularities on
X_{s}
are rational, and the semi-continuity theorem applied to the resolution.
∎

Finally we observe that there is a natural variation of Newton polygons for the minimal resolutions of a family of surfaces.

Proposition 2.8

(Upper semi-continuity of Newton polygons)
.

Let
S
be an integral algebraic variety over a field
k
of characteristic
p
, and let
f:X\to S
be a proper and generically smooth relative surface whose geometric fibers are normal with at worst rational double points singularities.
For each integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function

(3)

\psi:s\longmapsto\text{Newton polygon of}~\mathrm{H}^{n}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\breve{X}_{s^{p}}/W(k(s)^{p}))[1/p]

on
S
is upper semi-continuous.

Proof.

Let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
. Our goal is to prove that for every
s\in S
,
\psi(s)\geq\psi(\eta)
and the subset

(4)

S_{>\eta}:=\{s\in S\mid\psi(s)>\psi(\eta)\}

is a closed in
S
. By de Jong’s theory of alterations
[
Jon96
]
, there exists a smooth variety
S^{\prime}
together with a proper surjective morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
. If the proposition holds for the pullback family
X|_{S^{\prime}}
over
S^{\prime}
, then it holds for the original family
X
over
S
. Therefore, we may assume that
S
is smooth.

Similarly, by comibining Artin–Brieskorn resolution
[
Art74
]
and de Jong’s alterations
[
Jon96
]
, we can find an open cover
\{S_{\alpha}\to S\}
, together with proper surjective morphisms
U_{\alpha}\to S_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha
such that the family
X|_{R_{\alpha}}
admits simultaneous resolution
Y_{\alpha}
, and each
U_{\alpha}
is smooth over
k
(cf. the proof of
Proposition

2.6
).
In particular, we obtain the identifications between the fibers
(Y_{\alpha})_{t}
and the minimal resolution
\breve{X}_{t}
, for each point
t\in U_{\alpha}
.

Now, notice that since the closedness of a subset can be checked on an open cover, we may replace
S
by one of the
S_{\alpha}
for a fixed
\alpha
.
By the first paragraph, we may further replace

S_{\alpha}
by
U_{\alpha}
.
Therefore, we reduce to the case when
S
is smooth over
k
, and
X
is smooth over
S
.
The conclusion then follows from
[
Ked22
, Thm. 3.12]
(a), which is due to Grothendieck and Katz, plus a few additional arguments by Kedlaya.
∎

We also comment on the purity of the Newton polygon.

Remark 2.9

(Purity of Newton polygons)
.

It is natural to ask whether the variation of Newton polygons in
Proposition

2.8
satisfies purity, namely, whether
S_{>\eta}
defined in (
4
) is of codimension
1
in
S
if non-empty.

The closest result in this direction is the purity theorem by de Jong–Oort
[
JO00
, Thm. 4.1]
, which is about locally free F-crystals (not merely F-isocrystals).
Kedlaya provided an argument to show that the purity result also holds for F-isocrystals, at the expense of assuming that the base is a smooth variety (
[
Ked22
, Thm. 3.12(b)]
).

However, in the setting of
Proposition

2.8
, even when
S
is smooth we do not see a way of proving the purity of the crystalline cohomology of the minimal resolutions using the above results:
If we use
[
Art74
]
to resolve the family
X
over a quasi-finite cover
S^{\prime}
of
S
, then the problem is that we do not know if the crystalline cohomology of
X_{S^{\prime}}
is locally-free, or has a locally-free lattice;
if we further resolve this quasi-finite cover by a smooth cover using alterations, then the problem is that we lose control on the fiberwise relative dimensions, as alterations are only generically finite.
However, the situation of
Proposition

2.8
naturally appears when we consider a coarse moduli of surfaces, so it will be interesting to figure out whether purity still holds despite these obstructions. In particular, it is potentially useful for the Tate conjecture (cf.
Remark

4.35
).

2.3.
Specialization of line bundles and cohomology

For later use, we discuss some generalities on specializations of line bundles and cohomology.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
S
be a noetherian scheme with geometric points
t
and
s
.
A
path
from
t
to
s
is a tuple
(\gamma,t^{\prime}\to t,s^{\prime}\to s)
, consisting of a morphism of schemes
\gamma:T\to S
together with morphisms of geometric points
t^{\prime}\to t
and
s^{\prime}\to s
, where
T=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
for a strictly Henselian discrete valuation ring
R
, and
t^{\prime},s^{\prime}
are geometric generic and special points on
T
respectively, satisfying the condition that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.}

We abbreviate the tuple
(\gamma,t^{\prime}\to t,s^{\prime}\to s,t\rightsquigarrow s)
as
\gamma:t\rightsquigarrow s
.
Note that a path induces an étale path from
t
to
s
in the sense of
[
Sta25
,
Tag 03VD
]
, which we shall denote by the same letter.

We recall some basic facts about the Néron–Severi group which we may often use implicitly.

Theorem 2.11
.

Suppose that
X\to S
is a smooth and proper morphism between algebraic spaces with irreducible geometric fibers. Then we have the following.

(a)

The relative Picard functor
\mathrm{Pic}\,_{X/S}
is representable by a separated algebraic space locally of finite type over
S
.

(b)

Each finite type closed subspace of
\mathrm{Pic}\,_{X/S}
is proper over
S
.

(c)

If
S
is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring with the generic point
\eta
, then there is a natural isomorphism
\mathrm{Pic}\,(X_{\eta})\simeq\mathrm{Pic}\,(X)
.

(d)

If
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
and
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
is an extension of separably closed fields, then the base change map
\mathrm{NS}(X_{k})\to\mathrm{NS}(X_{k^{\prime}})
is an isomorphism.

(e)

Every path
\gamma:t\rightsquigarrow s
on
S
naturally determines a specialization map

\mathrm{sp}_{\gamma}:\mathrm{NS}(X_{t})\to\mathrm{NS}(X_{s}).

which is injective after tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Part (a) and Part (b) are stated in
[
BLR90
, §8.3, Thm. 3]
, whose proof implies Part (c) (cf.
[
MP12
, Eqn.(3.4)]
):
The idea is that a Weil divisor on
X_{\eta}
gives rise to a Weil divisor on
X
by taking the Zariski closure, and any line bundle on
X
is trivial if its restriction on
X_{\eta}
is trivial.
Part (d) follows from Part (a) because the Néron–Severi group
\mathrm{NS}(X_{k})
is identified with the set of connected components of
\mathrm{Pic}\,_{X/k}
, and hence does not increase under a separably closed extension (cf.
[
MP12
, Prop. 3.1]
).
Part (e) follows from Part (c) and Part (d).
Note that unlike Prop. 3.3 of
loc. cit.
, our specialization map is canonically defined, because by
Definition

2.10
a path is defined over a strictly Henselian ring.
For the injectivity statement in (e), see
[
MP12
, Prop. 3.6]
.
∎

We remark that the specialization maps also exist for endomorphisms of abelian schemes. In this context, Part (c) of
Theorem

2.11
is a consequence of the Néron extension property.

Recall that simultaneous resolutions of a family of surfaces are often non-unique, even over a discrete valuation ring (cf. page 1 of
[
Art74
]
). However, the specializations of Néron–Severi classes or cohomology groups do not depend on the resolution, due to the following observation.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{Spec}(R)
for a strictly Henselian discrete valuation ring
R
, with
s
the special point,
\eta
the generic point, and
\bar{\eta}
a geometric point over
\eta
.
Let
X
be a relative surface over
S
with normal geometric fibers such that
X_{\eta}
is smooth and
X_{s}
is not ruled. Suppose that
\pi:Y\to X
and
\pi^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to X
are two simultaneous resolutions. Let
f_{*}:Y_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Y^{\prime}_{*}
be the unique isomorphism such that
\pi^{\prime}_{*}\circ f_{*}=\pi_{*}
for
*=s,\eta
. Then the following diagram commutes:

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y_{\bar{\eta}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y^{\prime}_{\bar{\eta}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y^{\prime}_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal isomorphisms are given by the smooth and proper base change theorem.
8
8

8

Or in other words, the isomorphisms are given by the étale path
\bar{\eta}\to s
induced by
T
.

We remark that the map
f_{\eta}
exists simply because
\pi_{\eta}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{\eta}
are isomorphisms.
The map
f_{s}
exists because
\pi_{s}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
are birational, and
Y_{s}
and
Y^{\prime}_{s}
are both the minimal resolution of
X_{s}
, so that any birational map
Y_{s}\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{s}
extends to an isomorphism.

Proof.

We recall the basic fact of étale cohomology that both of the vertical arrows are induced from the cycle classes of the correspondences (cf.
[
Mil13
, Lem. 25.3]
).
Let
\Gamma_{*}\subseteq Y_{*}\times_{*}Y^{\prime}_{*}
be the graph of
f_{*}
for
*=s,\eta
, which we shall view as a correspondence between
Y_{*}
and
Y^{\prime}_{*}
. Let
\Gamma_{\eta,s}
denote the specialization of
\Gamma_{\eta}
(i.e., the special fiber of the Zariski closure of
\Gamma_{\eta}
in
Y\times_{S}Y^{\prime}
).
Then the diagram (
5
) commutes if the map
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(Y_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
is induced by the correspondence
\Gamma_{\eta,s}
because cycle class maps are compatible with specialization.
However, by
[
MM64
, Thm 1]
we know that
\Gamma_{\eta,s}=\Gamma_{s}+\Gamma_{s}^{\prime}
, where the projections of
\Gamma_{s}^{\prime}
to
Y_{s}^{\prime}
and
Y_{s}
are both
0
(as an algebraic cycle).
Therefore,
\Gamma_{\eta,s}
and
\Gamma_{s}
induce the same map
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{Y}_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{Y}_{s},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
.
Technically,
[
MM64
, Thm 1]
is phrased in terms of schemes, but it does hold for algebraic spaces in the generality which is applicable in the current context, see
[
FLTZ22
, Thm A.2]
(see also
[
Mat15
, Thm 5.4]
).
∎

At the end of the subsection, we record an extension result for the cohomology sheaves of a family of surfaces, which is often used together with the above observations.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
S
be a normal integral Noetherian scheme, and
f:X\to S
be a proper relative surface whose geometric fibers are normal and nonruled, such that the generic fiber
X_{\eta}
is smooth.
Let
S^{\circ}\subseteq S
be the maximal open subscheme such that
f^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f|_{S^{\circ}}
is smooth.
Assume that
X\to S
is Zariski-locally resolvable.

Then for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and each prime
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\times}
, the
\ell
-adic sheaf
R^{i}f^{\circ}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
over
S^{\circ}
extends uniquely to an
\ell
-adic sheaf
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
over
S
.
Moreover, the extension has the following properties:

(a)

For every open
U\subseteq S
and a simultaneous resolution
g:Y\to U
of
f|_{U}
, the identification of
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
and
R^{i}g_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
over
U\cap S^{\circ}
extends to
U
.

(b)

For every geometric point
\bar{s}
over a point
s\in S
, there is a canonical
\mathrm{Gal}_{k(s)}
-equivariant isomorphism
(\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})_{\bar{s}}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\breve{X}_{\bar{s}},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
, where
\breve{X}_{\overline{s}}
is the minimal resolution of
X_{\overline{s}}
.

Proof.

Recall that for a normal integral Noetherian scheme
T
with open dense
V\subseteq T
, the restriction functor bewteen the categories of finite étale covers
\textsf{F{\'{E}}t}(T)\to\textsf{F{\'{E}}t}(V)
is fully faithful (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BQI
,
Tag 0BQ3
]
) and the natural map
\pi_{1}(V)\to\pi_{1}(T)
is surjective (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BN6
]
) for any choice of base points.
In particular, To construct
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, we find an open cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
S
such that on each
U_{\alpha}
,
X
admits a simultaneous resolution
g_{\alpha}:Y_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
.
As
Y_{\alpha}|_{U_{\alpha}\cap S^{\circ}}=X|_{U_{\alpha}\cap S^{\circ}}
, we have identifications bewteen
R^{i}g_{\alpha,\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
and
R^{i}g_{\beta,\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
over
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\cap S
, and hence over
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
by the full-faithfulness above.
Therefore, we can glue these sheaves.
This proves (a), and (b) additionally follows from
Lemma

2.12
.
∎

Construction 2.14
.

The full-faithfulness used above is also true for
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS (resp. F-isoscrystals) when
S
is a smooth variety over
\mathbb{C}
(resp. over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
), as in
[
PS03
, Cor. 12]
(resp.
[
DK17
, Thm. 2.2.3]
).
Therefore, we likewise define
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS
(\breve{R}^{i}f_{*}\mathbb{Z},\breve{R}^{i}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X/S})
(resp. F-isocrystal
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}[1/p]
) to be the one satisfying the analogous properties of
Lemma

2.13
.
Moreover, in the latter situation, if
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
is locally free, then we apply
Theorem

2.2
to define an F-crystal
\breve{R}^{i}f_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{X/W}
over
S
.

2.4.
A Beauville–Laszlo gluing lemma for projective schemes

In the following, for a given scheme
S
, we let
\mathcal{C}_{S}
be the category of pairs
(Z,\mathcal{L})
, where
Z
is a projective scheme over
S
and
\mathcal{L}
is a relative ample line bundle for
Z/S
.
We also let
\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S}
be the category of projective
S
-schemes.
Then we have a variant of the Beauville–Laszlo gluing but for projective schemes.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
S
be a smooth variety over
k
, let
s\in S
be a closed point, and let
U
be its complement open subvariety
S\backslash\{s\}
,
We temporarily denote
S_{s}
to be the affine scheme
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{S,s})
, and let
W\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}U\times_{S}S_{s}
be the fiber product.

(i)

There is a natural functor of categories

\mathcal{C}_{U}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{W}}\mathcal{C}_{S_{s}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S},

whose composition with the pullback functor
\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S}\to\mathrm{Proj\,}_{U}\times_{\mathrm{Proj\,}_{W}}\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S_{s}}
is identified with the forgetful functor.

(ii)

Assume
X\to S
is a qcqs algebraic space such that there are ample line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{U}
and
\mathcal{L}_{s}
over
X_{U}
and
X_{s}
respectively that coincide after base changing to
X_{W}
.
Then the map
X\to S
is projective, and in particular
X
is an algebraic scheme.

Proof.

As the statement is Zariski local with respect to
S
, we may and do assume that
S
is an affine scheme.
For (i), let
\bigl((X_{U},\mathcal{L}_{U}),(X_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}),\alpha_{W}:(X_{U},\mathcal{L}_{U})|_{W}\simeq(X_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s})|_{W})
be an object in the fiber product category
\mathcal{C}_{U}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{W}}\mathcal{C}_{S_{s}}
.
We first show that there is a projective scheme
\mathbb{P}
over
S
whose base changes to
U
,
S_{s}
and
W
admit compatible closed immersions from
X_{U},X_{s}
and
X_{W}
respectively.
We let
\mathcal{A}_{U}
and
\mathcal{A}_{s}
be the graded rings
\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(X_{U},\mathcal{L}_{U}^{i})
and
\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{H}^{0}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}^{i})
respectively.
Then we get the following closed immersions into projective spaces

X_{U}\to\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U}),\quad X_{s}\to\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s}).

On the other hand, by assumption the base changes at
X_{W}
satisfy
\alpha_{W}:(\mathcal{L}_{U})|_{W}\simeq(\mathcal{L}_{s})|_{W}
.
Moreover, since both
S_{s}
and
U
are flat over
S
, by flat base change for coherent cohomology (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 02KH
]
), we get

\mathrm{H}^{0}(X_{U},\mathcal{L}_{U}^{i})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}\mathcal{O}_{W}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{0}((X_{U})|_{W},(\mathcal{L}_{U})|_{W}^{i}),\quad\mathrm{H}^{0}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}_{s}^{i})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{s}}}\mathcal{O}_{W}\simeq\mathrm{H}^{0}((X_{s})|_{W},(\mathcal{L}_{s})|_{W}^{i}).

As a consequence, the closed immersions
X_{U}\to\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U})
and
X_{s}\to\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s})
are identified via
\alpha
after base changing to
W
, and we get a commutative diagram of closed immersions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}\mathcal{O}_{W})\simeq\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{s}}}\mathcal{O}_{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s}),}

where both squares are cartesian.

Then we recall from
[
Bha16
, Prop. 5.6.(4)]
that the base change functors induce a symmetric monoidal equivalence of the categories

(6)

\mathrm{Qcoh}_{S}\simeq\mathrm{Qcoh}_{U}\times_{\mathrm{Qcoh}_{W}}\mathrm{Qcoh}_{S_{s}},

which restricts to an equivalence of coherent sheaves by
[
Bha16
, Prop. 5.6.(2)]
and the regularity of
S
.
Apply this at the graded quasi-coherent algebras
\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{U},\mathcal{A}_{s},\alpha:(\mathcal{A}_{U})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}\mathcal{O}_{W}\simeq(\mathcal{A}_{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{s}}}\mathcal{O}_{W}\bigr)
,
we see there is a graded quasi-coherent algebra
\mathcal{A}
over
S
that specializes to
\mathcal{A}_{U}
and
\mathcal{A}_{s}
and is compatible with transition isomorphism
\alpha
.
Moreover, as both
\mathcal{A}_{U}
and
\mathcal{A}_{s}
is generated by first finite amount of graded components, so is
\mathcal{A}
.
Furthermore, notice that since each
\mathcal{A}_{U,i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{s,i}
are coherent, we see each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is coherent over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
as well.
As a consequence,
\mathcal{A}
is a finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebra,
and we can take
\mathbb{P}
to be the projective scheme
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A})
over
S
.
Then the base changes of
\mathbb{P}
to
U
and
S_{s}
coincide with projective spaces
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U})
and
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s})
, which are compatible with the transition isomorphism
\alpha
.
For simplicity, we will use
\mathbb{P}_{U}
,
\mathbb{P}_{s}
and
\mathbb{P}_{W}
to denote
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U})
,
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s})
and
\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}\mathcal{O}_{W})\simeq\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathcal{A}_{s}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{s}}}\mathcal{O}_{W})
from now.

Now we proceed to produce a projective scheme
X
over
S
as a closed subscheme of
\mathbb{P}
, which specializes to the given data at the beginning.
Let
V\subset\mathbb{P}
be an affine open subscheme, and let
\mathbb{V}_{\ast}
for
\ast\in\{U,s,W\}
be its base changes,
which are affine open inside
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
by the affineness of the morphisms
U\to S
,
S_{s}\to S
and
W\to S
.
Moreover, the fiber products
V_{\ast}\times_{\mathbb{P}_{\ast}}X_{\ast}
are affine open subspaces of
X_{\ast}
for
\ast\in\{U,s,W\}
.
So by Beauville–Laszlo gluing (
6
) above, we can produce an affine closed scheme
T_{V}\subset V
that specializes to
V_{\ast}\times_{\mathbb{P}_{\ast}}X_{\ast}
for
\ast\in\{U,s,W\}
under natural base changes.
Finally, by
[
Bha16
, Prop. 5.6.(6)]
, we know the equivalence (
6
) is compatible with flat base changes of
V
(in particular open immersions).
So by ranging over all affine open
V\subset\mathbb{P}
, we can glue
T_{V}
to a closed scheme
X
inside
\mathbb{P}
, which specializes to
X_{U}
,
X_{s}
and
X_{W}
under base changes.

To prove (ii), we first notice that by (i), we can find a projective scheme
X^{\prime}
over
S
such that its image under the functor

\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Proj\,}_{U}\times_{\mathrm{Proj\,}_{W}}\mathrm{Proj\,}_{S_{s}}

is
(X_{U},X_{s},\alpha:(X_{U})|_{W}\simeq(X_{s})|_{W})
.
So it is left to show that as qcqs algebraic spaces,
X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic.
By construction of
X^{\prime}
, we know it satisfies the formula that
X^{\prime}\times_{S}\ast\simeq X_{\ast}
, which also holds true for
X
by assumption.
In particular, the assumption of
[
Bha16
, Prop. 5.6]
applies to both
X
and
X^{\prime}
.
As a consequence, by
[
Bha16
, Prop. 5.6.(6)]
, both
X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic to the pushout of the diagram below in the category of qcqs algebraic spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{s}.}

Hence by the uniqueness of the pushout, we see
X\simeq X^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Recently, Achinger and Youcis proved that a Beauville-Laszlo gluing datum is always effective in the category of algebraic spaces (
[
AY24
]
). As illustrated by Matsumoto, such a statement is false for projective schemes because there may not be a pair of ample line bundles that glue (
[
Mat15
, Ex. 5.2]
, cf.
[
AY24
, Ex. 3.4]
).
Our
Proposition

2.15
.(ii) above is saying that this is the only obstruction for the statement to be true for projective schemes.

2.5.
An application of MMP for threefolds

Theorem 2.17
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
or
p\geq 5
.
Suppose that
X
and
X^{\prime}
are smooth
k
-threefolds and
f:X\to S
and
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S
are projective morphisms between smooth quasi-projective varieties such that
K_{X}
and
K_{X^{\prime}}
are both relatively nef over
S
. Then every birational map
\alpha:X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
can be decomposed into a sequence of flops.

Proof.

When
k
is of characteristic
0
, this is a special case of
[
Kaw08
, Thm. 1]
when we are simply taking the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors
B
and
B^{\prime}
therein to be
0
.
The proof only relies on the existence of minimal model program (MMP), including cone theorem, the based point free theorem, and termination of MMP with scaling, which all hold true for threefolds in characteristic
p\geq 5
by
[
HX15
,
HW22
]
.
For the reader’s convenience, we briefly sketch the proof of
[
Kaw08
]
below for general
B
and
B^{\prime}
, explaining the necessary modifications in characteristic
p
.

Without any change, the first two paragraphs of the proof of
[
Kaw08
, Thm. 1]
shows that the map
\alpha
is an isomorphism in codimension one, and there are only flips in this MMP, since the other operation (which is a divisor contraction) changes the picard group (
[
KM98
, §3.31]
).
In particular, for a Cartier divisor
L
on
X
with its strict transform
L^{\prime}
on
X^{\prime}
, we have
\mathrm{H}^{i}(X,\mathcal{O}(L))\simeq\mathrm{H}^{i}(X^{\prime},\mathcal{O}(L^{\prime}))
, and
L
is big if and only if
L^{\prime}
is so (
[
KM98
, Def. 2.59]
).
We then choose
L^{\prime}
an effective
f^{\prime}
-ample divisor on
X^{\prime}
, with
L
its strict transform on
X
and
l\ll 1
a positive rational number such that
(X,B+lL)
is klt (which exists since being klt is an open condition).
In this case, the divisor
L
is big and can be written as the sum
A+E
, where
A
is
f
-ample and
E
is
f
-nef.
Moreover, for a small enough
\epsilon>0
, by the openness of the klt condition again, the pair
(X,B+(l-\epsilon)L+\epsilon E)
is klt as well.
In particular, the base point free theorem in characteristic
p

[
ABL22
, Cor. 1.5]
(applied at the log pair
(X,B+(l-\epsilon)L+\epsilon E)
) implies that the sum
m(K_{X}+B+lL)
is
f
-free for
m\in\mathbb{N}
large enough.
As a consequence, the global sections of a positive multiple of the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
K_{X}+B+lL
defines a projective
S
-morphism and coincides with that of the
f^{\prime}
-ample
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
K_{X^{\prime}}+B^{\prime}+lL^{\prime}
.
The latter implies that the map
\alpha
is an isomorphism.
Hence we may assume
K_{X}+B+l^{\prime}L
is not
f
-nef over
S
for
0<l^{\prime}\leq l
.
Furthermore, since
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and
B
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor, by
[
KM98
, Def. 6.10]
we may assume
l
is small enough so that a flop for
(X,B)
is the same as a flip for
(X,B+l^{\prime}L)
.

To proceed, we let
H
be an effective divisor on
X
such that
(X,B+lL+tH)
is klt and
K_{X}+B+lL+tH
is
f
-nef for some
t>0
.

our goal is to run the minimal model program for the pair
(X,B+l^{\prime}L)
with the scaling
H
, so that the index
l^{\prime}
is decreasing each time by a ratio
e
that only depends on
X
and
B
.
To this end, we let

e=\frac{1}{2m\dim(X)+1}

for a positive integer
m
with
m(K_{X}+B)
being a Cartier divisor.
Then
[
Kaw08
, Lem. 2]
shows that there exists an extremal ray
R
for
(X,B+elL)
over
S
such that
K_{X}+B+elL+et_{0}H
is
f
-nef, and

((K_{X}+B+elL+et_{0}H)\cdot R)=((K_{X}+B)\cdot R)=0,

where

\displaystyle t_{0}=\min\{t\in\mathbb{R}\mid

\displaystyle((K_{X}+B+lL+tH)\cdot R)\geq 0\text{ for all extremal rays }R\text{ for }

\displaystyle(X,B+lL)\text{ over }S\text{ such that }((K_{X}+B)\cdot R)=0\}.

Here we note that the only input of the above existence in
loc. cit.
is the inequality
0>((K_{X}+B+lL)\cdot C)\geq-6
, which is proved in
[
HW22
, Thm. 1.4]
.

Finally, we may run MMP for the pair
(X,B+elL)
with scaling of
H
.
By
[
HW22
, Thm. 1.1]
, the flip for the contraction morphism
\pi:X\to Z
with respect to the extremal ray
R
exists.
Moreover, by the equalities above, since
(K_{X}+B+lL+t_{0}H)\cdot R=0
, the
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor
(K_{X}+B+lL+t_{0}H)
and its strict transform on the flip are relatively numerically trivial with respect
Z
(and thus are anti-nef and nef with respect to
Z
separately).
So thanks to the negativity lemma (
[
KM98
, Lem. 3.38]
), we know the pair
(X,B+lL+t_{0}H)
remains klt after the flip.
Furthermore, the equalities above imply that the
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor
k(K_{X}+B)-k(K_{X}+B+elL)=(-elkL)
is
\pi
-big.
We can thus apply the base point free theorem
[
ABL22
, Cor. 1.5]
at the
\pi
-nef Cartier divisor
K_{X}+B
and the log pair
(X,B+elL)
, to see that there exists a large
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that both
mk(K_{X}+B)
and
(m+1)k(K_{X}+B)
(and so is their difference
k(K_{X}+B)
) lift to ample Cartier divisors of some projective morphism
Y\to Z
.
But notice that since the map
X\to Z
is defined by contracting the extremal ray
R
, the equation
k(K_{X}+B)\cdot R=0
enforces that
Y=Z
and
k(K_{X}+B)
is defined over
Z
.
Hence the divisor
k(K_{X}+B)
remains a Cartier divisor after the flip.
In this way, we can continue the process for
(X,B+elL)
with the scaling
H
, which terminates after finite amount of steps by
[
HW22
, Thm. 1.2]
.
So we are done.∎

Corollary 2.18

(Resolution of a constant family is constant)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p\geq 5
.
Let
X_{0}
be a projective surface over
k
with only RDP singularities and
X=X_{0}\times C
for some smooth curve
C
over
k
.
Let
Y_{0}
be the minimal resolution of
X_{0}
, and let
(Y/C,\pi:Y\to X)
be any minimal simultaneous resolution of
X/C
.
Then
Y/C
is isomorphic to the constant family
Y_{0}\times C
.

Proof.

Let us first prove the statement assuming that the total space
Y
is a quasi-projective over
k
.
We first notice that the assumption that
X_{0}
only has RDP singularities implies that
K_{Y}
is
\pi
-nef.
To see this, it suffices to show that for any
\pi
-exceptional curve
D
, the intersection
K_{Y}\cdot D
is non-negative.
As
\pi
is fiberwise the minimal resolution,
D
lies in a single fiber, i.e.,
D\subseteq Y_{c}
for some
c\in C(k)
.
Moreover, by the RDP assumption, the curve
D
is a rational
(-2)
-curve on
Y_{c}
and in particular we have
D^{2}=-2
.
So by adjunction formula of surfaces, we get
(K_{Y}+D)\cdot D=\mathrm{deg}(K_{D})=-2
, and thus
K_{Y}\cdot D=0
.

Note that as both
Y
and
Y_{0}\times C
are minimal resolutions of
X
, they are birational equivalent and are relative nef over
C
.
By the algebraicity assumption of
Y
and by
Theorem

2.17
, any birational morphism of varieties
Y_{0}\times C\dashrightarrow Y
decomposes into a sequence of flops.
However, there are no flops that can be defined on
Y_{0}\times C
.
Indeed, in order to do so one must choose a numerical class of relative exceptional curves (namely a class of those curves
D\subset Y_{0}\times C
that are mapped onto single points in
C
.
But since
Y_{0}\times C
is a constant family, every exceptional curve
D_{c}
is contained in a single fiber
Y_{0}\times c
and in particular is algebraic equivalent to a curve
D_{c^{\prime}}\subset Y_{0}\times c^{\prime}
, where
c^{\prime}\subset C
is any point that is in the same connected component of
c
.
As the consequence, the contraction of the relative numerical class
[D_{c}]
will simultaneously contracting a codimension
1
locus in
Y_{0}\times C
, which in particular would not be a flop (
[
KM98
, Def.6.10]
).

Now we consider the general case that
Y
is an algebraic space, and we want to prove that
Y
is in fact a scheme.
By
Lemma

2.19
below, there exists an open dense subscheme
U\subseteq C
such that
Y|_{U}
is a scheme, so that by the previous paragraphs we know
Y|_{U}\simeq Y_{0}\times U
.
By induction we may assume that
C\smallsetminus U
is a single point
c
.
Let
C_{c}
(resp.
\widehat{C}_{c}
) be the spectrum of the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{C,c}
of
C
at
c
(resp. formal completion of
C
at
c
), and similarly let
Y|_{C_{c}}
(resp.
Y_{\widehat{C}_{c}}
be the base change of
Y
at
C_{c}
(resp. formal completion of
Y
at
c
).
Then
Y|_{C_{c}}
(resp.
Y|_{\widehat{C}_{c}}
) is a smooth proper algebraic space over
C_{c}
(resp. formal scheme over
\widehat{C}_{c}
), and there is a natural map
\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{C_{c}})\to\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{\widehat{C}_{c}})
.
Let
\eta
be the generic point of
C
and thus for
C_{c}
.
There is then a specialization map
\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{\eta})\to\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y|_{C_{c}})\to\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{c})
(
Theorem

2.11
).
Moreover, by the assumption that
Y|_{U}=Y_{0}\times U
, we know
Y_{\eta}=Y_{0}{\otimes}_{k}k(\eta)
, and we can form a diagram

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{\eta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{c}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\otimes}_{k}k(\eta)}
specialize
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textrm{identify }Y_{0}\simeq Y_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

which a priori may not commute.
However, we can tell that the injectivity the morphism
\mathrm{NS}(Y_{\eta})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{NS}(Y_{c})_{\mathbb{Q}}
(which is true thanks to
Theorem

2.11
.(e)) implies its surjectivity, as the source and the target of the map are at least abstractly isomorphic, thanks to
Theorem

2.11
.(d).
So as the ampleness is a numerical condition, the latter implies that there exists some
\zeta\in\mathrm{Pic}\,(Y_{\eta})
which specializes to an ample line bundle
\zeta\in Y_{c}
.
Furthermore, since the effectivity and the intersection numbers of line bundles on
Y_{\eta}
are preserved under specialization, by the Nakai–Moishezon criterion, we know
\zeta
must also be ample.
Therefore, we may now apply
Proposition

2.15
to conclude that
Y
is projective over
C
and hence is trivial by the first two paragraphs above.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
S
be an integral scheme with generic point
\eta
and let
f:X\to S
be a smooth and proper morphism in the category of algebraic spaces.
If the generic fiber
X_{\eta}
is a (projective) scheme, then up to shrinking
S
(i.e., replacing
S
by an open dense subscheme
U\subseteq S
) such that
X
is also a (projective) scheme.

Proof.

By spreading out the scheme
X_{\eta}
, and by shrinking
S
if necessary, we can find a smooth and proper scheme
X^{\prime}
over
S
such that there is an isomorphism
\psi_{\eta}:X^{\prime}_{\eta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}X_{\eta}
.
It then suffices to show that up to shrinking
S
again, the isomorphism
\psi_{\eta}
can be extended to an isomorphism
\psi:X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}X
in the category of algebraic spaces over
S
. Then by
[
Ols06
, Thm 1.1]
the fibered categories
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X^{\prime},X)
,
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X^{\prime})
and
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X)
are in fact algebraic spaces locally of finite presentation over
S
. Now consider the fiber
Z
of the morphism given by composition

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X^{\prime},X)\times\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X^{\prime})\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X)

over
\mathrm{id}_{X}\in\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X)
. Then
Z
is also an algebraic space locally of finite presentation and the pair
(\psi_{\eta},\psi^{-1}_{\eta})
defines a point in
Z(k(\eta))
. Now by
[
Sta25
,
Tag 05N0
]
, up to shrinking
S
, we may extend this
k(\eta)
-point to an
S
-point, which justifies that we can spread out
\psi_{\eta}
to an isomorphism
\psi:X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}X
.
∎

Remark 2.20
.

In
Corollary

2.18
, as the minimal resolutions of the geometric fibers are unique, it is easy to see that
Y/C
is isotrivial (i.e., has isomorphic geometric fibers over the same residue field).
The content here is that
Y/C
must in fact be trivial on the nose.
Below we sketch another proof of
Corollary

2.18
using Artin’s representability statement
[
Art74
, Thm. 1]
, where assuming
p\geq 5
is not needed.

Consider the resolution functor
R\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{Res}_{X_{0}/k}
defined in page
1
of
loc. cit.
.
Any simultaneous resolution of
X_{0}\times C
would define a morphism
C\to R
.
Now, by
[
Art74
, Thm. 1]
, we know
R
is represented by a
quasi-separated
algebraic space over
k
and has a single geometric point.
On the other hand, a theorem of Rydh implies that algebraic space with a single point is a scheme if and only if it is decent
[
Sta25
,
Tag 047Z
,
086U
]
.
As a consequence, since quasi-separated algebraic spaces are indeed decent
[
Sta25
,
Tag 03I7
]
, we know
R
is a scheme such that
R_{\mathrm{red}}=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
.
As
C
is reduced,
C\to R
factors through
R_{\mathrm{red}}
and the result follows.

2.6.
Hodge diamond of elliptic surfaces in positive characteristic

Let
C
be a smooth projective curve over an algebraically closed field
k
. Let
X
be a smooth surface over
k
equipped with an elliptic fibration
\pi:X\to C
.
Assume that there exists a zero section
\sigma:C\to X
.
In particular,
X
has no multiple fibers.
Let
L:=(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\vee}
be the fundamental line bundle (cf.
[
Mir89
, II.4]
).
Set
h:=\mathrm{deg\,}L
.

Proposition 2.21
.

If the
j
-invariant map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is finite and separable, then
\Omega_{C/k}\,{\cong}\,\pi_{*}\Omega_{X/k}
.

In fact, here it is unnecessary for
C
to be projective—it works for an affine curve as well. The argument below is essentially an expansion of Liu’s answer to a MathOverflow question (
[
Liu
]
), which works verbatim in positive characteristics as well as long as the
j
-invariant map is separable.

Proof.

Consider the canonical short exact sequence

0\to\pi^{*}\Omega_{C/k}\to\Omega_{X/k}\to\Omega_{X/C}\to 0,

which induces a long exact sequence

0\to\Omega_{C/k}\to\pi_{*}\Omega_{X/k}\to\pi_{*}\Omega_{X/C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\to}}(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}){\otimes}\Omega_{C/k}\to\cdots.

To see that
\pi^{*}\Omega_{C/k}\to\Omega_{X/k}
is injective, note that over some open dense
U\subseteq C
, the map
\pi|_{U}
is smooth, so that
(\pi|_{U})^{*}\Omega_{U/k}=\pi^{*}\Omega_{C/k}|_{X_{U}}\to\Omega_{X_{U}/k}
is smooth.
Let
\iota:U\hookrightarrow C
and
\widetilde{\iota}:X|_{U}\hookrightarrow X
be the natural inclusions. Then there is a commutative diagram
