\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.89 -24.01 L 21.73 -24.01}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.70506pt}{-17.35268pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -7.89 -24.01)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.71 C 0.95 2.71 1.72 2.1 1.72 1.36 C 1.72 0.61 0.95 0 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.90501pt}{-17.35268pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 22.01 -24.01)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The morphism
\theta
is generically injective, as the
j
-invariant map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a finite and separable. Indeed, let
s\in C(k)
be a general point, the fiber
\theta_{s}
is nothing but the Kodaira–Spencer map

\mathrm{H}^{0}(\Omega_{X_{s}})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{s}}){\otimes}(T_{s}C)^{\vee}.

To finish the proof, it suffices to show that
\pi_{*}\Omega_{X/C}
is torsion-free (or equivalently a vector bundle, since
C
is a curve), because then
\ker(\theta)
, being a rank
0
subsheaf of a torsion-free, has to vanish.
This then implies that
\Omega_{C/k}\to\pi_{*}\Omega_{X/k}
is an isomorphism.

Write
\Omega
for
\Omega_{X/C}
and
\omega
for the relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{X/C}
.
Now consider the decomposition
0\to\Omega_{\mathrm{tor}}\to\Omega\to\mathcal{F}\to 0
where
\mathcal{F}
is the torsion-free quotient. By the flat base change theorem and
[
LS00
, Prop. 1]
,
\mathrm{H}^{0}(\Omega_{\mathrm{tor}})=0
.
Here we note that the assumption of
loc. cit.
is satisfied thanks to the existence of the zero section: For any
s\in C(k)
, the fiber
X_{s}
has at least an irreducible component of multiplicity
1
, i.e., the one which meets the zero section.
It remains to show that
\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F})
is torsion-free. Since
\Omega_{\mathrm{tor}}=\ker(\Omega\to\omega)
(see the second paragraph of
[
LS00
, §1]
),
\mathcal{F}
is a subsheaf of
\omega
, so that it suffices to observe that
\mathrm{H}^{0}(\omega)
is torsion-free: This follows easily from the facts that the formation of the relative dualizing sheaf commutes with arbitrary base change
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0E6R
]
, and that for any point
s\in C
,
\dim\mathrm{H}^{0}(\omega|_{X_{s}})
is the arithmetic genus of
X_{s}

[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BS2(5)
]
, which is constant by flatness.
∎

Theorem 2.22
.

If
h>0
and the
j
-invariant map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is finite and separable, then the following holds:

(a)

The Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence of
X
degenerates at
E_{1}
-page.

(b)

The Hodge diamond of
X
is independent of
k
:
h^{0,1}=h^{1,0}=h^{1,2}=h^{2,1}=g
,
h^{0,2}=h^{2,0}=h-1+g
, and
h^{1,1}=10h+2g
(cf. the case over
\mathbb{C}

[
Mir89
, Lem. IV.1.1]
).

(c)

If
\mathrm{char\,}k=p>0
, then
\mathrm{H}^{k}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W(k))
is torsion-free for every
k
.

Proof.

First of all, we have
h^{0,1}=g(C)
.
Indeed, the Leray spectral sequence gives us an exact sequence

0\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(C,\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\to\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\to 0.

As
h>0
(so that
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=L^{\vee}
has no global sections), and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\,{\cong}\,\mathcal{O}_{C}
, we have
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\,{\cong}\,\mathrm{H}^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})
.
The equality
h^{1,0}=g(C)
follows from Prop.
2.21
.

By the universal coefficient theorem, for any
i
we have

0\to\mathrm{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W){\otimes}k\to\mathrm{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)\to\mathrm{Tor}_{1}^{W}(\mathrm{H}^{i+1}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W),k)\to 0.

And by the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence we always have
h_{\mathrm{dR}}^{i}\leq\sum_{j}h^{j,i-j}
, with equality taken only when all arrows in or out of the terms
\mathrm{H}^{i-j}(\Omega_{X}^{i})
on the
E_{1}
-page vanish.

Recall that
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
is always torsion-free. In fact, this is true for any smooth proper variety over
k
, as the Albanese morphism
X\to\mathrm{Alb}(X)
induces an isomorphism between their
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-/W)
(cf.
[
Ill79
, II, Remarque 3.11.2]
), and
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{crys}}
of an abelian variety is always torsion-free.
By
[
Lie09
, Prop. 2.1(1)]
, the Picard scheme of
X
is smooth, so we have
\mathrm{rank\,}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)=2h^{0,1}
. This forces
h^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}=h^{0,1}+h^{1,0}
, and hence
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
is torsion-free. To see that
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
is torsion-free, we first note that by Poincaré duality
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
and
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
have the same rank, and by Serre duality we have
h^{2,1}=h^{0,1}
and
h^{1,2}=h^{1,0}
. Then we have

2h^{0,1}=\mathrm{rank\,}\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)\leq h^{3}_{\mathrm{dR}}\leq h^{2,1}+h^{1,2}.

Hence the equality must be taken, which forces
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W)
to be torsion-free.
In particular, by the universal coefficient theorem, this forces the natural map
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(X/W){\otimes}k\to\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)
to be an isomorphism.

At this point we can easily tell the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence
\{E_{n}\}
degenerates at
E_{1}
-page.
Note that we have argued that
h^{k}_{\mathrm{dR}}=\sum_{i+j=k}h^{i,j}
for
k\neq 1
, and hence the terms
\mathrm{H}^{j}(\Omega_{X}^{i})=E_{\infty}^{i,j}
for
i+j\neq 1
.
This forces the differentials involving the
E_{n}^{i,j}
terms with
n\geq 1
and
i+j=1
to vanish.
That
h^{0,2}=h-1+g
now follows from the definition of
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and the isomorphism
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})
, and
h^{2,0}=h^{0,2}
follows by Serre duality.
The only undetermined Hodge number is
h^{1,1}
.
However, the crystalline Euler number (defined as the alternating sum of ranks) is the same as the
\ell
-adic Euler number, which is
12h
.
Hence
h^{1,1}
is
10h+2g
.
∎

2.7.
Inseparable
j
-invariant maps

We analyze the
j
-invariant map of an elliptic surface in terms of its Weierstrass normal form, assuming that the base field is algebraically closed and has characteristic
p\geq 5
.
We follow the notations as in last subsection.
Recall that the Weierstrass normal form of
X
is given by the equation (cf.
[
Kas77
, Thm. 1]
)

(8)

Y^{2}Z=X^{3}-a_{4}XZ^{2}-a_{6}Z^{3},

where
[X:Y:Z]
is the relative coordinate of the
\mathbb{P}^{2}
-bundle
\mathbb{P}(L^{2}\oplus L^{3}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{C})
over the curve
C
, and
a_{4},a_{6}
are elements of
\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{4}),\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{6})
respectively.
In terms of the elements
a_{4}
and
a_{6}
, the
j
-invariant of the generic fiber of
\pi:X\to C
, as an element in the fraction field
k(C)
, is given by
1728\cdot(4a_{4}^{3}/\Delta)
, where
\Delta=4a_{4}^{3}-27a_{6}^{2}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{12})
is the discriminant.
Note that in order for
\pi:X\to C
to have a smooth generic fiber,
\Delta
does not vanish identically on
C
; in particular, the element
a_{4}\neq 0\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{4})
.
Notice that being an element of
k(C)
, the
j
-invariant can be simultaneously viewed as a map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which we referred to as the
j
-invariant map
.
Denote the degree of this map by
\mathrm{deg}j
.

For
Proposition

2.23
and
Corollary

2.24
below, assume that
p\geq 5
, and
g=h=1
, where
g
is the geometric genus and
h
is the degree of the fundamental line bundle
L
.
Let
e\in C
be the unique point such that
L\simeq\mathcal{O}_{C}(e)
.
We shall view
C
as an elliptic curve with origin at
e
.
Below for any nonzero global section of a line bundle on
C
, we view its divisor simultaneously as a multiset of closed points on
C
.
Here, to avoid confusing the addition of points on the elliptic curve
C
with the addition of divisors, we denote the former by the symbol
\oplus
and the latter by the symbol
+
.
Note that a line bundle on
C
of degree
0
admits a nonzero global section if and only if it is trivial (i.e.,
\simeq\mathcal{O}_{C}
).
Therefore, if
s\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{r})
is any nonzero section, then
s
can be viewed as as unique (up to scalar) section in
\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{r}(-\mathrm{div}(s)))
, and we have the following induced formula for the summation of the points under the group law of the elliptic curve
C
:

(9)

\oplus_{x\in\mathrm{supp\,}(\mathrm{div}(s))}m_{x}(\mathrm{div}(s))\cdot x=e.

Here for a multiset
A
in
C
, we use
m_{x}(A)
to denote the multiplicity of a given point
x\in\mathrm{supp\,}(A)
, and in particular we have
\mathrm{val}_{x}(s)=m_{x}(\mathrm{div}(s))
.

Proposition 2.23
.

If the
j
-invariant map is nonconstant and inseparable, then
p=5
or
7
. When
p=5
, there are two possibilities:

(i)

\mathrm{deg}j=10
, and there are points
y,z\neq x
in
C
, such that
\mathrm{div}(a_{4})=\{4x\}
,
\mathrm{val}_{x}(a_{6})=1
, and
\mathrm{div}(\Delta)=\{2x,5y,5z\}
.

(ii)

\mathrm{deg}j=5
, and there are points
x\neq y
in
C
, such that
\mathrm{div}(a_{4})=\{3x,y\}
, and
\mathrm{div}(a_{6})=\{2x,4y\}
.

When
p=7
, there is one possibility:

(iii)

\mathrm{deg}j=7
, and there are points
x\neq y
in
C
, such that
\mathrm{div}(a_{4})=\{3x,y\}
, and
\mathrm{div}(a_{6})=\{x,5y\}
.

Proof.

Note that the multiset of zeroes of
j
, which is a meromorphic function on
C
, is given by
3\mathrm{div}(a_{4})\smallsetminus 2\mathrm{div}(a_{6})
.
Therefore, the order of this multiset is
\mathrm{deg}j
.
It is easy to see that under the assumption, we have
\mathrm{deg}j\leq 12
, so
p=5,7
or
11
.

For simplicity, let us write
A,B
for the multisets
\mathrm{div}(a_{4})
and
\mathrm{div}(a_{6})
respectively.
In order for the
j
-invariant map to be inseparable, the following conditions needs to be satisfied:

(P)

The multiplicity of
j
(as a meromorphic function) at any point on
C
is divisible by
p
.

(P’)

p\mid m_{x}(3A\smallsetminus 2B)
for any
x\in 3A\smallsetminus 2B
.

(P”)

p\mid\mathrm{deg}j=\#(3A\smallsetminus 2B)=12-\#(3A\cap 2B)
.

Clearly, we have the implications
P\Rightarrow P^{\prime}\Rightarrow P^{\prime\prime}
.
The case
p=11
cannot happen, because then by (P’) we must have
3A\smallsetminus 2B=\{11x\}
, but either
x\in B
or
x\not\in B
would lead to a contradiction.
Hence we are left with three possibilities: (i)
p=5
and
\mathrm{deg}j=10
, (ii)
p=\mathrm{deg}j=5
and (iii)
p=\mathrm{deg}j=7
.

(i) As
\mathrm{deg}j=10
,
\#(3A\cap 2B)=2
.
Hence we must have
3A\cap 2B=\{2x\}
for some
x\in B
.
By (P) we know that
(3A\smallsetminus 2B)=\{5x^{\prime},5x^{\prime\prime}\}
for some
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in C
. It is clear then that
A=\{2x^{\prime},2x^{\prime\prime}\}
. If
x^{\prime}\neq x^{\prime\prime}
, then
3A\smallsetminus 2B=\{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\}
cannot be of the form
\{2x\}
. Hence we must have that
A=\{4x\}
. As
2B
cancels out exactly two copies of
x
,
x
has multiplicity exactly
1
in
B
. The statement that
\mathrm{div}(\Delta)=2x+5y+5z
for some
y,z\neq x
then follows by applying (P) to the poles of
j
.

(ii) By (P’) we must have
3A\smallsetminus 2B=\{5x\}
for some
x
. The only integer solution to the equation
3m_{x}(A)-2m_{x}(B)=5
in the range
1\leq m_{x}(A)\leq 4
is
(m_{x}(A),m_{x}(B))=(3,2)
.
Therefore,
A=\{3x,y\}
for some
y\neq x
. As
y\not\in 3A\smallsetminus 2B
, we must have
m_{y}(B)\geq 2
.
A quick inspection of Kodaira’s table
[
Mir89
, p.41, IV.3.1]
9
9

9

It is well known that Kodaira’s classification works verbatim in characteristic
p\geq 5
as the case over
\mathbb{C}
.
The
J
used in
loc. cit.
is the reduced
j
-invariant, i.e.,
j/1728=4a_{4}^{3}/\Delta.
.
tells us that the Weirstrass normal form of
X
has to have a type III fiber at
y
.
But then the multiplicity of
j
at
y
is
2m_{y}(B)-3
is divisible by
5
, which forces
m_{y}(B)=4
.

(iii) The analysis is similar to that of (ii), so we omit the details.
∎

Corollary 2.24
.

View the vector space
\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{4})\times\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{6})
as the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{4}\times\mathbb{A}^{6}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{10}
over
k
. The set
I
of
k
-points
(a_{4},a_{6})
such that the corresponding elliptic surface
X
has finite inseparable
j
-invariant map is contained in a subscheme of dimension at most
2
.

Proof.

Let
\{\Delta=0\}
be the subscheme of
\mathbb{A}^{12}
such that
\Delta(a_{4},a_{6})
vanishes identically, and set
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{A}^{12}\smallsetminus\{\Delta=0\}
.
For every
r\in\mathbb{N}
, let
C^{[r]}
denote the Hilbert scheme of
r
-points on
C
, which is well known to be the same as the
r
th symmetric power as
C
is a smooth curve.
Then the association
(a_{4},a_{6})\mapsto\mathrm{div}(\Delta)
defines a morphism
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\to C^{[12]}
.
Note that a point on
C^{[r]}
can also be viewed as a degree
r
effective divisor.

Let us start with case (i) in
Proposition

2.23
. As
a_{4}\neq 0\in L^{4}(-4x)
,
x
is a
4
-torsion point under the group law by (
9
). Hence there are at most
16
different choices for
x
. Let us fix one. Since
h^{0}(L(-4x))=1
, there is a line in
\mathbb{A}^{4}
parametrizing those
a_{4}
such that
\mathrm{div}(a_{4})=4x
.
Let
\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}
be the fiber of
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
over this line.
Let
C^{\prime}\in C^{[2]}
be the subscheme parametrizing those pairs
y+z
such that
2x\oplus 5y\oplus 5z=e
.
Let
Z\subseteq C^{[12]}
be the image of the morphism
C^{\prime}\to C^{[12]}
given by
y+z\mapsto 2x+5y+5z
.
Since two sections
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{12})
define the same divisor if and only if
s\in k^{\times}s^{\prime}
, every fiber of the morphism
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\to C^{[12]}
(and hence the composition
\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\to C^{[12]}
) has dimension at most
1
.
Now,
I
is contained the set of
k
-points of
\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\times_{C^{[12]}}C^{\prime}
.
As
\dim C^{\prime}=1
,
\dim\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\times_{C^{[12]}}C^{\prime}\leq 1+1=2
.

Now consider case (ii). The equations
3x\oplus y=e
and
2x\oplus 4y=e
imply that
x
is a
10
-torsion point. Hence there are finitely many of them. Fixing each
x
, each
a_{4}
and
a_{6}
are well defined up to scalars, so we obtain the dimension bound. Case (iii) is entirely similar.
∎

2.8.
Ramified covers of curves over discrete valuation rings

We start by recalling the relative Abhyankar’s lemma from
[
Gro71
]
.

Proposition 2.25
.

[
Gro71
, Exposé XIII, Prop. 5.5]

Let
X\to S
be a smooth map of schemes, and let
D
be a reduced relative normal crossing divisor relative to
S
.
Assume
U^{\prime}\to U=X\smallsetminus D
is a finite étale cover such that its fiber over each geometric generic point of
S
is tamely ramified along
D
.
Then étale locally around any point of
U
, the map
U^{\prime}\to U
can be extended to a finite disjoint union of standard tamely ramified covers of
X
.
In particular,
U^{\prime}\to U
is tamely ramified (along
D
) relative to
S
.

Here we recall from
[
Gro71
, Exposé XIII, 5.3.0]
(see also
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0EYF
]
) that a standard tamely ramified cover of
X=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
that is ramified at
D=\mathrm{Spec}(R/f))
is of the form
\mathrm{Spec}(R[t]/t^{n}-f)
, where the integer
n
is invertible in
R
.
In particular, the above result is stable under any base change of
S
.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
g:C^{\prime}\to C
be a finite flat morphism between smooth proper curves over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that the special fiber
C^{\prime}_{k}\to C_{k}
is separable.
Suppose that
g
is étale over
\mathcal{C}\smallsetminus D
for a relative Cartier divisor
D
that is étale over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Then the reduced subscheme of
g^{-1}(D)
is also étale over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Proof.

As the claim is étale local with respect to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we replace
\mathcal{O}_{K}
by its maximal unramified extension and assume that
D
is a disjoint union of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-points
D_{1},\cdots,D_{n}
.
Suppose that the reduction
g_{k}^{-1}(D_{i})_{k}
is supported on
k
-points
P_{i,1},\cdots,P_{i,n_{i}}
and has multiplicity
m_{i,j}
at
P_{i,j}
.
Then by the separatedness of the map
g_{k}
and Riemann–Hurwitz formula (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0C1F
]
), we have

(10)

\chi(C^{\prime}_{k})-(\mathrm{deg}\,g)\cdot\chi(C_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(m_{i,j}-1).

Here we implicitly use the fact that the map
g_{k}
is tamely ramified over each
D_{k}
, thanks to the relative Abhyankar’s Lemma
Proposition

2.25
.

On the other hand, for each
i,j
, we set
\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}
to be the set of closed points
Q
on
C^{\prime}_{K}
such that
Q
specializes to
P_{i,j}
.
Let
m_{Q}
be the multiplicity of the map
g_{K}
at
Q
, and let
e_{Q}
be the ramification index of the field extension
K(Q)/K
(which by assumption is also the degree of
K(Q)/K
).
Then by Riemann–Hurwitz formula again, we get

(11)

\chi(C^{\prime}_{K})-(\mathrm{deg}\,g)\cdot\chi(C_{K})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}(m_{Q}-1)\cdot e_{Q}.

Notice that by the flatness of the map
g
and the flat base change theorem of the coherent cohomology (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 02KH
]
), the left hand side of (
10
) is equal to that of (
11
).
Hence we get the equality

(12)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(m_{i,j}-1)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}(m_{Q}-1)\cdot e_{Q}.

Moreover, by the flatness of the map
g
, the preimage
g^{-1}(D)=D\times_{C}C^{\prime}
is finite flat over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
In particular, we get

(13)

\dim_{k}g^{-1}(D)_{k}=\dim_{K}g^{-1}(D)_{K}.

Here we note that by summing over the connected components of
g^{-1}(D)_{K}
and
g^{-1}(D)_{k}
individually, we have

(14)

\displaystyle\dim_{k}g^{-1}(D)_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}m_{i,j};

(15)

\displaystyle\dim_{K}g^{-1}(D)_{K}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}m_{Q}\cdot e_{Q}.

Hence we get another equality on the multiplicities and the ramification indexes

(16)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}m_{i,j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}m_{Q}\cdot e_{Q}.

Now, by substituting (
16
) into (
12
) and cancel the terms with multiplication indexes, and by the positivity of each
e_{Q}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}e_{Q}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}}1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}|Q_{i,j}|\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i},

where the last equality follows from the surjectivity of the specialization map.
Hence each set
Q_{i,j}
has only one point (which we shall also denote by
Q_{i,j}
), and the residue field extension
K(Q_{i,j})/K
is trivial and hence unramified.
Thus the claim follows from
Lemma

2.27
below.
∎

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
D=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
be a connected finite flat
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-scheme.
Assume
(R[1/p])_{\mathrm{red}}
is equal to
\mathrm{Spec}(K)
.
Then
R_{\mathrm{red}}
is isomorphic to (and hence étale over)
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Here we note that by the flatness of
R
over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
Lemma

2.27
in particular implies that in the setting of
Proposition

2.26
, the multiplicity of
P_{i,j}\in C_{k}^{\prime}
coincides with the multiplicity of
Q_{i,j}\in C_{K}^{\prime}
, i.e.
m_{i,j}=m_{Q_{i,j}}
.

Proof.

We let
\overline{R}
be the image of the composition
R\to R[1/p]\to(R[1/p])_{\mathrm{red}}=K
, which is a finite
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-subalgebra in
K
.
If
\overline{R}\neq\mathcal{O}_{K}
, the
p
-adic valuation of any element
x\in\overline{R}\smallsetminus\mathcal{O}_{K}
is negative.
However, the
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-submodule
\mathcal{O}_{K}[x]
is not finitely generated over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, contradicting to the assumption that
\overline{R}
is finite over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Hence
\overline{R}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Finally, since
\ker(R\to\overline{R})
is contained in
\ker(R[1/p]\to(R[1/p])_{\mathrm{red}}=K)
, we see
\ker(R\to\overline{R})
is nilpotent, and hence
R_{\mathrm{red}}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
∎

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and let
D\subseteq X
be a relative Cartier divisor whose special fiber
D_{k}
is generically reduced on each connected component.
Let
C\subseteq X
be a closed subscheme which is smooth and proper of relative dimension one over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, such that
C\cap D
is étale over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Assume
X_{1}^{\circ}
(resp.
C_{1}^{\circ}
) is a connected finite étale cover of
X^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X\smallsetminus D
(resp.
C^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C\smallsetminus C\cap D
).
Then the closed immersion
C^{\circ}\hookrightarrow X^{\circ}
lifts to a map
C_{1}^{\circ}\to X_{1}^{\circ}
if and only if this is true on the special fiber.

In fact, we show below that the reduction map induces a natural bijection between the lifts
C_{1}^{\circ}\to X_{1}^{\circ}
and the lifts
C_{1,k}^{\circ}\to X_{1,k}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

By assumption, we have the following diagram of schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\circ}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where the both vertical arrows are finite étale covers.
In particular, the fiber product
C_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C^{\circ}\times_{X^{\circ}}X_{1}^{\circ}
is a finite étale cover of
C^{\circ}
.
So to show the statement, it suffices to show that the reduction map induces an isomorphism of hom groups, namely

\mathrm{Hom}_{C^{\circ}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}}(C_{1}^{\circ},C_{2})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{C^{\circ}_{k,\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}}(C_{1,k}^{\circ},C_{2,k}^{\circ}),

where
(-)_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}
is the étale site of the given scheme.

Moreover, we notice that since the complement divisor
D=C\smallsetminus C^{\circ}
is étale over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
by assumption, it is in particular a reduced and normal crossing divisor in
C
that is relative to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
So by the relative Abhyankar’s lemma in
Proposition

2.25
, we see both of the étale covers
C_{i}^{\circ}
for
i=1,2
and their reductions are tamely ramified along the complements.
To proceed, we apply
[
Gro71
, Exposé XIII, Cor. 2.8]
at the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K}),}

where the
1
-acyclicity of the map
C\to\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
with respect to the set
\mathbb{L}=\{\text{prime numbers\,}\ell~|~\ell\neq p\}
follows from
[
AGV71
, Exposé XV, Thm. 2.1]
.
The loc. cit. implies that for each finite group
G
of order prime to
p
, the reduction map induces a bijection of groups

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{t}}(C^{\circ},G)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{t}}(C^{\circ}_{k},G),

where
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{t}}(-,G)
is the equivalent classes of
G
-torsors that are tamely ramified along the normal crossing complement above.
In particular, we obtain the isomorphism of tame fundamental groups

10
10

10

The original definition of the tame fundamental group in
[
Gro71
, Exporé XV, 2.1.3]
is for the prescribed compactification with normal crossing divisors.
However, it is shown in
[
KS10
, Thm. 1.1]
the notion is independent of such choices when the compactifications are regular and proper with normal crossing divisors.

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{t}}(C^{\circ},\overline{x})\simeq\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{t}}(C^{\circ}_{k},\overline{x}_{k}),

where
\overline{x}
is a geometric point of
C^{\circ}
.
We let
(-)_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}^{\mathrm{t}}
be the full subcategory of the étale site
(-)_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}
consists of the étale objects that are tame along the normal crossing complement.
Then by the general result on the Galois category
[
Gro71
, Exporé V, Prop. 6.9]
, the isomorphism above implies that the reduction map induces a fully faithful functor of Galois categories

(C^{\circ})_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}^{\mathrm{t}}\longrightarrow(C^{\circ}_{k})_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}^{\mathrm{t}},

which concludes the proof.
∎

3.
Period morphisms

In this section, we discuss the period morphism for a
smooth
family of projective varieties with
h^{2,0}=1
.
The key property of the period morphism is that there are matching isomorphisms between the pullback of the automorphic sheaves on Shimura varieties and the (primitive) cohomology sheaves of the varieties in question.
For Betti and
\ell
-adic cohomology, this has been done in
[
HYZ25
]
by extending the construction in
[
Mad15
]
using the inputs from
[
Moo17
]
.
Here we fill in the treatment of crystalline and de Rham cohomology.

The main difficulty to overcome is that we cannot assume that the motives of the varieties in question are abelian, so that the matching isomorphism of de Rham cohomology over
\mathbb{C}
does not automatically descent over
\mathbb{Q}
as in
[
Mad15
, Prop. 5.6(2)]
; moreover, its compatibility with the
p
-adic matching isomorphism does not follow from Blasius’ result
[
Bla94
]
.
Instead, we shall achieve this by making use of the
p
-adic Riemann-Hilbert functor in
[
DLLZ23
]
and the analytic density of Noether–Lefschetz loci (cf. proof of
Lemma

3.9
) to conclude that there is a natural descent over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, which is enough for our applications.

3.1.
General set-up

Let
p>2
be a prime and
\mathcal{S}
be a smooth connected scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
of finite type with generic point
\eta
and
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Q}}
be its generic fiber.
Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{S}
be a smooth projective morphism with geometrically connected fibers of relative dimension
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\dim\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{S}\geq 2
.
Let
\Theta
be a relatively ample line bundle on
\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{S}
and
\Lambda\subseteq\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})_{\mathrm{tf}}
be a sublattice which contains the class of
\Theta_{\eta}
.
Here we are taking an ad hoc definition for the Néron–Severi group when the base field is not alegebraically closed:
\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{im}(\mathrm{Pic}\,(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})\to\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}}))
.
Note that
\Theta
defines symmetric bilinear pairings on
(R^{2}f_{\mathbb{C}*}\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{\mathrm{tf}}
over
S_{\mathbb{C}}
,
R^{2}f_{\mathbb{Q},\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
over
\mathcal{S}
,
(R^{2}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\mathrm{tf}}
over
S
, and
R^{2}f_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{S}}
individually, via the formula
\langle x,y\rangle=x\cup y\cup c_{1}(\Theta)^{d-2}
, as each element in
\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})_{\mathrm{tf}}
gives rise to a global section
c_{1}(\Theta)
of these sheaves.
Let
\mathbf{P}_{B},\mathbf{P}_{\ell},\mathbf{P}_{p}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}
be the orthogonal complement of
\Lambda
in these sheaves. Over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we similarly define the F-isocrystal
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]
on
\mathrm{crys}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. When the second crystalline cohomology of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is locally free (e.g., when
Lemma

2.3
is applicable), we also define
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}
. For each fiber, write
\mathrm{PNS}(-)
for the orthogonal complement of
\Lambda
in
\mathrm{NS}(-)
.

Definition 3.1
.

(Algebraic Monodromy)
Let
T
be a normal integral notherian scheme and
\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
be an étale
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-local system on
T
(
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{T}^{\times}
). For any geometric point
t\to T
, we define the
algebraic monodromy group

\mathrm{Mon}(\mathsf{W}_{\ell},t)
to be the Zariski closure of the image of the monodromy representation
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(T,t)\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathsf{W}_{\ell,t})
. Denote by
\mathrm{Mon}^{\circ}(\mathsf{W}_{\ell},t)
its identity component.

Assumption 3.2
.

We shall consider families
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{S}
under the following conditions:

(i)

For some (and hence every)
s\in S(\mathbb{C})
,
h^{2,0}(\mathcal{X}_{s})=1
.

(ii)

For some
s\in S(\mathbb{C})
, the Kodaira–Spencer map

\mathrm{KS}_{s}:T_{s}S_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}),\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}))

is nonzero.

(iii)

For some (and hence every) geometric point
s\to\mathcal{S}
,
\mathbf{P}_{p,s}=\mathrm{PH}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{s},\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{\mathrm{tf}}
is self-dual.

(iv)

For some (and hence every) geometric point
s\to\mathcal{S}
,
\mathrm{Mon}(\mathbf{P}_{2},s)
is connected.

Additionally, assume one of the following:

(v)

For some
s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C})
, the map
\mathrm{KS}_{s}
in (ii) has rank
\geq 2
.

(vi)

The motive
\mathfrak{h}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{s})\in\mathsf{Mot}_{\mathrm{AH}}(\mathbb{C})
determined by
\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{s},\mathbb{Q})
is abelian for every
s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C})
, where
\mathsf{Mot}_{\mathrm{AH}}(\mathbb{C})
is the category of motives with absolute Hodge cycles over
\mathbb{C}
(cf.
[
Pan94
, §2]
11
11

11

Here we are following the notations of
[
Mad15
, §2]
. The notation
\mathfrak{h}^{i}(X)
means the submotive of
\mathfrak{h}(X)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(X,0,\mathrm{id})
whose cohomological realizations give
\mathrm{H}^{2}
.
).

Construction 3.3
.

Choose a base point
b\in S(\mathbb{C})
which lies above the generic point
\eta
of
\mathcal{S}
(or
S
).
Let
S^{\dagger}
be the connected component of
S_{\mathbb{C}}
which contains
b
.
Let
L
be a quadratic form over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
such that
L\,{\cong}\,\mathrm{PH}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{b},\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})_{\mathrm{tf}}
and fix such an isomorphism. Let
V\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
G
(resp.
\widetilde{G}
) be the reductive
\mathbb{Q}
-group
\mathrm{SO}(V)
(resp.
\mathrm{CSpin}(V)
).
By a slight abuse of notation we also write
G
(resp.
\widetilde{G}
) for its
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-model
\mathrm{SO}(L)
(resp.
\mathrm{CSpin}(L)
).
Denote by
\Omega
the Hermitian symmetric space
\{\omega\in\mathbb{P}(V{\otimes}\mathbb{C})\mid\langle\omega,\bar{\omega}\rangle>0\}
.
Let
\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
Let
\mathsf{K}^{p}
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{p}
) be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
(resp.
\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
) such that
\mathsf{K}^{p}
contains the image of
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{p}
.
Set
\mathsf{K}:=\mathsf{K}_{p}\mathsf{K}^{p}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}:=\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}_{p}\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{p}
.

We briefly recall some basic facts and commonly used notations of Shimura varieties and various sheaves:
The reflex field of
(\widetilde{G},\Omega)
is
\mathbb{Q}
and by
[
Kis10
]
there is an canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
There is a suitable sympletic space
(H,\psi)
and a Siegel half space
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
such that there is an embedding of Shimura data
(\widetilde{G},\Omega)\hookrightarrow(\mathrm{GSp}(\psi),\mathcal{H}^{\pm})
which eventually equips
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
with a universal abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
.
12
12

12

Technically, in
[
Kis10
]
and
[
Mad16
]
,
\mathcal{A}
is only defined as a sheaf of abelian schemes up to prime-to-
p
quasi-isogeny.
However, for
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{p}
sufficiently small, we can take
\mathcal{A}
to be an actual abelian scheme (cf.
[
Kis10
, (2.1.5)]
).

Let
a:\mathcal{A}\to\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
be the structural morphism.
Define the sheaves
\mathbf{H}_{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R^{1}a_{\mathbb{C}*}\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
,
\mathbf{H}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R^{1}a_{*}\underline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
(
\ell\neq p
),
\mathbf{H}_{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R^{1}a_{\mathbb{Q}*}\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
,
\textbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R^{1}a_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{crys}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}R^{1}\bar{a}_{\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
(where
\bar{a}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a{\otimes}\mathbb{F}_{p}
).
The abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
is equipped with a “CSpin-structure”: a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading,
\mathrm{Cl}(L)
-action and an idempotent projector
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{?}:\mathrm{End}(\mathbf{H}_{?})\to\mathrm{End}(\mathbf{H}_{?})
for
?\in\{B,\ell,p,\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{crys}\}
on (various applicable fibers of)
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
.
We use
\mathbf{L}_{?}
to denote the images of
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{?}
.
Given any
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
-scheme
T
, an endomorphism
f\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
is called a
special endomorphism
(
[
Mad16
, Def. 5.2, see also Lem. 5.4, Cor. 5.22]
) if for some (and hence all)
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{T}^{\times}
, the
\ell
-adic realization of
f
lies in
\mathbf{L}_{\ell}|_{T}\subseteq\mathrm{End}(\mathbf{H}_{\ell}|_{T})
; if
\mathcal{O}_{T}=k
for a perfect field
k
in characteristic
p
, then equivalently
f
is called a
special endomorphism
if the crystalline realization of
f
lies in
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys},T}
. We write the submodule of
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
consisting of special endomorphisms as
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
.
The sheaves
\mathbf{L}_{?}
and special endomorphisms on
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
descend to its étale quotient
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)
, and the descents are customarily denoted by the same letters.

Below we assume that
\mathsf{K}^{p}
is of the form
\prod_{\ell\neq p}\mathsf{K}_{\ell}
for
\mathsf{K}_{\ell}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
and is contained in the stabilizer of the lattice
\mathrm{PH}^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{b},\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{p})_{\mathrm{tf}}\subseteq V{\otimes}\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f}
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Under Assumption
3.2
, up to replacing
\mathcal{S}
by a connected finite étale cover, there exists a period morphism
\rho:\mathcal{S}\to\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)
with the following properties:

(a)

There exists an isometry of
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-VHS
(\alpha^{\dagger}_{B},\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}):\rho_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{B},\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})|_{S^{\dagger}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\mathbf{P}_{B},\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
over
S^{\dagger}
.
13
13

13

In
[
HYZ25
]
we used
S^{\circ}
instead of
S^{\dagger}
to denote the connected component of
S_{\mathbb{C}}
that contains the point
b
.
Here we changed it to avoid a clash of notations with
Section

4
.

(b)

For every prime
\ell\neq p
, there exists an isometry of
\ell
-adic local systems
\alpha_{\ell}:\rho^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell}
over
\mathcal{S}
whose restriction to
S^{\dagger}
agrees with
\alpha^{\dagger}_{B}{\otimes}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
.

(c)

There exists an isometry of
p
-adic local systems
\alpha_{p}:\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}^{*}\mathbf{L}_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{p}
over
S
whose restriction to
S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}
agrees with
\alpha_{B}^{\dagger}{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proof.

This can be extracted from the proof of
[
HYZ25
, Prop. 5.3.3(a)]
. We give a sketch of the arguments for readers’ convenience. By an
\ell
-independence of the group of connected components of algebraic monodromy groups
[
LP92
, Prop. 6.14]
, the assumption (iv) is used to make sure that
\mathrm{Mon}(\mathbf{P}_{\ell},s)
for every prime
\ell\neq p
. Therefore, up to replacing
\mathcal{S}
be a
finite
étale cover, the image of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(S,b)
in
G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
is contained in
\mathsf{K}^{p}
. The VHS
(\mathbf{P}_{B},\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})
on
S^{\dagger}
, together with suitable level structures, defines a morphism
\rho^{\dagger}:S^{\dagger}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
F
be the field of definition of
S^{\dagger}
. Then
[
HYZ25
, Thm. 4.1.9]
implies that
\rho^{\dagger}
descends to
F
, so that by applying the forgetful functor from the category of
F
-schemes to that of
\mathbb{Q}
-schemes, we obtain a
\mathbb{Q}
-morphism
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}:S\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)
. Moreover, we obtain isomorphisms
\alpha_{\ell}:\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{S}
and
\alpha_{p}:\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}^{*}\mathbf{L}_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{p}
over
S
which are compatible with
\alpha_{B}^{\dagger}
over
S^{\dagger}
. Our assumption (vi) corresponds to assumption (a) in
loc. cit.
, and our assumption (v) ensures that, if (a) in
loc. cit.
is not satisfied, then (b) is, thanks to
[
HYZ25
, Lem. 2.2.5]
. The condition denoted by “
\#
” in
[
HYZ25
, (4.1.6)]
is automatically satisfied when
\mathsf{K}
is small enough. The fact that
\rho_{\mathbb{Q}}
extends (necessarily unique) to a
\rho
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
is a consequence of the extension property of the canonical model (
[
Kis10
]
, cf.
[
HYZ25
, Thm. 3.4.4]
). Then
\alpha_{\ell}
extends over
\mathcal{S}
as the natural map
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(S,b)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{S},b)
is surjective.
∎

3.2.
Recollections of the
p
-adic Riemann-Hilbert functor

We briefly recall the
p
-adic Riemann-Hilbert functor. Let
K
be a
p
-adic field with perfect residue field and fix an algebraic closure
\bar{K}
. Let
T
be a smooth rigid analytic variety over
K
. Let
\mathsf{Loc}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(T)
denote the category of (lisse)
p
-adic étale local systems.
In
[
LZ17
, Thm. 3.9]
, Liu and Zhu defined a functor
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
which sends a
\mathbb{L}\in\mathsf{Loc}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(T)
to a vector bundle
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{L})
with integrable connection
\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}
and filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}
on
T
,
satisfying the following properties.

•

The formation of
(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{L}),\nabla_{\mathbb{L}})
commutes with base change. That is, if
f:T^{\prime}\to T
is a morphism between smooth rigid-analytic varieties over
K
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

(17)

f^{*}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{L}),\nabla_{\mathbb{L}})\simeq(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(f^{*}\mathbb{L}),\nabla_{f^{*}\mathbb{L}}).

•

The filtration satisfies Griffiths transversality.

•

The restriction of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
onto the full subcategory of
de Rham
local systems (in the sense of Scholze
[
Sch13
, Def. 8.3]
) is a tensor functor and preserves the rank.

•

When
T=\mathrm{Spa}(K)
is just a point, the functor
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
recovers the classical Fontaine’s
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
functor (which we denote by the same symbol), i.e.
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{L})=\bigl(\mathbb{L}(\mathrm{Spa}(\mathbb{C}_{p})){\otimes}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{Gal}_{K}}
.

One can of course apply
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-local systems after inverting
p
.
We recall (a special case of) Scholze’s result
[
Sch13
, Thm. 8.8]
in the following form:

Theorem 3.5
.

(Scholze)

Let
f:X\to T
be a smooth proper morphism between smooth rigid-analytic varieties over
K
. Then (the torsion-free part of)
R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is de Rham and there is a functorial isomorphism bewteen filtered flat vector bundles

(18)

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq R^{i}f_{*}\Omega_{X/T}^{\bullet}.

Proof.

By
[
Sch13
, Thm. 8.8]
and its proof, there is a natural filtered isomorphism of pro-étale sheaves

R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p}{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR},T}\simeq R^{i}f_{*}\Omega_{X/T}^{\bullet}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR},T}.

Recall that on
T
,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-)=\nu_{*}(-{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR},T})
, where
\nu
is the projection of topoi
T_{\textrm{pro{\'{e}t}}}\to T_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}
. By the projection formula of finite projective modules, and the fact that
\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR},T}=\mathcal{O}_{T}
(
[
Sch13
, Cor. 6.19]
), we obtain (
18
) by applying
\nu_{*}
to the above isomorphism.
∎

Now suppose that
T
is a smooth algebraic variety over
K
and let
T^{\mathrm{an}}
be its analytification. Then there is an algebraic de Rham functor
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{\mathrm{dR}}
which sends de Rham
p
-adic local systems on
X
to a filtered flat vector bundle over
X
, whose analytification is identified with
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{L})
(
[
DLLZ23
, Thm 1.1]
). Using this functor, we obtain an algebraic version of (
18
).

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
f:X\to T
be a smooth proper morphism between smooth
K
-varieties. Then (the torsion-free part of)
R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is de Rham and there is a functorial isomorphism
\mathsf{c}_{\mathrm{dR}}:\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq R^{i}f_{*}\Omega_{X/T}^{\bullet}
bewteen filtered flat vector bundles over
\mathcal{O}_{T}
whose analytification is given by (
18
).

Proof.

It suffices to produce an isomorphism between the underlying flat vector bundles, as the property of respecting the filtration can be checked after analytification.
As explained in the proof of
[
DLLZ23
, Lem. 4.1.2]
, by several results of André and Baldassarri, the analytification functor from the category of algebraic regular connections on
T
whose exponents contain no Liouville numbers to the category of analytic ones on
T^{\mathrm{an}}
is fully faithful. Therefore, it suffices to show that both
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(R^{i}f_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}*}\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
R^{i}f_{*}\Omega_{X/T}^{\bullet}
are regular and have non-Liouville exponents.
For the former, this has been explained in
loc. cit.
that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbb{L})
is regular and has rational exponents for any local system
\mathbb{L}
.
For the latter, the same is true by the local monodromy theorem (e.g., see
[
Kat70
, §13]
).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

In the above theorem, if
L
is a relative line bundle on
X/T
, then
\mathsf{c}_{\mathrm{dR}}
sends the
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
of its
p
-adic Chern class to its de Rham Chern class.
Indeed, by compatibility of Chern classes with base change it suffices
to observe this when
T
is a point.
For a reference in the latter case, see
[
IIK21
, Prop. 11.2]
.

Corollary 3.8
.

Consider the Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)
in
Section

3.1
. Let
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
\mathbf{H}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
be the restrictions of
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
\mathbf{H}_{p}
to
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
respectively. Define
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
\mathbf{L}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
similarly. The natural isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbf{H}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
induces an isomorphism of filtered flat vector bundles
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbf{L}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\simeq\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbf{H}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
are given by the first
p
-adic and de Rham cohomology of the universal abelian scheme on
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, so that the natural isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathbf{H}_{p,\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is given by
Theorem

3.6
. As
\mathbf{L}_{?}
is the image of the idempotent projector
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{?}:\mathrm{End}(\mathbf{H}_{?})\to\mathrm{End}(\mathbf{H}_{?})
for
?=p,\mathrm{dR}
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
sends the restriction of
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{p}
over
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(G)_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
to that of
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathrm{dR}}
. This can be checked at closed points, for which the statement follows from Blasius’ result. See
[
Mad16
, Prop. 4.7]
.
14
14

14

Note that here we are only talking about abelian varieties. The usage of Blasius result is to be avoided only for matching isomorphisms.

∎

3.3.
Matching de Rham and crystalline realizations

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
B
be a smooth connected
\mathbb{C}
-variety and
\mathbb{V}=(\mathbb{V}_{B},\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR}})
be a polarized
\mathbb{Q}
-VHS of pure weight
2
. Assume that there is no locally constant rational class which is of type
(1,1)
everywhere. Suppose that at a point
t_{0}\in B
, there exists some
\lambda\in\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR},t_{0}}^{(1,1)}
such that the Kodaira-Spencer map induces a surjection
T_{t_{0}}B\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR},t_{0}}^{(0,2)}
.
Let
U\subseteq B
be any complex analytic disk containing
t_{0}
.

Then some non-empty open ball
\mathbb{B}
of
\mathbb{V}_{B,t_{0}}
has the following property: For every
\beta\in\mathbb{B}
, there exists a point
t\in U
such that
\beta_{t}\in\mathbb{V}_{B,t}
obtained by
\beta
via parallel transport within
U
is of Hodge type
(1,1)
.

Proof.

This is implied by the proof of the density of Hodge loci
[
Voi13
, Thm 3.5]
. We sketch the argument for readers’ convenience. Up to replacing
B
by an étale cover and splitting off a direct summand, we may assume that there is no local section of
\mathbb{V}
which is constant of type
(1,1)
. Denote by
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
the real bundle
\mathbb{V}_{B}{\otimes}\mathbb{R}
over
S
. Let
F^{1}\mathcal{V}
(resp.
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
) denote the total space of the vector bundle
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR}}
(resp.
\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR}}
,
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{R}}
) restricted to
U
. The connection on
\mathcal{V}
defines a canonical trivialization of holomorphic vector bundle
\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{U}=\mathbb{V}_{t_{0}}\times U
, so there is a natural projection
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}_{t_{0}}
.
Let
\phi
denote its restriction to
F^{1}\mathcal{V}
.

It follows from the assumption that for some
\widetilde{\lambda}\in(\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap F^{1}\mathcal{V})_{t_{0}}
,
\nabla_{t_{0}}\widetilde{\lambda}\colon T_{B,t_{0}}\to\mathbb{V}^{0,2}_{t_{0}}
is surjective. By Lem. 3.6 in
loc. cit.
,
\phi_{\mathbb{R}}\colon F^{1}\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mathbb{R},t_{0}}
is a submersion, in particular an open map, near
\widetilde{\lambda}
. Take
\mathbb{B}
to be the intersection of
\mathcal{V}_{B,t_{0}}
with a small open neighborhood of
\widetilde{\lambda}
. Then the projection of
\phi^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{B})
to
U
is the Hodge locus.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

In the setting of
Theorem

3.4
, let
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}:\mathbf{L}(-1)_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
be the isometry
of flat vector bundles given by
\mathbb{D}^{{\mathrm{alg}}}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\alpha_{p})
.
Then for any isomorphism
\tau:\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{C}
, the restriction of
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
to
S^{\dagger}\subseteq S_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\simeq}}S_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
is equal to
\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.

First, we remark that by
Theorem

3.6
and
Remark

3.7
, we may identify
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbf{P}_{p}|_{S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}})
with
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
. Together with
Corollary

3.8
,
\mathbb{D}^{{\mathrm{alg}}}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\alpha_{p})
gives rise to an isomorphism
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
as above.

Proof.

Note that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S_{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR}}
are both isomorphisms
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-1)|_{S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}}
.
Our goal is to show that
\gamma:(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S_{\mathbb{C}}})^{-1}\circ\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR}}
is the identity on
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}}
. Let
s\in S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}
be any
\mathbb{C}
-point.
Since
\gamma
is horizontal with respect to the Gauss-Manin connection and
S^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{C}}
is connected, it suffices to show that the fiber
\gamma_{s}
of
\gamma
at
s
is the identity on
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s}=\mathrm{PH}^{2}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}/\mathbb{C})
.

We first claim that, if
z\in\mathbf{P}_{B,s}\cap\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
, then
\gamma_{s}(z)=z
.
Lefschetz theorem on
(1,1)
classes tells us that
z
comes from a line bundle
\xi\in\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{s})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
On the other hand,
\alpha_{B}(z)\in\mathbf{L}_{B,s}
is a Hodge class, and correponds to an element of
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{s})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Under the isomorphism
\tau
, the triple
(s,\xi,\zeta)
descends to a finite extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, i.e., there exists
s_{0}\in S(K)
,
\xi_{0}\in\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{s_{0}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\zeta_{0}\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{s_{0}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
(s,\xi,\zeta)=(s_{0},\xi_{0},\zeta_{0}){\otimes}_{K}\mathbb{C}
.
Under the de Rham comparison isomorphism,
c_{1,p}(\xi)
is sent to
c_{1,\mathrm{dR}}(\xi)

[
IIK21
, Prop. 11.2]
.
The same is true for the cycle class of
\zeta_{0}
.
Note that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}_{p,s}(-1){\otimes}B_{\mathrm{dR}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{P}_{p,s}{\otimes}B_{\mathrm{dR}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s_{0}}(-1){\otimes}_{K}B_{\mathrm{dR}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s_{0}}{\otimes}_{K}B_{\mathrm{dR}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{p,s}{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR},s_{0}}{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}_{\mathrm{dR}}}

As the isomorphism
\alpha_{B,s}
sends
c_{1,p}(\xi)
to
\mathrm{cl}_{p}(\zeta)
, by the compatibility
\alpha_{p,s}=\alpha_{B,s}{\otimes}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we know that the map
\alpha_{p,s}
sends
c_{1,p}(\xi)
to
\mathrm{cl}_{p}(\zeta)
as well.
Therefore, we must have that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR},s_{0}}
sends
c_{1,\mathrm{dR}}(\xi_{0})
to
\mathrm{cl}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\zeta_{0})
. This implies that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
sends
c_{1,\mathrm{dR}}(\xi)
to
\mathrm{cl}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\zeta)
, which does the the same as
\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
. This proves the claim.

Suppose that
s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbb{C}}
is another
\mathbb{C}
-point, and
z^{\prime}\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s^{\prime}}
is obtained from
z
via parallel transport along some path
[0,1]\to S^{\dagger}
connecting
s
and
s^{\prime}
.
Then
\gamma_{s}
fixes
z
if and only if
\gamma_{s^{\prime}}
fixes
z^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathbf{P}_{B,s}{\otimes}\mathbb{C}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
, there exists a basis
z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}\in\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
such that each
z_{i}\in\mathbf{P}_{B,s}
.
By
Assumption

3.2
.(ii), Lem.
3.9
is applicable to
\mathcal{X}|_{S^{\dagger}}
.
Hence for every
z_{i}
, there exists a point
s_{i}
near
s
such that parallel transport along some path connecting
s
and
s_{i}
sends
z
to a Hodge
(1,1)
-class. Therefore, we may conclude by the observation in the preceeding paragraph.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

In the setting of Thm
3.4
, there exist an isometry of F-isocrystals
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}:\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)[1/p]|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}}
which satisfies the following properties:

(a)

The isomorphism between flat vector bundles given by evaluating
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}
on the rigid-generic fiber of the
p
-adic completion of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
agrees with
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
under the crystalline-de Rham comparison isomorphism.

(b)

For any isomorphism
\tau:\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\,{\cong}\,\mathbb{C}
, the restriction of
\alpha_{\mathrm{dR}}
to
S_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\simeq}}S_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
agrees with
\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}
on
S^{\dagger}
.

(c)

If for every closed point
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
and
i=2,3
,
\mathrm{H}^{i}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}/W(k(s)))
is
p
-torsion-free, then
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}
restricts to an isometry of locally free F-crystals
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}}
.

Proof.

To construct
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}
, one can apply the crystalline Riemann–Hilbert functor
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{crys}}
defined recently in
[
GY24
, Thm. 1.10]
(or the
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}
functor in
[
TT19
, Def. 3.12]
as here the
p
-adic base field is unramified).
By
[
GY24
, Thm. 1.10.(iv)]
these functors are compatible with Liu–Zhu’s
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}
functor, which gives (a). Part (b) was proved in
Lemma

3.10
.

It remains to show (c).
Before the argument, we note that by
Lemma

2.3
,
R^{2}f_{\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathrm{crys}*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is locally free.
Hence we can define the
integral
crystalline F-crystal
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}
.
By taking the relative de Rham cohomology sheaf for the morphism
f
, we also obtain the integral flat connection
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.
Now we first show that the isomorphism
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
extends over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
.
In other words, there exists an (necessarily unique) isomorphism
\alpha_{\mathrm{dR}}:\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-1)|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}
whose restriction to
S_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.
Let
U=\mathrm{Spec}(A)\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{W}
be any affine open on which both
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}
and
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-1)|_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}}
are trivialized.
Then over
U
,
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}
is given by an element
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(A[1/p])
.
Our goal is to show that the matrix coefficients are integral, i.e.,
M\in\mathrm{GL}_{m}(A)
.
By the same argument as in
[
Mad15
, Prop. 5.11]
, it suffices to show the following: For every closed point
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
with residue field
k\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}k(s)
, if we set
K_{0}=W[1/p]
, then for every
\widetilde{s}\in\mathcal{S}(W(k))
lifting
s
and
t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widetilde{s}[1/p]
, the isomorphism
\alpha_{t}:\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-1)|_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{t}
sends the
W
-lattice
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}(-1)|_{\widetilde{s}}
isomorphically onto
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\widetilde{s}}
.

The above claim follows from the recent advance in integral
p
-adic Hodge theory: Let
\mathfrak{S}
be the ring
W[\![u]\!]
and view
W
as a
\mathfrak{S}
-algebra via
u\mapsto 0
.
Let
\mathrm{BK}(-)
denote the Breuil–Kisin functor associates to each
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear crystalline
\mathrm{Gal}_{K}
-representation a finite free
\mathfrak{S}
-module in
[
BMS18
, Thm 4.4]
.
Then the F-isocrystal
\mathrm{BK}(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{t}},\mathbb{Z}_{p})){\otimes}_{\mathfrak{S}}K
is naturally identified with
(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{t}},\mathbb{Z}_{p}){\otimes}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{\mathrm{Gal}_{K}}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{s}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
.
By the torsionfree assumption on crystalline cohomology, we know from
[
BMS18
, Thm. 14.6.(iii)]
that under
\mathsf{c}_{\mathrm{crys}}:\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{s}},\mathbb{Q}_{p}))\simeq\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathcal{X}_{s_{0}}/W){\otimes}K
, the image of the
W
-lattice
\mathrm{BK}(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{t}},\mathbb{Z}_{p})){\otimes}_{\mathfrak{S}}W
is precisely
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}/W)
.
By restriction,
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys},s}\subseteq\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathcal{X}_{s}/W)
is the image of
\mathrm{BK}(\mathbf{P}_{p,\bar{t}}){\otimes}_{\mathfrak{S}}W
.
Likewise,
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys},s}
is recovered by
\mathrm{BK}(\mathbf{L}_{p,\bar{t}}){\otimes}_{\mathfrak{S}}W
inside
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\mathbf{L}_{p,\bar{t}})
(where the torsion-freeness of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}
follows from
[
Mad16
, Prop. 4.7(iv)]
).
Since
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{t}=\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\alpha_{p}|_{t})
, where the latter by assumption is further isomorphic to
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\alpha_{p}|_{t})\simeq\mathrm{BK}(\alpha_{p}|_{t})\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}W[1/p]
, and since
\alpha_{p}|_{t}
sends the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice
\mathbf{L}_{p,\bar{t}}(-1)
isomorphically onto
\mathbf{P}_{p,\bar{t}}
, we have that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{t}\simeq\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\alpha_{p}|_{t})
sends
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys},s}(-1)
to
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys},s}
.

Via the crystalline-de Rham comparison isomorphism, there exists globally an isometry of crystals
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}
whose evaluation on the
p
-adic completion of
\mathcal{S}_{W}
agrees with that of
\alpha_{\mathrm{dR}}
. After inverting
p
, it agrees with the isometry of F-isocrystals defined in the first paragraph.
∎

4.
Proofs of theorems

In this section, we state and prove the precise form of
Theorem

1.3
(see
Theorem

4.2
) and then specialize it to prove
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
.

To deal with the singular fibers of the given family of surfaces
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
, we introduce the notion of
extended period morphism
in
Definition

4.3
, extending the Kuga–Sakate construction which only exists within the smooth locus.
Then we prove that for any point
t\in\mathsf{M}
and a special endomorphism
\zeta
of the Kuga–Satake abelian variety of
t
, there is always an extended period morphism through
t
such that
\zeta
deforms to the locus of milder singularities.

4.1.
A general statement

Recall that for a proper morphism
f:S\to T
between schemes, the discriminant locus
\mathrm{Disc}(f)
is the closed subset
\{t\in T\mid S_{t}\textrm{ is singular}\}
, equipped with reduced subscheme structure.
Note that the formation of the underlying closed subset of
\mathrm{Disc}(f)
commutes with arbitrary base change, but it may become non-reduced.

Set-up 4.1
.

Let
p>2
be a prime and
\mathsf{M}
be a connected scheme which is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be a projective and generically smooth morphism with geometrically irreducible fibers of dimension
2
. Assume the following conditions:

(a)

Generic fiber:
There exists a lattice
\Lambda\subseteq\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})_{\mathrm{tf}}
, where
\eta
is the generic point of
\mathsf{M}
, such that
p\nmid|\Lambda^{\vee}/\Lambda|
and
\Lambda
contains the class of an ample line bundle.

(b)

Compactification of the base:
There exists a smooth scheme
\mathsf{B}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
and a smooth projective morphism
\pi:\overline{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathsf{B}
such that
\mathsf{M}
embeds as an open subscheme into
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
.

(c)

Complement of the base:
The complement
\mathsf{H}
of
\mathsf{M}
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, equipped with the reduced scheme structure, has an open subscheme
\mathsf{H}^{+}\subseteq\mathsf{H}
which is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
such that for every
b\in\mathsf{B}
and every irreducible component
H
of
\mathsf{H}_{b}
of codimension
1
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
, the intersection
H\cap\mathsf{H}^{+}_{b}
is dense in
H
.

(d)

Geometric genus and Kodaira–Spencer map:

There exists a point
s\in\mathsf{M}(\mathbb{C})
such that
h^{2,0}(\mathcal{X}_{s})=1
, and the Kodaira–Spencer map
\nabla_{s}:T_{s}\mathsf{M}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}),\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{s}}))
has rank
>1
and does not vanish on the subspace
T_{s}\mathsf{B}_{\pi(s)}\subseteq T_{s}\mathsf{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
(e.g., when
\mathrm{rank\,}\nabla_{s}>\mathrm{min}(1,\dim\mathsf{B}_{\mathbb{C}})
).

(e)

Discriminant locus:
The discriminant locus
\mathsf{D}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{Disc}(f)\subseteq\mathsf{M}
is a proper subscheme of
\mathsf{M}
and there exists an open subscheme
\mathsf{D}^{+}\subseteq\mathsf{D}
which is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
such that for every
b\in\mathsf{B}
and for every irreducible component
D
of
\mathsf{D}_{b}
of codimension
1
in
\mathsf{M}_{b}
, the intersection
D\cap\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}
is dense in
D
.

(f)

Singularity:
For every geometric point
s\to\mathsf{D}
, the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{s}
has at worst rational double points.

Here we emphasize that most of the items in
Set-up

4.1
are quite general for moduli of surfaces, except for condition (d), which is pivotal to the Kuga–Satake construction. Moreover, let us remark that by the semi-continuity theorem, any geometric fiber has
h^{2,0}\geq 1
, and hence cannot be ruled (cf.
Lemma

2.12
).

We define
\sf{D}^{-}
to be the closed subscheme
\mathsf{D}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}^{+}
with the reduced structure.
By construction, for each point
b\in B
, the intersection
\mathsf{D}^{-}\cap\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
is of codimension
\geq 2
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
.
In addition, we let
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
be the open subscheme
\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}
, namely the smooth locus of the given family.
Summarizing the geometric objects mentioned above, we get the following diagram of schemes:

(19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}|_{\mathsf{M}^{\circ}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}|_{\mathsf{D}^{+}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{M}^{\circ}=\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}|_{\mathsf{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{X}|_{\mathsf{D}^{-}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}/\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}^{-}=\mathsf{D}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{H}}
proper smooth
“mildest singularity”
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
RDP
“worse singularity”
smooth

Here in the diagram, every
\rightarrowtail
arrow is a closed embedding, and every
\hookrightarrow
arrow is the open immersion of the complement of the
\rightarrowtail
arrow right below.
The objects in the ground level are base schemes and various loci, while the objects in the second level corresponds to the restrictions of the family in question.

This section is devoted to proving the following theorem. Recall that we use
\breve{X}
to denote the minimal resolution of a normal surface over a field, which is unique up to unique isomorphism.

Theorem 4.2

(Tate conjecture for the minimal resolution of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
)
.

Assume Set-up
4.1
.
Let
s\in\mathsf{M}
be a (not necessarily closed) point with residue field
k:=k(s)
, let
\bar{s}\in\mathsf{M}(\bar{k})
be a geometric point over
s
, and assume one of the following conditions.

(i)

The point
s
lies in
\mathsf{D}^{+}
or
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
.

(ii)

\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
is not supersingular (i.e., the Newton polygon of
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s^{p}}/W(k^{p}))[1/p]
is not a straight line.)

(iii)

There is an equality of Hodge numbers
h^{i,j}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s})=h^{i,j}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})
for every
i,j
.

Then the map

c_{1}:\mathrm{NS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{\bar{s}}){\otimes}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\varinjlim_{H}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{\bar{s}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1))^{H}

is an isomorphism, where
H
runs through open subgroups of
\mathrm{Gal}_{k}
.

4.2.
Extended period morphism

We start with the period morphism defined on the smooth locus
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
, by applying various inputs from
Construction

3.3
.
We choose small enough level structures
\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}^{p}\subseteq\widetilde{G}(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
and
\mathcal{K}^{p}\subseteq\mathrm{GSp}(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
such that
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathsf{K}}(\widetilde{G})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{K}}(\mathrm{GSp})
is a closed embedding.
We then apply
Theorem

3.4
to obtain the Kuga–Satake period morphism

\rho:\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G}),

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
is a connected, finite étale cover over
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}=\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}
.
Let
\mathbf{P}_{?}
for
?\in\{B,\ell,p,\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{crys}\}
denote the coefficients sheaves on various fibers of
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
given by primitive cohomologies as in
Section

3.1
.
Then by
Theorem

3.4
,
Corollary

3.8
and
Proposition

3.11
, we know the coefficients sheaves
\mathbf{P}_{?}
are naturally isomorphic to the pullbacks
\rho^{*}\mathbf{L}(-1)_{?}
for corresponding automorphic sheaves on the Shimura variety
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
.
We will simply write
\mathscr{S}
for
\mathscr{S}_{\widetilde{\mathsf{K}}}(\widetilde{G})
.

To eventually deal with the singular fiber in the given family, we will need to extend the period morphism to treat the points in the singular locus.
This leads to the following notion that will play a key role in the entire analysis.

Definition 4.3
.

We define an
extended period morphism
to be a map
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
, where

•

T
is a normal integral
\mathsf{M}
-scheme such that the induced family
\mathcal{X}|_{T}\to T
is Zariski-locally resolvable;

•

the map
\tau
fits into the following commutative diagram

(20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
T^{\circ}\hookrightarrow T
is the open inclusion map of an open dense subscheme.

One of the crucial properties of the extended period morphism is that various cohomology sheaves over
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
and their matching isomorphisms with automorphic sheaves can be extended to the entire scheme
T
.
Concretely, we have the following constructions.

Construction 4.4

(Extension of cofficients and matching isomorphisms)
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism.
By applying the extension results from
Lemma

2.13
and
Construction

2.14
to the primitive cohomology over
T^{\circ}
,
we can obtain the matching isomorphism of coefficients over the entire scheme
T
.

•

(
\ell
-adic coefficients) By
loc. cit.
the pullback
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{T^{\circ}}
uniquely extends to
T
and we denote the extension by
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,T}
.
By the full-faithfulness of the restriction functor from the category of étale local systems on
T
to those on
T^{\circ}
, the isomorphism
\alpha_{\ell}|_{T^{\circ}}:\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1)|_{T^{\circ}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{T^{\circ}}
extends to an isomorphism
\tau^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1)=\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1)|_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,T}
, which we shall denote by
\alpha_{\ell,T}
.

•

(Variations of Hodge structures) Similarly, when
T
is a smooth variety over
\mathbb{C}
, we obtain the canonically extended variation of Hodge structures
(\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{B,T},\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{dR},T})
over
T
.
If additionally
T^{\circ}
maps to
(\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ})^{\dagger}
(the connected component of
b
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}
), then we can extend the isomorphisms
(\alpha^{\dagger}_{B},\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR}})|_{T^{\circ}}
to
(\alpha^{\dagger}_{B,T},\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR},T}):\tau^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{B}(-1),\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}}(-1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{B,T},\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{dR},T})
.

•

(F-(iso)crystals) If
T
is a smooth variety over a perfect field
\kappa
of characteristic
p
, the primitive crystalline cohomology of the resolved family defines an F-isocrystal
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{crys},T}[1/p]
,
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]|_{T^{\circ}}
extends to
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys},T}[1/p]
.
If in addition the crystalline cohomology sheaf
\breve{R}^{2}(f_{T})_{\mathrm{crys},*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{T}/W(\kappa)}
is locally free, then we may also define the F-crystal
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{crys},T}
over
T
, and extend the isomorphism
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T^{\circ}}
to
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys},T}
(
Theorem

2.2
).

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
F
be any discrete valuation field and
\ell
be a prime invertible in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Then every
s\in\mathscr{S}(\widetilde{G})(F)
extends to an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-point if and only if the inertia subgroup of
\mathrm{Gal}_{F}
has finite image in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\bar{s}})
.

Proof.

This follows from an argument of Deligne (
[
Del72
, §6.6]
, cf.
[
And96
, Lem. 9.3.1]
).
By considering the Siegel embedding of
\mathscr{S}
and by the Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich criterion, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{A}_{s}
has good reduction.
Let
I\subseteq\mathrm{Gal}_{F}
be the inertia group.
As the local monodromy is quasi-unipotent, the fact that
I
has finite image in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\bar{s}})
implies that its image in
\mathrm{CSpin}(\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\bar{s}})
is also finite.
However, since we assumed level structure for
\mathscr{S}
is sufficiently small (in particular it is neat), this image has to be trivial. Hence we may conclude by the Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich criterion.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
T=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
for a discrete valuation ring
R
and let
\eta
be its generic point. Whenever there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{M},}

the composite
\eta\to\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}
extends to a morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

By the existence of Artin–Brieskorn resolution of
\mathcal{X}_{T}
over a finite ramified extension of
T
(
[
Art74
, Thm. 2]
), the action of
\mathrm{Gal}_{k(\eta)}
on
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
factors through a finite quotient, and hence the Galois action on
\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\tau(\bar{\eta})}
is finite as well.
Then we conclude by
Lemma

4.5
.
∎

Another useful observation is that for a relative curve with étale boundary in a compactification, to check the period morphism extends, it suffices to do so on the mod
p
reduction.
This will be used in conjunction with
Proposition

2.28
.

Proposition 4.7

(Extension criterion in mixed characteristic)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
.
Let
\overline{T}
be a smooth proper curve over
W=W(k)
, and let
D,H\subseteq\overline{T}
be disjoint relative effective Cartier divisors that are étale over
W
.
Set
T\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{T}\smallsetminus H
and
T^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T\smallsetminus D
.
Then a morphism
\tau^{\circ}:T^{\circ}\to\mathscr{S}_{W}
extends over
T
if and only if its special fiber
\tau^{\circ}_{k}
extends over
T_{k}
.

Proof.

Set
K\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}W[1/p]
.
Let
a
and
b
be geometric points on the special and generic fibers of
T^{\circ}
respectively such that
b
specializes to
a
along a path.
As
\overline{T}
is a compactification of both
T
and
T^{\circ}
whose boundaries are normal crossing divisors, we know by the relative Abhyankar’s lemma in
Proposition

2.25
that
(\tau^{\circ}_{k})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
is tamified ramified along
(\overline{T}\smallsetminus T)_{k}
and
(\overline{T}\smallsetminus T^{\circ})_{k}
.
Moreover, there are specialization maps of tame fundamental groups
\pi_{1}^{t}(T^{\circ}_{\bar{K}},b)\to\pi_{1}^{t}(T^{\circ}_{\bar{k}},a)
and
\pi_{1}^{t}(T_{\bar{K}},b)\to\pi_{1}^{t}(T_{\bar{k}},a)
(
[
Gro71
, XIII, §2.10]
).
The fact that
\tau_{k}^{\circ}
extends over
T_{k}
implies that
(\tau^{\circ}_{k})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
is unramified over
T_{k}
.
This in turn implies that
(\tau^{\circ}_{\bar{K}})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
is unramified over
T_{\bar{K}}
.
Indeed, the situation is that we have a commutative diagram below with solid arrows

(21)

\hbox to237.29pt{\vbox to73.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 118.64128pt\lower-36.29419pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.75pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
