and we want the dashed arrow to exist, which is true thanks to the commutativity of the rest of the diagram.

Now, by
Lemma

4.5
, the map
\tau^{\circ}_{\bar{K}}
extends to the entire generic fiber
T_{\bar{K}}
, by the equality
\pi_{1}^{t}(T^{\circ}_{\bar{K}},b)=\pi_{1}(T^{\circ}_{\bar{K}},b)
and the existence of the dotted arrow.
Since the extended map
\tau_{\bar{K}}:T_{\bar{K}}\to\mathscr{S}_{\bar{K}}
descends to
K
when restricted to an open dense subscheme
T^{\circ}_{\bar{K}}
, we must have that
\tau_{\bar{K}}
itself descends to
K
as well, giving rise to an extension of the original morphism
\tau^{\circ}_{K}:T^{\circ}_{K}\to\mathscr{S}_{K}
over
T_{K}
.
This in turn implies that
(\tau^{\circ}_{K})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
over
T_{K}^{\circ}
is unramified over
T_{K}
. As
(\tau_{K})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
extends to the generic point of the special fiber
T_{k}
, the purity of
\ell
-adic local systems tells us that
(\tau_{K})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
is unramified over
T
.
Hence by the extension property of the canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}
(
[
Kis10
]
, cf.
[
HYZ25
, Thm. 3.4.4]
) again, the map
\tau_{K}
extends to a map of schemes
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
,
which agrees with the map
\tau^{\circ}
when restricted to
T^{\circ}
.
∎

4.3.
Motivic alignment

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of motivic alignment for extended period morphisms.
It relates the divisors of the given surface
\mathcal{X}_{t}
with the special endomorphisms of the Kuga–Satake abelian variety
\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)}
, for points
t\in T
in the given family.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism and
t\to T
be a geometric point. For every
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we say that the pair
(t,\zeta)
is
motivically aligned
if for every
\ell\neq\mathrm{char\,}k(t)
the isomorphism
\alpha_{\ell,T}|_{t}:\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\tau(t)}(-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell,t}
sends the cycle class of
\zeta
into the the first Chern class of some element in
\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}
; if
\mathrm{char\,}k(t)=p
, then we in addition require that (after replacing
k(t)
by its perfection)
\alpha_{\mathrm{crys},t}
sends the crystalline cycle class of
\zeta
to the crystalline first Chern class of some element in
\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Moreover,

•

we say that
t
is motivically aligned if the pair
(t,\zeta)
is motivically aligned for every
\zeta
;

•

we say that a field-valued point
t^{\prime}
is motivically aligned if some (and hence every) geometric point over
t^{\prime}
is motivically aligned;

•

we say that
\tau
is motivically aligned if it is motivically aligned at every point.

Remark 4.9

(Diagram of motivic alignment)
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism, let
t\in T
be a motivically aligned point, and let
\bar{t}
be a geometric point over
t
.
By the Kummer sequence of the
\ell
-adic cohomology, we deduce that the Chern class map defines an injection
c_{1}:\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{PH}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{\bar{t}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1))=\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,\bar{t}}
.
Similarly, the cycle class map defines an injection
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\tau(\bar{t})}
.
Consequently, the injectivity and motivic alignment implies a commutative diagram

(22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{t}})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\tau(\bar{t})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PH}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{\bar{t}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1))=\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,\bar{t}}}(1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ell,\bar{t}}}

where the bottom arrow is the matching isomorphism for
\ell
-adic cohomology.
We call this the
diagram of motivic alignment
.

The main reason of introducing the above notion is that, in order to extend the method of
[
Mad15
]
, we translate the Tate conjecture into a problem of finding motivically aligned extended period morphism.
This is illustrated by the following statement.

Lemma 4.10
.

To prove
Theorem

4.2
, it suffices to construct an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
such that up to replacing
k
by a finite extension, the point
s
lifts to a motivically aligned
k
-point on
T
.

Proof.

Assume such
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
exists, and consider the diagram (
22
) with
t=s
.
Then the conclusion follows from the fact that
\alpha_{\ell,\bar{s}}
is
\mathrm{Gal}_{k}
-equivariant, and that

\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{s}}){\otimes}\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}\to\varinjlim_{H}\mathbf{L}_{\ell,\tau(\bar{s})}^{H}

is an isomorphism, thanks to the Tate theorem for special endomorphisms by Madapusi Pera
[
Mad15
, Thm. 6.4]
.
∎

For the rest of the subsection, we give several useful observations on the motivically aligned extended period morphism.

We first notice that by the Lefschetz
(1,1)
-theorem, the motivic alignedness is automatically true in characteristic zero.
This encapsulates the argument of
[
Mad15
, Thm. 5.17(4)]
.
The germ of this idea can be traced back to
[
Nyg83
]
and
[
NO85
]
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism, and let
t\in T
be a geometric point
t
in characteristic
0
.
Then
t
is motivically aligned.

Proof.

We may reduce to the case when
k(t)=\mathbb{C}
. By resolution of singularities, we can always find a smooth
\mathbb{C}
-variety
T^{\prime}
admitting a proper and birational morphism to
T_{\mathbb{C}}
.
For the purpose of proving the proposition, there is no harm in replacing
T
by
T^{\prime}
and assume that
T
is a smooth
\mathbb{C}
-variety.
Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
acts transitively on the set of connected components of
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Therefore, by take the base change along automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}
, we may assume that
T
maps into
(\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ})^{\dagger}
. Recall that in
Construction

4.4
, we extended the isomorphism
(\alpha^{\dagger}_{B},\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR}})|_{T^{\circ}}
to the isomorphism
(\alpha^{\dagger}_{B,T},\alpha^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{dR},T}):\tau^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{B},\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathbb{C}})(-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{B,T},\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{dR},T})
on the scheme
T
.
By the Lefschetz
(1,1)
-theorem,
\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}
can be identified with the
(0,0)
-classes in
\mathrm{PH}^{2}_{B}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t},\mathbb{Q}(1))
.
Similarly,
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
can be identified with those in
\mathbf{L}_{B,\tau(t)}
.
∎

Our next observation is that the motivic alignedness is invariant under deformation.

Lemma 4.12

(Motivic alignedness is closed under specialization)
.

Suppose that
t^{\prime},t
are geometric points on
T
with a chosen path
\gamma:t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow t
,
and
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{t^{\prime}})
specializes to
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{t})
.

(a)

If the pair
(t^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})
is motivically aligned, then so is
(t,\zeta)
.

(b)

The converse is also true provided that if
\mathrm{char\,}k(t^{\prime})=p
, we additionally assume that for the scheme theoretic image
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
of
t^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}|_{T^{\prime}}
admits a simultaneous resolution whose total space is a projective scheme near
t
.

Proof.

The forward direction follows from the existence of the following diagrams for various specialization morphisms and their compatibility with each other. For
\ell
-adic (
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{T}^{\times}
) cohomology we have a diagram

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{t^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}_{\ell_{0},t^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{P}_{\ell_{0},t^{\prime}}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{L}_{\ell_{0},t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{P}_{\ell_{0},t}(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PNS}(\breve{\mathcal{X}_{t}})_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sp}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\alpha_{\ell,t^{\prime}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{sp}_{\gamma}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\alpha_{\ell,t}}}}}

Here the left and the right diagrams commute because the specialization of cycles is compatible with that of
\ell
-adic cohomology. That the central diagram commutes is a tautological consequence of the existence of a global matching isomorphism
\alpha_{\ell}
.
When
\mathrm{char\,}k(t^{\prime})=p
, the analogous statement for crystalline cohomology follows from a similar argument and we omit the details.
If
\mathrm{char\,}k(t^{\prime})=0
and
\mathrm{char\,}k(t)=p
, then apply the crystalline comparison theorem and its compatibility with cycle class maps (cf.
[
IIK21
, Cor. 11.6]
).

Proving the converse amounts to deforming the line bundles.
By
Proposition

4.11
, it suffices to consider the case when both
t
and
t^{\prime}
are in characteristic
p
.
As the statement would not change if we replace the discrete valuation ring by its completion, we reduce to the case when the path
\gamma
is defined by the ring
k[\![x]\!]
.
Let
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}
be an element such that
(t,\zeta)
is motivically aligned, i.e., there exists an element
\xi\in\mathrm{PNS}(\mathcal{X}_{t})_{\mathbb{Q}}
whose cycle class matches that of
\zeta
via
\alpha_{\ell,t}
.
We need to show that
\xi
lifts to an element in
\mathrm{PNS}(\mathcal{X}_{t^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
At this point we use the isomorphism of F-isocrystals
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{crys},T}[1/p]\simeq\tau^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)[1/p]
.
The assumption that
\zeta
lifts to
\zeta^{\prime}
implies that the cycle class of
\zeta
gives rise to a global section of the F-crystal
\gamma^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)
. This implies that
c_{1,\mathrm{crys}}(\xi)
also lifts to a global section of the F-isocrystal
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]|_{\gamma}
.
Then we conclude by
[
Mor19
, Thm. 3.6]
.
15
15

15

We can drop the algebraizability assumption in
(b)
and use the more elementary
[
Ogu79
, Cor. 1.13]
when there are sufficient torsion-freeness assumptions on cohomology.

∎

4.4.
Admissible curves

As we will see later, for points away from the locus
\mathsf{D}^{-}
, we will extend period morphisms along curves by “taking limits”, and then deform these curves over a
p
-adic discrete valuation ring. To guarantee that the curves we choose are sufficiently amenable to deformation, we recall the following key notion from
[
HYZ25
, Def. 6.1.1]
.

Definition 4.13
.

(strongly dominant family of curves)

Let
S
and
T
be two smooth irreducible
k
-varieties,
g\colon\mathscr{C}\to T
be a smooth family of connected curves, and
\varphi\colon\mathscr{C}\to S
be a morphism with equi-dimensional fibers (i.e., there exists some integer
d\geq 0
such that every geometric fiber of
\varphi
is non-empty and equi-dimensional of dimension
d
).
Let
U
be the maximal open subvariety of
\mathscr{C}
on which the composition of the (relative) tangent bundles
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}/T)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}\mathscr{C}\to\varphi^{*}(\mathcal{T}S)
does not vanish, where
\mathcal{T}S
is the tangent bundle of
S
.
By assumption, there is then a natural morphism
U\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}S)
, induced by the identification
\mathscr{C}\,{\cong}\,\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}/T))
and the above sequence of the tangent bundles.

Suppose that

(i)

the induced morphism
\mathscr{C}\to T\times S
is quasi-finite,

(ii)

for every
k
-point
s\in S
, the fiber
U_{s}:=U\cap\varphi^{-1}(s)
is dense in
\varphi^{-1}(s)
;

(iii)

the morphism
U\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}S)
has equi-dimensional fibers.

Then we say that the family of curves
\mathscr{C}/T

homogeneously dominates

S
(via the morphism
\varphi
).
If there exists an open dense subvariety
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
such that the restriction
\mathscr{C}|_{T^{\prime}}
homogeneously dominates
S
, then we say that
\mathscr{C}/T

strongly dominates

S
.

Roughly speaking, the family
\mathscr{C}/T
homogeneously dominates
S
if there are curves parameterized by
T
passing through every given point on
S
in any given direction, and the sub-family of such curves has a fixed dimension.

We make the following elementary observation on the reducedness of the intersection.

Lemma 4.14
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.13
, suppose that
g:\mathscr{C}\to T
strongly dominates
S
and
D\subseteq S
is a reduced closed proper subvariety.
Then for a general
t\in T
, the fiber product
\mathscr{C}_{t}\times_{S}D
is reduced (i.e., the image of
\mathscr{C}_{t}
passes through
D
transversely).

Proof.

This follows from a simple dimension counting argument as in the proof of the Bertini theorem, so here we just give a sketch of the idea: There is no harm in assuming that
\mathscr{C}
homogeneously dominates
S
, and note that the reducedness assumption on
D
implies that the smooth locus
D^{\mathrm{sm}}
is open and dense.

For each
s\in S
, the subset
T(s):=\{t\in T\mid\mathscr{C}_{t}\times_{S}\{s\}\neq\emptyset\}
has codimension at least
\dim S-1
in
T
.
As the singular locus
D^{\mathrm{sing}}
of
D
is codimension at least
2
in
S
,
\{t\in T(s)\mid s\in D^{\mathrm{sing}}\}
is of codimension at least
1
in
T
.
Likewise, for every subspace
V\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{s}S
, viewed as a subvariety of
\mathcal{T}S
, consider

T(s,V):=\{t\in T\mid U_{t}\times_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}S)}V\neq\emptyset\textrm{ or }(\mathscr{C}\smallsetminus U)_{t}\times_{S}\{s\}\neq\emptyset\}.

One readily checks that
Definition

4.13
.(ii, iii) implies that

\{t\in T(s,V)\mid s\in D^{\mathrm{sm}},V=\mathcal{T}_{s}D^{\mathrm{sm}}\}

is of codimension at least
1
as well.
∎

As we shall see, we will make use of complete intersection curves, and hence we make the following construction.

Construction 4.15
.

Assume the notations as in
Set-up

4.1
.
We choose an embedding of
\mathsf{B}
-schemes
\overline{\mathsf{M}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{N}_{\mathsf{B}}
for large enough
N
, and let
r
be the integer
\dim\overline{\mathsf{M}}-\dim\mathsf{B}-1
.
Let
(d_{1},\cdots,d_{r})
be a tuple of positive integers and consider the product

\overline{\mathcal{T}}=(\prod_{i=1}^{r}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{N}}(d_{i})|\times\mathsf{B}).

Then every point
t\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
is given by a tuple of the form
([H_{1}],\cdots,[H_{r}],b)
such that
b\in\mathsf{B}(k(t))
, and
[H_{i}]
is a
k(t)
-point of
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{N}_{k(b)}}(d_{i})|
, which corresponds to a degree
d_{i}
hypersurface
H_{i}
in
\mathbb{P}^{N}_{k(b)}
.
Let
\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\overline{\mathcal{T}}
be the universal complete intersection whose fiber over each
([H_{1}],\cdots,[H_{r}],b)
is the intersection
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}\cap H_{1}\cap\cdots\cap H_{r}
, which by our choice is a hypersurface in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
of dimension
\geq 1
.
Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{T}}
be the open locus over which
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is smooth, irreducible and projective, and we write
\mathscr{C}
for the restriction of
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
to
\mathcal{T}
.

It is not hard to check that if
d_{i}
’s are chosen to be sufficiently large, then for every geometric point
b^{\prime}\to\mathsf{B}
,
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{T})_{b^{\prime}}
is a family of curves which
strongly dominates

\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b^{\prime}}
.
In general, for any field-valued point
b
on
\mathsf{B}
, we say that an irreducible smooth projective curve
C\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
is an
admissible curve
if it arises as a fiber of
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{T})_{b}
for such a family. Summarizing the construction, we get the following diagram of schemes

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
family of curves
strongly dominant

4.5.
Locus of the mildest singularity

In this subsection, we prove the Tate conjecture for the minimal model of the surfaces
\mathcal{X}_{s}
that are smooth or lie on
\mathsf{D}^{+}
(those which morally have the mildest singularity).
For any such point
s
, we will construct an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
from a curve
T
in positive characteristic such that
s
is contained in
T
.
Then we prove that any special endomorphism of the Kuga–Satake abelian variety of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
admits a mixed characteristic deformation along a lift of the curve
T
, by combining the tools developed in
Section

2.8
and
Section

3
together with a key trick in
[
HYZ25
]
.

As a quick preparation, we recall the “
\lambda
-number” introduced in
loc. cit.
, and give it a name.

Definition 4.16
.

Let
S
be a noetherian integral normal scheme.
Let
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\times}
be a prime and let
\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
be an étale
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-local system over
S
.
We denote by
\lambda(\mathsf{W}_{\ell})
the
monodromy invariant number
of
\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
, defined as the integer
\dim\varinjlim_{U}\mathsf{W}_{\ell,\bar{s}}^{U}
where
\bar{s}
is a geometric point on
S
and
U
runs through open subgroups of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(S,\bar{s})
.

In the above definition, one may alternatively define
\lambda(\mathsf{W}_{\ell})
as
\dim\varinjlim_{U}\mathsf{W}_{\ell,\bar{\eta}}^{U}
, where
\eta
is the generic point of
S
and
U
runs through the open subgroups of
\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{\eta}/\eta)
(for a fixed geometric point
\bar{\eta}
over
\eta
), thanks to the fact that the natural map
\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{\eta}/\eta)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(S,\bar{s})
is a surjection (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0BQM
]
.)

Definition 4.17
.

Let
T
be a noetherian base scheme and
S
be a smooth
T
-scheme of finite type.
Let
\ell\in\mathcal{O}_{T}^{\times}
be a prime and let
\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
be an étale
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-local system.
We say that the local system
\mathsf{W}_{\ell}

has constant fiberwise monodromy invariant number
(with respect to
T
) if there exists a number
\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}=\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}(\mathsf{W}_{\ell})
such that for every geometric point
\bar{t}\to T
and every connected component
S^{\circ}_{\bar{t}}
of
S_{\bar{t}}
, we have
\lambda(\mathsf{W}_{\ell}|_{S^{\circ}_{\bar{t}}})=\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}
.

Now we prove the motivic alignment for an admissible curve that is away from the worst locus
\mathsf{D}^{-}
.
For the convenience of discussion, in the following for any scheme
\overline{T}
over
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, we use
T
to denote the fiber product
\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}
, and use
T^{\circ}
to denote the fiber product
\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}^{\circ}
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
, let
b
be a
k
-point on
\mathsf{B}
.
Let
\overline{C}
be an admissible curve in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
such that it intersects
\mathsf{D}_{b}\cup\mathsf{H}_{b}
transversely (i.e., the intersection is reduced) and the intersection is contained in
\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}\cup\mathsf{H}^{+}_{b}
. Set
C:=\overline{C}\cap\mathsf{M}
.

Let
C_{1}\to C
be a connected, finite, and generically étale cover.
Then any extended period morphism
\tau:C_{1}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
is motivically aligned.

Proof.

Step 0: Construct intermediate curve
C_{0}
.

Let

be the unique connected component

Let

Then the morphism

\overline{C}_{0}\to\overline{C}

\overline{\mathsf{M}}
.
We let
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{C}^{\circ}_{0}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}
.
Then we claim that the map

.
Indeed, if we choose a compactification

of

and take

to be the unique smooth compactification of

, then

will extend to a morphism

which factors through

and send the open subscheme

into

.
So the claim follows by noticing that
C_{0}
is the image of
C_{1}
in
\overline{C}_{0}
.

To sum up, we obtain the commutative diagram on the left below.
As we will see in Step 2, we shall deform the morphisms over

and obtain the diagram on the right.

.

, we have the equality between the Picard numer and the monodromy invariant number

.

\mathrm{Spec}(W)
(z,\zeta)

\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{H}
\mathrm{Def}(z,\zeta)

Step 3: Prepare for motivic alignment.
Let
k^{\prime}
be any algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
, let
z=\mathrm{Spec}(k^{\prime})\to C_{1}
be a geometric point.
We want to prove that for the extended period morphism
\tau
, the pair
(z,\zeta)
is motivically aligned for every
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(z)})
.
Note that now we know two facts:

(i)

By
Theorem

3.4
(b), the local system
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\circ}}
is isomorphic to
(\tau^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1))|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\circ}}
.

(ii)

By Step 1, the local system
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\circ}}
has constant fiberwise monodromy invariant number over
W
, and
\lambda^{{\mathrm{geo}}}(\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\circ}})=\dim\mathrm{PNS}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

By Artin-Brieskorn resolution
[
Art74
, p.322]
, the Henselianization of the resolution space of
\mathcal{X}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
at
z
is finite surjective over the Henselianization of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
at
z
. Therefore, we can find an étale neighborhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
z
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, and a finite surjective morphism
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
W
-schemes, which is generically étale on both the special fiber and the generic fiber over
W
, such that
\mathcal{X}|_{\mathcal{U}}
admits a simultaneous resolution
\breve{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{U}
(cf. the proof of
Proposition

2.6
).
We may lift
\bar{\eta}
to a geometric generic point on
\mathcal{U}
, which we still denote by
\bar{\eta}
.
By resolving singularities using Lipman’s theorem
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0ADX
,
0BGP
]
, we may assume that
\mathcal{U}
is a regular scheme of finite type over
W
, except that now
\mathcal{U}
is not necessarily finite over
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, but remains proper.
The composition
\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathscr{S}_{W}
is then an extended period morphism.

Step 4: Deform
(z,\zeta)
to char
0
.
Consider the deformation space
\mathrm{Def}(z,\zeta)
of
(z,\zeta)
along
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. It is a formal scheme over
W
which pro-represents the functor that sends every Artin local ring
R
with residue field
k^{\prime}
to the set of
R
-points on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
extending
z
along which
\zeta
deforms. We argue that
\mathrm{Def}(z,\zeta)
is flat over
W
.
At this point the conclusion follows from the same proof as
[
HYZ25
, Thm. 5.2.3]
, where we take the
\mathsf{M}
therein to be the scheme
\mathcal{C}_{1}
in the current context.
For the reader’s convenience, we explain the modification needed in order to adapt the argument in
loc. cit.

If the flatness does not hold, then since
\mathrm{Def}(z,\zeta)
is of codimension one in the formal completion of
\mathcal{C}_{1}{\otimes}_{W}W(k^{\prime})
at
z
, the special endomorphism
\zeta
must comes from the specialization of some element in
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\eta}_{p}})
, where
\bar{\eta}_{p}
is a geometric generic point of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.
The latter implies that

\dim\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\eta}_{p}})_{\mathbb{Q}}>\dim\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\eta}})_{\mathbb{Q}}.

On the other hand, we have

\dim\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\eta}})_{\mathbb{Q}}=\dim\mathrm{PNS}(\mathcal{X}_{\bar{\eta}})_{\mathbb{Q}}=\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}(\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{\mathcal{U}}),

and at the same time we have

\dim\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{\eta}_{p}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\leq\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}(\tau^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1))|_{C_{1}})=\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}(\mathbf{P}_{\ell}|_{C_{1}}).

This contradicts the fact that
\tau^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\ell}(-1))|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\simeq\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,\mathcal{C}_{1}}
has constant fiberwise monodromy invariant
\lambda^{\mathrm{geo}}
, which thus concludes the proof.

Step 5: Establish motivic alignment.
Recall that our goal is to show that
(z,\zeta)
is motivically aligned for the geometric point
z
on
C_{1}
.
Since the extended period morphism
\mathcal{U}_{k}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
factors through
C_{1}
, it suffices to choose a lift
z^{\prime}
of
z
to
\mathcal{U}_{k}
and prove that
(z^{\prime},\zeta)
is motivically aligned.
As the
W
-scheme
\mathcal{U}
is proper and surjective over an étale neighborhood of
z
on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, Step 4 implies that we may lift
(z^{\prime},\zeta)
to characteristic
0
along
\mathcal{U}
.
Hence we may finish the proof by
Proposition

4.11
and
Lemma

4.12
.
∎

Corollary 4.19

(The Tate conjecture for mildly singular fibers)
.

Theorem

4.2
holds when
s\in\mathsf{D}^{+}(k)\cup\mathsf{M}^{\circ}(k)
.

Proof.

Let
b\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi(s)\in\mathsf{B}(k)
.
We can always find some admissible curve
\overline{C}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
which passes through
s
and intersects
\mathsf{H}_{b}\cup\mathsf{D}_{b}
transversely and only along
\mathsf{H}^{+}_{b}\cup\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}
.
To see this, one can show that when the
d_{i}
’s are large enough in
Construction

4.15
, the sub-family of
\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{T}
which passes through
s
in some fixed direction still strongly dominates
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
away from
s
.
Then one applies
Lemma

4.14
.

Then it suffices to construct an extended period morphism
\tau:C_{1}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
as in
Theorem

4.18
. We shall do so by reversing the construction in its proof:
Let
C_{0}^{\circ}
be a connected finite étale cover of a connected component of
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}\times_{\mathsf{M}^{\circ}_{k}}C^{\circ}
, let
\overline{C}_{0}
be its compactification (which admits a map to
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, and let
C_{0}
be the intersection
\overline{C}_{0}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}
.
Then the morphism
C^{\circ}_{0}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
extends to a morphism
C_{0}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
by
Corollary

4.6
.
Now the only problem is that
\mathcal{X}|_{C_{0}}
may not be Zariski-locally resolvable, but we may resolve this by passing to a suitable generically étale cover
C_{1}
of
C_{0}
thanks to
Proposition

2.6
.
∎

4.6.
Contraction of curves by the period morphism

To study the periods for the points in
\mathsf{D}^{-}
, we make some observations on when extended period morphism contracts a curve. In this subsection,
k
is a perfect field of characteristic
p
.

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
C
be a smooth
proper
curve over
k
.
Let
\rho:C\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
be a morphism such that the F-isocrystal
\rho^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]
has constant slope filtration and is nowhere supersingular. Then the map
\rho
contracts
C
to a point.

Here we remind the reader that an F-isocrystal is nowhere supersingular if its Newton polygon at any closed point is not a straight line.

Proof.

This observation is essentially due to Maulik, van der Geer and Katsura (
[
Mau14
, Cor. 5.5]
,
[
GK00
, Thm. 15.3]
) in the context of moduli of K3 surfaces,
and we explain how this extends to orthogonal Shimura varieties.
Consider the Hodge bundle
\lambda
given by
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, and let
\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}
be the determinant Hodge bundle of the uniserval abelian scheme
a:\mathcal{A}\to\mathscr{S}
, i.e.,
\det(a_{*}\omega_{\mathcal{A}/\mathscr{S}})
.
Then by adapting the argument of
[
Mau14
, Prop. 5.8]
, we have
\mathbb{Q}_{+}\cdot\lambda=\mathbb{Q}_{+}\cdot\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathrm{Pic}\,(\mathscr{S})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
As the determinant Hodge bundle
\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}
is ample, so is
\lambda
.

Let
n=\rm rank\,\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}
.
Let
\mathbf{L}
be the restriction of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}
at a point with perfect residue field.
If
\mathbf{L}
is not supersingular (i.e., is isoclinic of slope
0
), then its slope
<0
part is of the slope
-1/h
, where
h
is called the height of
\mathbf{L}
.
Consider now the height (i.e., Newton) stratification
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}=\mathscr{S}_{1}\supsetneq\mathscr{S}_{2}\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq\mathscr{S}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\supsetneq\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
, where
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
is the supersingular locus.
Then each stratum is a Cartier divisor of the previous one, and the Chern class of this Cartier divisor equals that of
\lambda
up to a scaling by
\mathbb{Q}_{+}
.
One can prove this by suitably adapting the proof of
[
GK00
, Thm. 15.1]
.

If
\rho
does not contract
C
to a point, then
\rho
is a finite morphism of
C
onto its scheme theoretic image and
\rho^{*}(\lambda)
is ample.
In particular, the preimage of the height stratification is non-trivial in
C
—this would contradict the assumption that
\rho^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]
has constant slope unless the image of
\rho
is entirely contained in the supersingular locus
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
.
∎

Next we recall a consequence of the Mazur–Ogus inequality.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
Z
be a smooth
k
-variety over
k
, equipped with a morphism
Z\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
.
Let
F_{Z/k}:Z\to Z^{\prime}
be the relative Frobenius morphism.
Then the Hodge filtration on
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{Z^{\prime}}
(and hence
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{Z}
) is determined by the Frobenius structure
\varphi_{\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)}:\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)|_{Z^{\prime}}\to\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)|_{Z}
17
17

17

Note that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}
is not an F-crystal but
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)
is.
.

More precisely, suppose that
\widetilde{Z}
is a Frobenius equivariant
p
-adic formal scheme that lifts
Z
over
W
.
Then

(26)

\mathrm{Fil}^{i}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{Z^{\prime}})=(p\varphi_{\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}})^{-1}\big(p^{i}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{Z})({\widetilde{Z}})\big)\mod p.

Proof.

When the pair
(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}},\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}})
is replaced by
(\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{crys}},\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR}})
, (
26
) is nothing but the Mazur–Ogus formula for the Hodge filtration (see
[
BO78
, Thm. 8.26]
).
Then we use that
(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}},\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}})
is a direct summand of the internal Hom of the object
(\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{crys}},\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{dR}})
.
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
C
be an integral curve over
k
with a morphism
C\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
.
Then
C
is contracted to a point if and only if
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{C}
is constant
F
-crystal equipped with a pairing (i.e., for any
k
-point
t\in C
, there is an isometry of crystals
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}\simeq(C\to\mathrm{Spec}(k))^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{t})
).

Proof.

Only the “if” direction requires a proof.
We may assume that
C
is smooth and let
t\in C
be any
k
-point. Let
u
be a uniformizer at
t
such that the formal completion of
C
at
t
is
\mathrm{Spf}(k[\![u]\!])
.
It suffices to show that the induced map
T_{\infty}:=\mathrm{Spf}(k[\![u]\!])\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
factors though the reduced point
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
, or equivalently, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced map
T_{m}:=\mathrm{Spec}(k[u]/u^{m})\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
factors through
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
. We shall prove this by induction. The base case
T_{1}=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
is clear.
Now suppose the statement holds for
m
, so that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{m}}=(T_{m}\to T_{1})^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{1}})
, which gives us an identification

\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{T_{m+1}}=(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{m}})(T_{m+1})=[(T_{m}\to T_{1})^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{1}})](T_{m+1})=(T_{m+1}\to T_{1})^{*}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{T_{1}}),

when we view
T_{m+1}
as a divided power thickening of
T_{m}
.

18
18

18

More precisely, in the first equality, we implicitly use that the evaluation of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{m+1}}
at the thickening
(T_{m+1},T_{m+1})
coincides with that of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{m}}
at the thickening
(T_{m+1},T_{m})
, together with the identification of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T_{m+1}}(T_{m+1})
with the de Rham cohomology
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{T_{m+1}}
.

As the
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{T_{m+1}})
is obtained from pulling back
\mathrm{Fil}^{1}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{T_{1}}
, the statement follows from
[
Mad16
, Prop. 5.16]
.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

Note that in characteristic
p
, we cannot simply argue that
C
is contracted to a point when the map kills all tangent vectors, as the map might factor through an inseparable morphism. However, the problem goes away when we consider nilpontents of arbitrarily large order.

4.7.
Locus of worse singularity

In this subsection, we prove the Tate conjecture for the minimal resolution of
\mathcal{X}_{s}
for a point
s\in\mathsf{D}^{-}
in positive characteristic, and in particular finish the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.
For any such point
s\in\mathcal{D}^{-}_{k}
, we shall construct an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
in positive characteristic, where
T
is a
\mathsf{M}_{k}
-scheme
T
of dimension
2
, such that
s
is contained in the image of
T
in
\mathsf{M}
.
As heuristics, we in particular prove that the “period” of the surface
\mathcal{X}_{s}
, defined as the image
\tau(T_{s})
in the Shimura variety
\mathscr{S}
, is in fact a single point, where
T_{s}
is the preimage of
s
in
T
and potentially could be of higher dimension.
The latter is achieved by an essential application of the minimal model program for threefolds in positive characteristic, specifically the Kawamawa theorem as in
Section

2.5
.
Knowing the constancy the of period and in particular the constancy of the Kuga–Satake abelian variety of
s
, we may then study the deformation of a special endomorphism along certain curve in
T
that is chosen with delicacy, where various arguments in intersection theory will come to place.

We start with some general observations on intersection theory.
Following
[
Ful98
]
, we use the following notation: If
D
is a Cartier divisor on an integral scheme
X
, we write
|D|
for its support.
For a
k
-dimensional irreducible closed subvariety
Z\subseteq X
(or a formal sum of such), we write
[Z]
for its class in
A_{k}(X)
(the group of
k
-cycles modulo rational equivalence).

Lemma 4.24
.

Suppose that
\pi:S\to S^{\prime}
is a generically finite morphism bewteen projective surfaces over a field and
S
is smooth. Then for every divisor
C
on
S
whose support is contracted by
\pi
(i.e.,
\pi(|C|)
is a point on
S^{\prime}
), the self-intersection
C^{2}
is negative.

Proof.

Let
A
be an ample divisor on
S^{\prime}
. Note that
\pi^{*}(A)
remains big and nef, as this property is invariant under generically finite pullback. In particular,
\pi(A)^{2}>0
. On the other hand,
\pi_{*}([C])=0\in A_{1}(S^{\prime})
as
\pi(|C|)
is a point (see e.g.,
[
Ful98
, §1.4]
). By the projection formula
[
Ful98
, Prop. 2.3(c)]
,
\pi^{*}(A).[C]=\pi_{*}([C]).A=0\in A_{0}(\pi(|C|))\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, the Hodge index theorem on
S
implies that
C^{2}<0
.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

Consider a diagram of morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.80228pt}{18.05472pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.49 24.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

between integral projective varieties over a field such that
S
is a smooth surface and
\pi
is generically finite. Suppose that
C\subseteq S
is a connected reduced curve which is contracted by both
\pi
and
\tau
.

(a)

If
\dim Y=1
, then there exists a connected curve
D\subseteq S
such that
D\subseteq\tau^{-1}(\tau(C))
,
D\cap C\neq\emptyset
, and
D
is not contracted by
\pi
.

(b)

If
\dim Y=2
, then for every Cartier divisor
E
on
Y
with
\tau(C)\in|E|
, there exists an integral curve
D\subseteq S
such that
D
is contained in
\tau^{-1}(E)
,
D\nsubseteq C
and
D\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Below we write
Q
for the image
\tau(C)
, which is a reduced point on
Y
.
We also assume that the image
\tau(S)
is not a point; otherwise the claim is much easier since the map
\pi
cannot contract every curve on
S
..

To prove (a), it is harmless to replace
Y
by its normalization and assume that
Y
is smooth and view
Q
as a divisor on
Y
. Then by the projection formula for the intersection product, we know the self-intersection product
[\tau^{-1}(Q)]^{2}
of the corresponding cycle class
[\tau^{-1}(Q)]
is equal to
0
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

4.24
, the divisor
|\tau^{-1}(Q)|
cannot be contracted by
\pi
and is not equal to
|C|
.
Note that by
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0AY8
]
, the support
|\tau^{*}(Q)|
is geometrically connected.
Therefore, the divisor
|\tau^{-1}(Q)|
must be of the form
C\cup D
for some divisor
D
satisfying the assumption in (a).

Now we prove (b).
Let
C_{1},\cdots,C_{n}
be the irreducible components of
|C|
.
Note that as
S
is a smooth surface, we may freely identify the Chow group
A_{1}(S)
with
\mathrm{Pic}\,(S)
.
By the assumption that
Q\in|E|
, the preimage
\tau^{-1}(E)
is of the form
\sum a_{i}C_{i}+\widetilde{D}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
\widetilde{D}
is a non-trivial divisor whose support does not contain any of the
C_{i}
’s as an irreducible component.
Write
[C^{\prime}]
for the element
\sum a_{i}[C_{i}]\in A_{1}(S)
.
Then as each
C_{i}
is contracted to a point, we know
\tau_{*}([C^{\prime}])=0\in A_{1}(Y)
.
Applying the projection formula to the map
\tau
, we have
[C^{\prime}].[\tau^{-1}(E)]=[\tau_{*}(C^{\prime})].[E]=0\in A_{0}(Q)\simeq\mathbb{Z}
.
On the other hand, we know
[C^{\prime}].[\tau^{-1}(E)]=[C^{\prime}]^{2}+[C^{\prime}].[\widetilde{D}]
and
[C^{\prime}]^{2}<0
by
Lemma

4.24
.
Hence we must have
[C^{\prime}].[\widetilde{D}]>0
.
This implies that we may take
D
to be some connected component of
|\widetilde{D}|
which intersects
C
, which necessarily exists.
∎

Our next lemma shows that for a one-dimensional family that is trivializable up the normalization, we may freely replace one fiber to another, for the purpose of proving the Tate conjecture.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
C
be an integral curve over an algebraically closed field
\kappa
, and
f:X\to C
be a smooth proper morphism of algberaic spaces with geometrically connected fibers. Take a prime
\ell\neq\mathrm{char\,}\kappa
. Let
\nu:C^{\nu}\to C
be the normalization.
Assume the following data:

•

a trivialization of the family
\alpha:X\times_{C}C^{\nu}\simeq X_{0}\times_{\kappa}C^{\nu}
for some smooth proper algebraic space
X_{0}
over
\kappa
;

•

a trivialization
\alpha_{\ell}
of the local system
\alpha_{\ell}
that identifies it with the constant local system with fiber
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
over
C
;

•

the pullback
\nu^{*}\alpha_{\ell}
is compatible with
\alpha
.

Then for every two
\kappa
-points
c,c^{\prime}\in C
, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathrm{NS}(X_{c})_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\mathrm{NS}(X_{c^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
which is compatible with the identification
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(X_{c},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{{\acute{e}}t}}(X_{c^{\prime}},\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
offered by
\alpha_{\ell}
.

Proof.

Choose points
\widetilde{c},\widetilde{c}^{\prime}\in C^{\nu}
which lift
c,c^{\prime}
respectively.
Then the map
\alpha
induces an isomorphism
X_{c}=X_{\widetilde{c}}\simeq X_{\widetilde{c}^{\prime}}=X_{c^{\prime}}
.
The resulting isomorphism
\mathrm{NS}(X_{c})_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\mathrm{NS}(X_{c^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
does the job. Note that the isomorphism
X_{c}\simeq X_{c^{\prime}}
may depend on the choices of
\widetilde{c},\widetilde{c}^{\prime}
, but the resulting
\mathrm{NS}(X_{c})_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\mathrm{NS}(X_{c^{\prime}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
does not, as the latter has to be compatible with
\alpha_{\ell}
over
C
.
∎

By applying
Corollary

2.18
, we prove that the extended period morphism
\tau
would contract any curve that is contracted by
\pi
.
For
Proposition

4.27
and
Theorem

4.28
below, let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism for a
k
-variety
T
, and let
C
be a smooth
k
-curve mapping to
T
.
Assume one of the following conditions:

(i)

For a general
t\in C
, the minimal resolution
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
is not supersingular, and the map
C\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
extends to its compactification
\overline{C}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
.

(ii)

For a general point
t\in C
and every
i,j
, we have an equality of Hodge numbers
h^{i,j}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t})=h^{i,j}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})
.

Then if the map
\pi:T\to\mathsf{M}
contracts
C
, so does
\tau
.

Proof.

Recall from
Definition

4.3
that the restriction of
\mathcal{X}
to
T
, and hence
C
, is Zariski-locally resolvable.
So we may take some open
U\subseteq T
such that
C\times_{T}U\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{X}|_{U}
admits a simultaneous resolution
\mathcal{Y}
, and for convenience, we replace
C
by its open subset
C\times_{T}U
.
Note that under the assumption, the restriction
\mathcal{Y}|_{C}
is a resolution of the constant family
\mathcal{X}|_{C}
.
Now thanks to
Corollary

2.18
, the family
\mathcal{Y}|_{C}
must also be a constant family.

We first prove the statement assuming (i).
Recall that we have an isometry of F-isocrystals
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)[1/p]|_{T^{\circ}}\simeq\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{T^{\circ}}
.
By the full faithfulness of the restriction functor for F-isocrystals in
[
DK17
, Thm. 2.2.3]
, the isometry extends over
T
.
Therefore, using that
\mathcal{Y}|_{C}
is a constant family, we deduce that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]|_{C}
is constant.
Moreover, since
C\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
extends to
\overline{C}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
, by applying
[
DK17
, Thm. 2.2.3]
again we know that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}[1/p]|_{\overline{C}}
is also constant.
If this F-isocrystal is not supersingular, then by
Proposition

4.20
,
\overline{C}
must contract to a point. This proves (i).

Now we consider (ii).
By the semi-continuity of Hodge numbers, up to shrinking
U
to an open we may assume that the Hodge number condition in (ii) is satisfied for every point on
U
.
By
Lemma

2.3
, the relative
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\mathrm{crys}}
of the family
\mathcal{Y}|_{U}
is a locally free F-crystal. Then we have by the matching of crystalline cohomology by period morphism
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)|_{U\cap T^{\circ}}\simeq\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{U\cap T^{\circ}}
. By
Theorem

2.2
, this extends to
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}(-1)|_{U}\simeq\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{U}
.
As
\mathcal{Y}|_{C}
is a constant family, we infer that
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{crys}}|_{C}
is a constant F-crystal.
By
Proposition

4.21
, this implies that the restriction of
(\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}},\mathrm{Fil}^{\bullet}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}})
to
C
is a constant filtered flat vector bundle.
So the conclusion follows from
Lemma

4.22
.
∎

Theorem 4.28
.

Let
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}
be an extended period morphism from a smooth connected
k
-surface
T
, and let
\pi
be the associated morphism
T\to\mathsf{M}
. Let
t\in T
be a closed point.
Assume the following conditions.

(i)

The map
\pi
factors through a surjective morphism onto a smooth
k
-subsurface
S\subseteq\mathsf{M}_{k}
such that
\dim S\cap\mathsf{D}^{-}_{k}=0
.

(ii)

The generic point of any integral
k
-curve on
T
that is not contracted by
\pi
is motivically aligned.

(iii)

The minimal resolution
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{t}
is non-supersingular, or
h^{i,j}(\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s})=h^{i,j}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})
for every closed point
s\in T
and every
i,j
.

Then
\tau
is motivically aligned at
t
.

Proof.

By
Definition

4.8
, we need to show that for every
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
, the pair
(t,\zeta)
is motivically aligned. We may assume that
k
is algebraically closed.
Let
Y
be the scheme-theoretic image
\tau(T)\subset\mathscr{S}
.
Let
\overline{S}
be the Zariski closure of
S
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{k}
.

We first define an integral
k
-curve
D\subset T
to be
good
with respect to the pair
(t,\zeta)
if it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

The point
t
is contained in
D
, and
D
is not contracted by
\pi
.

(2)

There exists a path
\gamma:s\rightsquigarrow t
, where
s
is a geometric generic point on
D
, such that
\zeta
lies in the image of the specialization map
\mathrm{sp}_{\gamma}:\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(s)})_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Granting this existence of a good curve, by assumptions (i) and (ii), we know that the point
s
is motivically aligned as it is not mapped into
\mathsf{D}^{-}_{k}
.
Hence by the fact that being motivically aligned is preserved under specialization (
Lemma

4.12
), we conclude that
(t,\zeta)
is also motivically aligned.
Here we note that even if a good curve
D
might be singular at
t
, a path
\gamma:s\rightsquigarrow t
always exists by a general fact about locally Noetherian schemes (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 054F
]
).

Now we give the construction of the good curve
D
(up to deforming
t
along a constant family), based on the possible dimension of the image
Y
.
As we shall see, the curve
D
will be constructed such that any path
\gamma
will satisfy the condition (2).

Case (a).
Assume
\dim Y=0
.
We let
D\subset T
be any curve through
t
that is not contracted by
\pi
, which exists because
\pi
is generically finite.
Let
\gamma:s\rightsquigarrow t
be any path from a geometric generic point over
D
.
As
D
is contracted by
\tau
,
\tau(s)
and
\tau(t)
map to the same point on
\mathscr{S}_{k}
, so that
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(s)})_{\mathbb{Q}}
are naturally isomorphic, which gives the property (2).

Case (b).
Assume
\dim Y=1
.
By the same reasoning as above, it suffices to show that there is a curve
D
that satisfies (1) and is contracted by the map
\tau
.
Let
\overline{Y}
be Zariski closure of
Y
in
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{k}
.
By applying resolution and compactification of surfaces as in
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0C5H
]
we can always find a smooth compactification
\overline{T}
of
T
such that

(27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
extends to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{S}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}}

We then let
C
be the reduced subscheme of the connected component of
\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\pi(t))=\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\{\pi(t)\}
containing the point
t
.
If
\dim C=0
, namely when
C=\{t\}
, then there is no curve
D
on
T
that contains the point
t
and is contracted by
\pi
.
In particular, any curve
D\subset T
that contains the point
t
would satisfy (1).
Notice that since
\overline{T}
is smooth and connected of dimension
2
, yet
\dim\overline{Y}=1
, by
[
Sta25
,
Tag 02FZ
]
each fiber of the map
\overline{\tau}
has dimension at least one.
Hence we may take a curve
D\subset\tau^{-1}(t)
through
t
.

We then assume that
\dim C=1
.
Since the curve
C
is contracted by
\overline{\pi}
, by assumption (iii) and the
Proposition

4.27
, we know
C
is also contracted by
\overline{\tau}
.
So by
Proposition

4.25
.(a), we may find a curve
D\subset\overline{\tau}^{-1}(\overline{\tau}(C))
that intersects with
C
and is not contacted by the map
\overline{\pi}
.
To continue, we discuss the two scenarios based on the position of the curve
C\subset\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\pi(t))\subset\overline{T}
.

(b1)

Assume the curve
C
is already contained in the open subscheme
T
of
\overline{T}
.
Note that since the curve
C
is contracted by
\pi
, the restriction
\mathcal{X}|_{C}
is a constant family.
By
Corollary

2.18
, any (a priori Zariski local) simultaneous resolution of
\mathcal{X}
over the normalization
C^{\nu}
of
C
must be a constant family.
Hence by
Proposition

4.27
,
C
is contracted by
\tau
.
This implies that
\breve{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell,T}|_{C}\simeq(\tau|_{C})^{*}\mathbf{L}_{\ell}
is a constant
\ell
-adic local system.
Therefore, we are in a situation to which
Lemma

4.26
applies, which implies that to prove that
(t,\zeta)
is motivically aligned, we may deform it along the constant family over
C^{\nu}
given by resolving
\mathcal{X}|_{C^{\nu}}
and assume that
t\in C\cap D
.

(b2)

In general, since the point
\pi(t)
lies in
\mathsf{M}
, the scheme
\overline{\pi}^{-1}(\pi(t))=\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\pi(t)
is always contained in
\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}
.
We let
T^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{T}\times_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M}
.
Using the extension property for curves in
Corollary

4.6
, the morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
extends to
T^{\prime}
: Indeed, the morphism extends to
\overline{T}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{k}
by construction, and in order to check whether a point
t^{\prime}\in T
maps into the interior
\mathscr{S}_{k}
, we may take a general curve passing through
t^{\prime}
and apply
Corollary

4.6
.

By
Proposition

2.6
, we can find a connected smooth
k
-surface
T^{\prime\prime}
, which is a connected generically étale cover of
T^{\prime}
, such that
\mathcal{X}|_{T^{\prime\prime}}
is Zariski-locally resolvable.
We take
C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
to be a connected curve that maps surjectively to
C
.
As a consequence, we may conclude by applying the arguments in the preceeding paragraph by replacing
(T,C)
by
(T^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})
.

Case (c).
Finally, we assume
\dim Y=2
, where our strategy is to extend the special endomorpohism to a hypersurface in
T
, using the basic facts about special divisors.
We let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
be the self-intersection number
\zeta\circ\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
, and let
\mathscr{Z}(m)
be the functor over
\mathsf{\mathsf{Sch}}/\mathscr{S}
defined by sending each
\mathscr{S}
-scheme
?
to the set

\{\Xi\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{?})\mid\Xi\circ\Xi=m\}
.
It is known that the functor
\mathscr{Z}(m)
is étale-locally representable by effective Cartier divisor on
\mathscr{S}
(cf.
[
AGHM18
, Prop. 4.5.8]
; see also
[
HM20
, Prop. 6.5.2]
).
In particular, there exists an étale cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
of
\mathscr{S}
, such that the restriction
\mathscr{Z}(m)|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}
is representable by an effective Cartier divisor
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
.
By construction, we may extend the special endomorphism
\zeta\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)})
to a special endomorphism in
\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}})
.
Namely, by picking a scheme
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
that is an étale neighborhood of the point
t
, we find a morphism
\iota:V\to\mathcal{U}
that covers
\tau
, where
V
is a connected component of
T\times_{\mathscr{S}}\mathcal{U}
.
There exists a point
v\in V
lifting
t\in T
and an effective Cartier divisor
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
passing through
\iota(v)
, together with a special endomorphism
\Xi\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{E}})
such that
\Xi_{\iota(v)}=\zeta
(under the identification
\mathcal{A}_{\iota(v)}=\mathcal{A}_{\tau(t)}
).

We let
Z
be the image
\iota(V)
and choose an arbitrary compactification
\overline{Z}
.
Then by applying the compactification and the resolution for surfaces as before (
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0C5H
]
), we can find a smooth compactification
\overline{T}
of
T
and
\overline{Z}
of
Z
which fit into a commutative diagram

(28)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

By taking the closure, the Cartier divisor
E\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}\cap Z
on
Z
can be extended to
\overline{Z}
and we denote the extension also by
\overline{E}
.

At this moment, we let
C
be the reduced subscheme of the connected component of
\overline{V}_{\pi(t)}=\overline{V}\times_{\overline{S}}\{\pi(t)\}
containing the point
v
.
If
\dim C=0
, i.e.,
C=\{v\}
, then we let
D
be the connected component of
|\iota^{-1}(E)|
passing through
v
, which then is not contracted by
\pi
(and satisfies the condition (1)).
In addition, since the special endomorphism
\zeta
extends to a special endomorphism
\Xi
over the entire divisor
E
, for any geometric generic point
s
of
D
, we obtain an element
\Xi_{\iota(s)}\in\mathrm{LEnd}(\mathcal{A}_{\iota(s)})
.
Hence any path
\gamma:s\rightsquigarrow v
on the curve specializes the element
\Xi_{\iota(s)}
to
\zeta
, and in particular the curve
D
is good with respect to the pair
(v,\zeta)
.

If
\dim C=1
, then again
C
is contacted by
\pi
and the family
\mathcal{X}_{C}
is a constant family by
Proposition

4.27
, as in case (b).
Assume first that
C\subseteq\overline{V}
is already contained in the open part
V
, as in case (b1).
By
Proposition

4.25
.(b), we may find a curve
D\subset\overline{\iota}^{-1}(\overline{E})
that is not in
C
but intersects
C
.
Thus by
Lemma

4.26
and the same arguments as in the case (b1), we may replace the pair
(v,\zeta)
by
(v^{\prime},\Xi_{\iota(v^{\prime})})
, where
v^{\prime}\in C\cap D
.
Under the latter assumption, the special endomorphism
\Xi_{\iota(v^{\prime})}
admits a generalization
\Xi_{\iota(s)}
, where
s
is any geometric generic point of
D
.
Moreover, since
D
is not in
C
, we know the curve
D
is not contacted by
\pi
.
Hence
D
is good with respect to the pair
(v^{\prime},\Xi_{\iota(v^{\prime})})
.
Finally, if
C
is not contained in
V
, one adapts the argument in (b2) and we omit the details.
∎

Now we can finish the proof of
Theorem

4.2
for those points that are left from
Corollary

4.19
.

Corollary 4.29

(The Tate conjecture for fibers with worse singularity)
.

Theorem

4.2
holds for
s\in\mathsf{D}^{-}
under the assumptions (ii) or (iii).

Proof.

Recall that in
Theorem

4.2
we set
k:=k(s)
. By
Lemma

4.10
, it suffices to construct an extended period morphism
\tau:T\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
which satisfies the hypothesis of
Theorem

4.28
such that the point
s
is in the image
S
of
T
.
Recall that we are allowed to replace
k
by a finite extension.
In particular, we may assume that all irreducible components of
\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}
and
\mathsf{H}_{b}
are geometrically irreducible. Set
b\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi(s)\in\mathsf{B}_{k}
.

We shall construct a diagram as below.

(29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}_{k}\times_{\mathsf{M}}S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{\circ}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{T}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{\circ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{k}}}
generically étale
conn. comp.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

Apply
Construction

4.15
to
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
and assume that the positive integers
d_{1},\cdots,d_{r-1}
’s there are chosen to be sufficiently big.
Let
\overline{S}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{b}
be a smooth subsurface which is obtained by intersecting the first
r-1
hypersurfaces
H_{1},\cdots,H_{r-1}
, contains
s
, has generically reduced intersection with every irreducible component of
\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}
and
\mathsf{H}_{b}
, and intersects
\mathsf{D}^{-}_{b}
is a discrete set of (possibily non-reduced) points.
Set
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{S}\cap\mathsf{M}_{b}
.
Next, take
T^{\circ}_{0}
to be a connected component of
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}^{\circ}\times_{\mathsf{M}}S
.
As in
Equation

27
of the preceeding proof, we may apply
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0C5H
]
to find a smooth proper compactification
\overline{T}_{0}
of
T_{0}^{\circ}
such that the morphisms
S^{\circ}\leftarrow T^{\circ}_{0}\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
extend to
\overline{S}\leftarrow\overline{T}_{0}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{k}
.
Let
T
be a connected generically étale cover of
T_{0}:=\overline{T}_{0}\times_{\overline{S}}S
such that
\mathcal{X}|_{T}
is Zariski-locally resolvable, which exists by
Proposition

2.6
.

To prove the result, it then suffices to show that the resulting extended period morphism
T\to\mathscr{S}_{k}
, which we denote by
\tau
, satisfies hypothesis (ii) of
Theorem

4.28
.
To check this, we may replace
k
by its algebraic closure.
Let
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
be the generic point of an integral
k
-curve
\widetilde{D}
in
\overline{T}
.
Set
s^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi(\widetilde{s}^{\prime})
and let
D
be the Zariski closure of
s^{\prime}
, or equivalently the scheme-theoretic image of
\widetilde{D}
.
There are two possibilities: Either
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{D}^{+}_{k}
or
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{M}^{\circ}_{k}
.
Note that in the former case
D
is an irreducible component of
\overline{S}\cap\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}
.
In either case, let
t\in D
be a general point, so that for some lift
\widetilde{t}\in\widetilde{D}
of
t
,
\mathcal{X}|_{\widetilde{D}}
has a simultaneous resolution near
\widetilde{t}
whose total space is a projective scheme (
Lemma

2.19
).

Now in
Construction

4.15
, we pick
H_{r}
to obtain an irreducible addmissible curve
\overline{C}\subseteq\overline{S}
which passes through
t
, intersects
\mathsf{D}_{b}\cup\mathsf{H}_{b}
transversely along
\mathsf{D}^{+}_{b}\cup\mathsf{H}^{+}_{b}
, and generically lies in the maximal open subset of
S
over which
T
is étale.
Set
C:=\overline{C}\cap S
and let
\widetilde{C}
be the connected component of
T\times_{S}C
that contains
\widetilde{t}
.
Then
\widetilde{C}
is generically étale over
C
.
By applying
Theorem

4.18
to the normalization of
\widetilde{C}
, we deduce that
\tau
is motivically aligned at
\widetilde{t}
.
As
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
specializes to
\widetilde{t}
by assumption,

Lemma

4.12
(b)
tells us that
\tau
is also motivically aligned at
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
.
∎

4.8.
Specializations to
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2

In this subsection, we illustrate how the general result in
Theorem

4.2
can be applied to concrete situations, and prove
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
.
We will give the explicit constructions of the moduli spaces in those two situations, and check that they satisfy the assumptions of
Set-up

4.1
.

For readers’ convenience, we note a simple observation:

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on an integral scheme
S
.
Then for any
r
, the map
\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L)\to\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{r})
given by raising a section to its
r
th power is a map of finite fibers of order at most
r
.

Proof.

Choose a trivialization
L|_{U}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{S}(U)
for some dense open
U\subseteq S
. Suppose that
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L)
are global sections and let
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{S}(U)
be the corresponding meromorphic functions via the trivialization.
Then by the assumption that
S
is integral,
s_{1}^{r}=s_{2}^{r}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(C,L^{r})
if and only if
f_{1}^{r}=f_{2}^{r}
. When
f_{1}
is fixed, solving the equation
f_{1}^{r}=f_{2}^{r}
in the faction field of
S
leads to at most
r
distinct roots.
∎

Now we give the construction of the universal Weierstrass elliptic surface (cf.
[
Kas77
]
,
[
HYZ25
, §7.1]
).

Construction 4.31

(Universal Weierstrass elliptic surface)
.

Note that to construct the Universal Weierstrass elliptic surface over curves of genus one, we need to parametrize both the Weierstrass model of the base curve and the Weierstrass model of the fibration.
So the Weierstrass equation will appear twice.

To start, we define
\Delta(a,b)
to be the polynomial of two variables
4a^{3}-27b^{2}
.
We let
\mathsf{B}
be the complement of the vanishing locus of
\Delta(b_{4},b_{6})
in the two dimensional affine space
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[b_{4},b_{6}])
.
Let
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathsf{B}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}}\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[x,y,z])
be the family of elliptic curves given by the reduced Weierstrass equation
y^{2}z=x^{3}-b_{4}xz^{2}-b_{6}z^{3}
.
Then the map
\varpi\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathsf{B}
is the universal Weierstrass model of elliptic curves.

To continue, we let
\underline{\infty}
be the infinity section given by
[0:1:0]
of the family
\mathcal{E}\to\mathsf{B}
, and let
\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}(\underline{\infty})
be the corresponding relative line bundle on
\mathcal{E}
.
For a given positive integer
r
, we let
\mathcal{V}_{r}
be the vector bundle over
\mathsf{B}
defined by
\varpi_{*}\mathcal{L}^{r}
.
Then we define
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
to be the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6}\oplus\mathcal{O})
over
\mathsf{B}
.

We let
\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})
denote the relative affine space defined by the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6}
over
\mathsf{B}
.
So
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6}\oplus\mathcal{O})
is the relative compactification of
\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})
by adding in the infinity section.
Moreover, from the description, a point on
\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4},\mathcal{V}_{6})
is given by a triple
(b,a_{4},a_{6})
, where
b
is a point on
B
and
(a_{4},a_{6})\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathsf{B},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{4})\times\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathsf{B},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{6})
, and we have
\mathcal{L}_{b}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{b}}(\underline{\infty}_{b})
.
We also define
\{\Delta=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})
to be the reduced closed subscheme parametrizing the points
(b,a_{4},a_{6})
such that
\Delta(a_{4},a_{6})=0\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{12})
.
Then we let
\mathsf{H}\subseteq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6}\oplus\mathcal{O})
be the union of the infinity section and
\{\Delta=0\}
.
It is not hard to see that for each
b\in\mathsf{B}
,
\dim\mathsf{H}_{b}\leq\dim\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{4})
, because for each fixed
a_{4}\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{4})
, there are at most
3
distinct
a_{6}
to make
\Delta
vanish identically (
Lemma

4.30
).

Now the Weierstrass moduli space for Weierstrass fibrations is defined as the open subscheme
\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{\mathsf{M}}\smallsetminus\mathsf{H}
. Let
\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})^{*}
denote the complement of the zero section in
\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})
.
We let
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{A}(\mathcal{V}_{4}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{6})^{*}\times\mathrm{Proj\,}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[X,Y,Z])
be the closed subscheme defined by the equation
Y^{2}Z=X^{3}-a_{4}XZ^{2}-a_{6}Z^{3}
for
a_{r}\in\mathcal{V}_{r}
.
Then the morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is the universal Weierstrass fibration.

Remark 4.32

(Weierstrass fibration has RDPs)
.

The singularities on the Weierstrass normal form of an elliptic surface are always rational double points (RDPs).
In characteristic
0
, this was proved by Kas
[
Kas77
, Thm. 1]
and the same proof works for characteristic
p\geq 5
verbatim.
Alternatively, the notes of Conrad
[
Con05
]
provides an argument for this fact which works for
p=2,3
as well: The Weierstrass normal form (which is called Weierstrass model in
loc. cit.
) is Gorenstein (
[
Con05
, §4]
) and has only rational singularities (
[
Con05
, §8]
).
Thus we may conclude by noticing that for a surface, an RDP is the same as a Gorenstein rational singularity.

We shall prove a slightly more general form of
Theorem

1.1
.
19
19

19

Indeed, by taking the minimal resolution, the elliptic curve
\mathcal{E}
therein can be extended to a (unique) minimal smooth elliptic surface
X
which satisfies the assumption of
Theorem

4.33
.
The BSD conjecture of
\mathcal{E}
is then equivalent to the Tate conjecture of
X
, as explained in the introduction.

Theorem 4.33
.

Let
k
be a finitely generated field over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
for
p\geq 5
.
Let
X
be a minimal smooth proper surface with an elliptic fibration
\pi:X\to C
over a genus
1

k
-curve
C
such that
\mathrm{deg}(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\vee}=1
, and assume
\pi
admits a section.
Assume either that
X
is not supersingular or has the expected Hodge numbers.
Then
X
satisfies the Tate conjecture.

Recall that
X
has the expected Hodge numbers (
Theorem

2.22
) whenever its
j
-invariant map is separable—which may fail only in the three cases listed in
Proposition

2.23
. In particular, this condition is always true when
p\geq 11
. We also remind the reader that if
\mathcal{E}
is isotrivial (i.e.,
j
-invariant map is constant), then
X
admits a dominant rational map from a product of curves, so that the Tate conjecture for
X
is well known, so we only consider the non-isotirival case.

Proof.

Up to replacing
k
by a finite extension, there exists a point
s\in\mathsf{M}(k)
such that
X
is the minimal resolution
\breve{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
for the universal family of Weierstrass fibration
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
in
Construction

4.31
and a point
s\in\mathsf{M}
.

Let us check that the family
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
satisfies the hypotheses of
Set-up

4.1
.
Let
\eta
be the generic point of
\mathsf{M}
.
We shall take the sublattice of polarization
\Lambda
to be the subgroup of
\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})_{\mathrm{tf}}
generated by the classes of the zero section
\mathbf{e}
and a general fiber in the elliptic fibration
\mathbf{f}
.
Then we have the intersection products that
\mathbf{f}^{2}=0
and
\mathbf{e}.\mathbf{f}=1
.
The fact that
\mathbf{e}^{2}=-1
follows from the assumption that
\mathrm{deg}(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\vee}=1
(
[
Mir89
, (II.3.6)]
).
Since
\Lambda
is self-dual and contains an ample line bundle,

\Lambda
satisfies condition (a).
The conditions (b) and (c) are from the construction of the scheme
\mathsf{M}
.
The statement about Kodaira–Spencer map in (d) can be deduced from the main theorem of
[
EGW24
]
(cf.
[
HYZ25
, Lem. 7.4.3]
).
Furthermore, condition (e) follows from
[
HYZ25
, Lem. 7.3.3]
.
Finally, by
Remark

4.32
above, we have (f). Now the result directly follows from
Theorem

4.2
.
∎

Example 4.34
.

Let
C
be a genus
1
curve defined over a finitely generated extension
k
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with a rational point
e\in E(k)
.
Set
L:=\mathcal{O}_{C}(e)
.
By Riemann-Roch theorem, we easily deduces that there exist global sections
a_{4},a_{6}
of
L^{4},L^{6}
respectively such that
\mathrm{val}_{e}(a_{4})=4
and
\mathrm{val}_{e}(a_{6})=4
.
Let
Y\to C
be the corresponding Weierstrass normal form. Then by Kodaira’s table
[
Mir89
, p.41, IV.3.1]
, we find that the fiber
Y_{e}
is irreducible of type
\mathrm{IV}^{*}
, whose singularity is an
E_{6}
-singularity on the surface
Y
.
When
p=5
, then
p
divides the order of the Weyl group corresponding to
E_{6}
, which is
2^{7}3^{4}5
.
This means that
p=5
is not “sufficiently good” for an
E_{6}
-singularity in the sense of
[
Kou23
]
.
Nonetheless, our
Theorem

4.33
is applicable to the minimal resolution
X=\breve{Y}
, which shows that our method is insensitive to this type of characteristic
p
subtleties that may influence local monodromy along a DVR (see for example Thm. 1.1 of
loc. cit.
which would be false without the
p
being sufficiently good assumption, cf. Shepherd-Barron’s
[
She21
, p. 598, Rmk(2)]
).

Remark 4.35

(Remaining cases for
p=5,7
)
.

If the purity statement for the Newton stratification is true for
\mathsf{M}
(see
Remark

2.9
), then we can prove
Theorem

4.33
for the remaining three cases in
Proposition

2.23
as well.
Indeed, suppose that
X
is such a surface and is supersingular. By
Corollary

2.24
and the fact that the supersingular locus should have codimension at most
5
by purity, we can deform
X
to another surface
X^{\prime}
within the supersingular locus, so that
X^{\prime}
does not belong to the three cases of
Proposition

2.23
. Then we deform all line bundles on
X^{\prime}
to the generic fiber of this family by Morrow’s generalization of Artin’s theorem
[
Mor19
, Thm. 3.11]
and then specialize to
X
.

Next we consider the surfaces of general type as in
Theorem

1.2
.
We first recall the following fact from
[
Cat80
,
Tod80
]
: The canonical model of a minimal smooth projective surface
X
over
\mathbb{C}
with properties
p_{g}(X)=1
and
K_{X}^{2}=1

can be written as the intersection of two degree
6
hypersurfaces in the weighted projective space
\mathbb{P}(1,2,2,3,3)
.
As argued in
[
HYZ25
, Thm. 8.0.1]
, over an algebraically closed field
\kappa
of characteristic
p\geq 5
, the same is true if we impose that the irregularity
q=h^{0,1}
is
0
(which is automatic over
\mathbb{C}
) as an additional requirement.

Now we give the construction of the parametrizing space and the universal family for the aforementioned surfaces of general type.

Construction 4.36
.

Fix a prime number
p\geq 5
.
We denote the weighted projective space
\mathbb{P}(1,2,2,3,3)
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
by
\mathbb{P}
.
Let
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(6)|
be the projective space associated to the linear system that parametrizes degree
6
hypersurfaces.
For
i=0,1
, let
\mathsf{P}_{i}
be a copy of
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(6)|
, let
\mathcal{H}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{P}_{i}\times\mathbb{P}
be the universal hypersurface, let
\mathsf{D}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{P}_{i}
be the discriminant locus of
\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\mathsf{P}_{i}
, and let
\mathsf{P}^{\circ}_{i}
be the open complement
\mathsf{P}_{i}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}_{i}
.

Now we let
\overline{\mathsf{M}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{P}_{0}\times\mathsf{P}_{1}
, and let
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
be the closed subscheme of
\mathbb{P}\times\overline{\mathsf{M}}
given by the incidence
\{(x,(\alpha,\beta)):x\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}\}
.
Let
\mathsf{M}
be the maximal open subscheme of
\mathsf{P}_{1}\times\mathsf{P}_{2}
over which
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is flat and the fibers has at worst RDP singularities (
Proposition

2.7
), and let
\mathsf{H}
be the reduced complement
\overline{\mathsf{M}}\smallsetminus\mathsf{M}
.
Then we obtain the induced family
f:(\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{\mathcal{X}}\otimes_{\overline{\mathsf{M}}}\mathsf{M})\to\mathsf{M}
.
The discriminant locus
\mathsf{D}\subseteq\mathsf{M}
is defined as the reduced subscheme consisting of the points
t\in\mathsf{M}
such that
\mathcal{X}_{t}
is singular, and we let
\mathsf{M}^{\circ}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{M}\smallsetminus\mathsf{D}
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

By the paragraphs before
Construction

4.36
and by
Theorem

4.2
, it suffices to prove that
Construction

4.36
satisfies the hypothesis of
Set-up

4.1
. Note that unlike
[
HYZ25
, Thm. C]
, here we additionally imposed the assumption
h^{1,0}=0
to make sure that the surfaces to which we apply
Theorem

4.2
have the expected Hodge diamond.

For condition (a), we take
\Lambda\subseteq\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{X}_{\eta})_{\mathrm{tf}}
to the sublattice spanned by the canonical divisor, whose self-intersection number is
1
by assumption.
So the dual lattice
\Lambda^{\vee}
is equal to
\Lambda
itself, and
\Lambda
contains an ample divisor.
For condition (b), we simply define
\mathsf{B}
to be
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
itself.
For (d), the statement about the Kodaira–Spencer map holds because the generic Torelli theorem holds for these surfaces, which have a moduli space of dimension
18
(see
[
Cat80
, p.1]
).
In addition, condition (f) holds by construction.

We are left with (c) and (e), which we shall check simultaneously.
Let
\kappa=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
or
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.
Let
D
be any irreducible component of
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
or
\mathsf{H}_{\kappa}
that has codimension
1
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{\kappa}
.
We first argue that the image of
D
under the projection
\overline{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathsf{P}_{1}
to the second factor cannot lie in
\mathsf{D}_{1,\kappa}
.
Indeed, suppose that this does happen.
Then
D
must be surjective onto
\mathsf{P}_{0,\kappa}\times\mathsf{D}_{1,\kappa}
, because using standard Bertini-type arguments, one easily sees that
\mathsf{D}_{1,\kappa}
is irreducible.
We then claim that there is a
\kappa
-point
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathsf{P}_{0,\kappa}\times\mathsf{D}_{1,\kappa}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{0,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
is smooth, which would contradict the assumption that
D
is within the discriminant locus of the family
\mathcal{X}\to\mathsf{M}
.
In fact, we can let
\beta
be a general point on
\mathsf{D}_{1,\kappa}
.
Then
\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
only has isolated singularities.
As
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(6)
is very ample, so is its restriction
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}}(6)
to
\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
.
In particular, the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}}(6)
has no base locus and its associated morphism, which is a closed embedding of
\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
, induces separably finitely generated field extensions (in fact isomorphisms) at every point.
Therefore, by the second theorem of Bertini (cf.
[
CGM86
, Cor. 2]
), a general section of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{H}_{0,\alpha}}(6)
is smooth.
Namely, for a general point
\alpha\in\mathsf{P}_{0,\kappa}
, the intersection
\mathcal{H}_{0,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
is smooth.

Now, to prove (c) and (e) of
Set-up

4.1
, it suffices to look at the intersection of
D
with the open subscheme
\mathsf{P}_{0}\times\mathsf{P}^{\circ}_{1}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{M}}
.
Suppose now that
\beta
is a
\kappa
-point of
\mathsf{P}^{\circ}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}
is smooth.
Then as we argued in
[
HYZ25
, Prop. 8.0.3]
, the embedding induced by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}}(6)
is a Lefschetz embedding.
This has two consequences:

(i)

For a general line
L
in the projective space
\mathsf{P}_{0}
, the singular fibers of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}|_{L}
only have ordinary double points as singularities.

(ii)

The support of
\mathsf{D}_{\beta}
, i.e., the point set
\{\alpha\in\mathsf{P}_{0}\mid\mathcal{H}_{0,\alpha}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1,\beta}\textit{ is singular}\}
, is irreducible.

Note that (i) implies that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}|_{L}=\mathcal{X}|_{L}
, which further implies that
D
cannot be an irreducible component of
\mathsf{H}_{\kappa}
.
As a consequence, the complement
\mathsf{H}_{\kappa}
has no irreducible component of codimension
1
in
\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, and condition (c) is trivally true.
Moreover, by Prop. 8.0.5
ibid
, the fiber of
\mathsf{D}
over
\beta
is reduced.
This implies that
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is generically reduced. Since
\mathsf{P}^{\circ}_{1}
is irreducible, (ii) implies that
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
is in fact irreducible.
As this is ture for both
\kappa=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, we know that
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
must lie in the Zariski closure of
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Thus by combining the above with the generic reducedness of
\mathsf{D}_{\kappa}
, we obtain condition (e) of
Set-up

4.1
. ∎
